Your father was a shrewd man and my good friend, but in regard to science, depth of understanding, mature judgment, and practiced insight into life, the son goes before the father. You know not how much I esteem you, my worthy Mr. Tyss.”

===Chinese===
"近来——"蒂斯先生说，语调透着心下的惶惑，"近来我经历的怪事这么多，倘若这一切不是明明白白摆在感官面前的事，我真要疑心到永远了。可如今，再狂再幻的事我都信了。您也许真是那位死了的扬·斯瓦默丹（Jan Swammerdam），所以作为一个死而复生的人，比寻常世人多知道些事情；可是说到多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克，或者加玛赫公主，或者随那位小姐还有什么别的名号，您可是大错特错了。且听事情的真相究竟如何。"

佩雷格林于是十分平静地讲起了他同那位小姐的奇遇：她如何闯进莱默希特（Lemmerhirt）的房间，一直讲到斯瓦默先生如何收留了她。他讲完，斯瓦默答道："在我看来，您乐于讲述的这一切，无非是一场离奇然而十分甜美的梦罢了。不过，这事姑且放下；我请求您的友谊——往后我兴许大大用得着它。忘了我从前那副乖戾的做派，让我们彼此亲近些吧。令尊是位精明人，也是我的好友；可要论学问、论理解的深度、论成熟的判断、论对世事人情的练达洞明，儿子已在父亲之上。您不知道我是何等敬重您，我可敬的蒂斯先生。"

===Original===
“Now is the time!” whispered Master Flea, and in the same moment Peregrine felt a slight passing pain in the pupil of his left eye. He knew that Master Flea had placed the microscopic glass in his eye, but he had not before had the slightest idea of its effects. Behind the tunicle of Swammer’s eyes he perceived strange nerves and branches, the perplexed course of which he traced deep into the forehead, and could perceive that they were Swammer’s thoughts. They ran much in this way: “I did not expect to get off so easily here, without being better questioned. If papa was an ignoramus, of whom I never thought anything, the son is still worse, with a greater infusion of childishness. With the simplicity of an idiot, he tells me the whole adventure with the Princess, not seeing that she must have already told me all, as my behaviour to her of necessity presupposes an earlier intimacy. But there is no help for it; I must speak him fair, because I want his help. He is simple enough to believe all I say, and, in his stupid good nature, to make many a sacrifice to my interest, for which he will reap no other thanks than that, when all is over, and Gamaheh mine again, I shall laugh soundly at him behind his back.”

===Chinese===
"正是时候！"跳蚤大师悄声道。同一瞬间，佩雷格林感到左眼瞳孔里掠过一阵轻微的、转瞬即逝的痛楚。他知道，跳蚤大师已把显微镜片嵌进了他的眼睛；可这镜片有何等神效，他先前连最微小的一点概念都没有。透过斯瓦默眼睛的那层薄膜，他看见一些奇异的神经和分支，乱纷纷地蜿蜒伸展；他一路追踪，直追进额头深处，认出那正是斯瓦默的念头。那些念头大致是这样运行的："没想到在这里竟这么容易就搪塞过去了，连句像样的盘问都没有。要说老子是个不学无术的蠢材——我从来不曾把他放在眼里——那儿子更是等而下之，孩子气还更重几分。他竟以白痴般的坦白，把同公主的整桩奇遇原原本本讲给我听，却看不出她必定早已把一切告诉了我，因为我对她的态度，势必要以一段早年的交情为前提。可是没法子，我得拿好话哄住他，因为我要用他。他蠢得足以相信我说的一切，又蠢得心地良善，肯为我的利益作出种种牺牲；他为此得到的报答，无非是等到一切收场、加玛赫重归我手之后，我在他背后痛痛快快地嘲笑他一场。"

===Original===
“It seemed to me﻿—” said Swammer, coming close to Peregrine. “It seemed to me, my dear Mr. Tyss, as if a flea were on your collar.”

The thoughts ran thus: “The deuce! that was, indeed, Master Flea! It would be a queer piece of business if Gamaheh should be right after all.”

Peregrine stepped nimbly back, protesting that he had no dislike to fleas.

===Chinese===
"我方才觉得——"斯瓦默说，一面凑近佩雷格林，"我方才觉得，亲爱的蒂斯先生，您的领子上仿佛有一只跳蚤。"

他的念头是这样转的："见鬼！那当真是跳蚤大师！倘若到头来加玛赫说的不错，这桩买卖可就邪门了。"

佩雷格林敏捷地退后一步，连声声明自己对跳蚤并无恶感。

===Original===
“Then,” replied Swammer, with a profound bow, “then for the present I most respectfully take my leave, my dear Mr. Tyss.”

The thoughts ran thus: “I wish the blackwinged devil had you, idiot!”

Master Flea took the microscopic glass out of the eye of the astonished Peregrine, and then said, “You have now, my dear sir, experienced the wonderful effects of the glass, which has not its equal in the world, and must perceive what a superiority it gives you over men, by laying open before your eyes their inmost thoughts. But, if you were to use it constantly, the perpetual knowledge of their real sentiments would overwhelm you, for the bitter vexation which you have just now experienced would be too often repeated. I will always be with you when you leave your house, sitting either in your collar, or in some convenient place, and if you wish to learn the thoughts of him who is conversing with you, you have only to snap your fingers, and the glass will be in your eye immediately.”

===Chinese===
"那么，"斯瓦默深深一躬，答道，"那么，眼下我就恭而敬之地告退了，亲爱的蒂斯先生。"

他的念头是这样转的："但愿那黑翅膀的魔鬼把你抓了去，白痴！"

跳蚤大师把显微镜片从惊愕不已的佩雷格林眼中取出，然后说道："亲爱的先生，你如今已领教这镜片的神效了，它是举世无双的；你也必定感觉得到，它把人们心底最隐秘的念头摊开在你眼前，给了你何等超越凡人的优势。可你若时时刻刻使用它，对世人真实心意的无休无止的了解，会把你压垮，因为你方才尝到的那份苦涩的懊恼，将会一而再、再而三地重来。往后你出门在外，我总陪伴着你，或者坐在你的衣领里，或者待在哪个方便的地方；你若想得知正与你交谈之人的心思，只消打个响指，镜片立时便到你眼中。"

===Original===
Peregrine, seeing the manifest advantages of such a gift, was about to pour out the warmest thanks, when two deputies from the council entered, and announced to him that he was accused of a deep offence, the consequence of which must be preliminary imprisonment and the seizure of his papers.

Mr. Peregrine swore high and low that he was not conscious of the slightest offence, but one of the deputies replied with a smile that perhaps in a few hours his innocence might be proved; till when, however, he must submit to the orders of the magistrate. After this, what was left to Mr. Tyss but to get into the coach, and suffer himself to be carried off to prison? It may be supposed with what feelings he passed Mr. Swammer’s chamber.

Master Flea sat in the collar of the prisoner.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林眼见这样一件馈赠好处昭彰，正要倾吐最热忱的谢意，恰在此时，市政会的两名专员走了进来，通知他说：有人告发他犯下一桩重罪，其后果必是先行羁押，并查抄他的文书。

佩雷格林先生指天画地地发誓，说自己丝毫不知有何罪过；其中一名专员却含笑回道：也许不出几个钟头，他的清白便可得证；但在那之前，他必须服从市政长官的命令。事已至此，蒂斯先生除了登车就道、由人押进监狱，还能有什么别的办法？他经过斯瓦默先生房门时心头是什么滋味，可想而知。

跳蚤大师就坐在囚徒的领子里。

## IV Fourth Adventure / 四 第四奇遇

===Original===
The mistake of the watchman in arresting Mr. George Pepusch for a thief was soon explained. In the meantime, however, some informalities had been discovered in his passport, and for this reason they required that he should produce some resident citizen of Frankfurt as his bail, till when he must be contented with his present place in prison.

Here then sat Mr. George Pepusch in a very neat room, meditating on whom he could find in Frankfurt to be his bail. He had been away so long that he feared he must be forgotten by those who had formerly known him well, and, as to foreign recommendations, he possessed none whatever. He began to look out of the window in a very melancholy mood, and cursed his fate aloud, when a window was opened close by him, and a voice exclaimed, “What! do I see right? Is it you, George?” Mr. Pepusch was not a little astonished on perceiving the friend, with whom he had been most intimate during his residence at Madras. “The deuce!” he exclaimed, “that I should be so forgetful, so utterly stupid! I knew that you had got safely into harbour, and in Hamburg heard strange things of your way of living, and, when I had got here, never thought of paying you a visit. But he who has such wonderful things in his head as I have﻿—Well, it is lucky that accident brought you to me! You see I am under arrest, but you can immediately set me free, by answering for my being really the George Pepusch, whom you knew years ago, and not a thief nor a robber.”

“Why,” replied Peregrine, “I should be an excellent bail, being myself under arrest!”
===Chinese===
更夫误把乔治·佩普施先生当作小偷抓了起来，这层误会不久便澄清了。然而他的护照又被查出几处不合规矩的地方，为这个缘故，官府要他寻一位定居法兰克福的公民出面作保；在保人到位之前，他只得安分地待在眼下的牢房里。

乔治·佩普施先生就这样坐在一间十分整洁的房间里，盘算着在法兰克福还能找谁替自己作保。他离开得太久，唯恐从前相熟的人早把他忘了个干净；至于外地的荐书，他更是一封也没有。他正满怀愁绪地朝窗外张望，高声咒骂自己的命运，忽听得身旁一扇窗子打了开来，一个声音喊道："怎么！我没有看错吧？是你吗，乔治？"佩普施认出这位正是他当年侨居马德拉斯（Madras）时过从最密的朋友，不由得惊讶万分。"见鬼！"他叫道，"我竟会这样健忘、这样彻底地糊涂！我明知你早已安然靠岸，在汉堡又听说了你那套过日子的种种奇闻，到了此地，竟从没想到登门拜望你。不过，谁让我脑袋里装着像我这样的奇事呢——也罢，幸亏机缘把你送到我跟前来！你瞧，我被扣押在这里，可你马上就能放我出去：只消担保我确是多年前你认识的那个乔治·佩普施，不是小偷，也不是强盗。"

"哦，"佩雷格林答道，"我自己也是个在押的人，岂不正是位顶好的保人！"

===Original===
He now related at large to his friend, how since his return to Frankfurt he had found himself deprived of both his parents, and had from that time led, amidst all the bustle of a city, a lonely joyless life, devoted to the memory of other days. To this George replied morosely, “Oh yes, I have heard of it, I have heard of the fools’ tricks you play, that you may waste life in a childish dream. You would be a hero of innocence, of childishness, and for this despise the just claims which society has upon you. You give imaginary family feasts, and bestow upon the poor the costly viands, the dear wines, which you have before served up to the dead. You give yourself Christmas boxes, and act as if you were a child, and then present to poor children these gifts, which are of the sort usually wasted in rich houses upon spoiled young ones. But you do not reflect that you are doing a scurvy benefit to the poor in tickling their gums with delicacies, that they may doubly feel their wretchedness, when afterwards they are compelled, by pressing hunger, to eat the vile bits that would be rejected by many a petted lapdog. Ha! how this alms-giving disgusts me, when I think that what you thus waste in a day would be sufficient to support them for months in a moderate manner. Then too you overload them with glittering gewgaws, when a common toy, presented by their fathers or mothers, gives them infinitely more pleasure. They eat themselves sick with your infernal marzipan, and with the knowledge of your splendid gifts, which in the end must be denied to them, you sow in their young minds the seeds of discontent and uneasiness. You are rich, full of youth, and yet withdraw yourself from all society, and thus frustrate the approaches of well-meaning minds. I will believe that the death of your parents may have shaken you, but if everyone who has suffered a real loss were to creep into his shell, by heavens! the whole world would be like a house of mourning, and I would not live in it. But, my friend! do you know that you are under the influence of the most determined egotism that ever lurked beneath a silly misanthropy? Go, go, Peregrine, I can no longer esteem you, no longer be your friend, if you do not change this way of life, and give up your abominable system of housekeeping.”

Peregrine snapped his fingers, and Master Flea instantly placed the microscopic glass in his eye. The thoughts of the angry Pepusch ran thus: “Is it not a pity that such a kind, understanding man should fall into these dangerous fancies, which at last will completely unnerve him, and deprive him of his best powers? But it is evident that his delicate mind, which is besides inclined to melancholy, could not endure the blow inflicted on him by the death of his parents, and he seeks for consolation in a mode of life which borders upon madness. He is lost if I do not save him. The more I esteem him, the harder I will attack him, and the stronger I will paint his folly.”

In these thoughts Peregrine saw that he had found his old friend unaltered, and, after Master Flea had taken the microscopic glass out of his eye, he said, “George, I will not contend with you as to what you say of my mode of life, for I know you mean it well with me, but I must tell you that it gives me real delight when I can make a day of festival to the poor, although in this I do not think of myself a detestable egotism, of which at least I feel unconscious. They are the flowers in my life, which else seems to me like a wild melancholy field of thistles.”
===Chinese===
他于是向朋友原原本本讲起来：自从回到法兰克福，他如何发觉自己双亲俱已亡故，从那时起，他如何在一片城市的喧嚣里过着孤独而无欢的日子，一心沉缅于追怀往昔。对此乔治悻悻答道："哦，是了，这些我都听说了，听说了你为把性命虚掷在孩子气的梦里而耍的那些傻瓜把戏。你想当一个天真无邪的英雄、一个孩子气的豪杰，为此竟蔑视社会对你提出的正当权益。你摆设想象中的家宴，又把先前供奉死者的珍馐美酒转手施舍给穷人。你给自己送圣诞礼物，装作自己还是个孩子，再把这类礼物送给穷孩子——而这路东西，本是阔人家随手糟蹋在娇宠的小祖宗身上的。可你不想想：你拿美味去搔穷人的牙床，行的是何等刻薄的善举；待到日后逼人的饥饿迫使他们去吃连许多娇宠的哈巴狗都不屑一顾的秽物碎屑时，他们便要加倍咂摸出自己的苦命来。哈！一想到你这样一天里挥霍掉的物件，本足教他们几个月里将就度日，这种施舍真叫我作呕。你还拿亮闪闪的廉价玩物往他们身上堆，殊不知一件寻常玩具，只要是父亲母亲给的，给他们的乐趣要多出百倍不止。他们吃你那害人的杏仁糖膏吃出病来；而他们既见识了你那些辉煌的礼物——到头来终究无福消受——你便在他们的幼小心灵里播下了不平与不安的种子。你有钱，又正当青春年少，却断绝一切交往，把好心人亲近你的一切门路都堵死了。我愿意相信，你父母的死或许震动过你；可要是每个遭了真丧痛的人都缩进自己的壳里去，天哪！整个世界就要变成一座举丧的宅子，我可是不情愿住在里头的。再说，我的朋友！你可知道，你正被一桩世上最顽固的利己主义支配着——它就藏在一副愚蠢的厌世相底下？去吧，去吧，佩雷格林，你要是不改掉这种活法、丢开你那套可憎的持家之道，我就再不能敬重你，再不能做你的朋友了。"

佩雷格林弹了个响指，跳蚤大师当即把显微镜片嵌进他的眼睛。怒气冲冲的佩普施心里转的是这样的念头："这样一位宅心仁厚、明白事理的人，竟陷进这些危险的妄想里去——到头来它们会把他彻底摧垮，夺走他最好的才力——岂不可惜？不过显而易见，他那天生细腻、又素来偏向忧郁的心灵，承受不住双亲之死给他的打击，便到一种近乎疯狂的生活方式里去讨安慰了。我若不救他，他就毁了。我越是敬重他，越要狠狠地攻讦他，把他的荒唐涂抹得越浓。"

从这些念头里，佩雷格林看出他的老朋友丝毫未改。等跳蚤大师把显微镜片从他眼中取出，他便说道："乔治，你对我这套活法的这番指摘，我不打算同你争，因为我知道你是好意。但我必须告诉你：每当我能给穷人办出一个节日来，我总感到真心的快乐；在这上头我可看不出什么可憎的利己主义——至少我自己全无觉察。这些节日是我生命中的花朵；少了它们，我的生命在我看来就只是一片荒凉忧郁的蓟草之野。"

===Original===
“What do you say of thistles?” interrupted George Pepusch hastily, “Why do you despise thistles, and place them in opposition to flowers? Are you so little versed in natural history as not to know that the most wonderful blossom in the world is that of the thistle, I mean the *Cactus grandiflorus*. And again, is not the thistle, Zeherit, the most beautiful Cactus under the sun? Peregrine, I have so long kept it from you, or rather was forced to keep it from you, because I myself had not the full conviction of it, but now learn, that I myself am the thistle, Zeherit, and will never give up my claims to the hand of the daughter of the worthy king, Sekakis, the heavenly Princess Gamaheh. I had found her, but in the same moment the diabolical watchmen seized me, and dragged me to prison.”

“How!” cried Peregrine, half petrified with astonishment, “are you too involved in the strangest of all histories?”

“What history?” asked Pepusch.

Peregrine did not hesitate to tell his friend, as he had before told Mr. Swammer, all that had happened at the bookbinder’s, and afterwards at his own house. He did not even conceal the appearance of Master Flea, although, as may be easily supposed, he kept to himself the secret of his possessing the microscopic glass.

George’s eyes burnt, he bit his lips, struck his forehead, and, when Peregrine had ended, cried out like a maniac, “The false one! the traitress!” Greedy, in the self-pangs of despairing love, to drain the last drop from the poison cup, which Peregrine had unconsciously proffered him, he made him repeat every little trait of Dörtje’s behaviour, interrupting him with murmurs of, “In the arms! On the breast! Glowing kisses!” Then again he started away from the window, and ran about the room with the gestures of a madman. In vain Peregrine cried out to him to hear the rest, exclaiming that he had much that was consolatory to say﻿—Pepusch did not the more leave off his raving.
===Chinese===
"你说什么蓟草？"乔治·佩普施急忙打断他，"你凭什么看轻蓟草，把它摆到花朵的对立面去？难道你的博物学竟这样浅陋，不知道天下最奇妙的花开在蓟草身上——我说的是*Cactus grandiflorus*（大花仙人掌）？再说，蓟草泽赫里特，难道不正是普天之下最美的仙人掌？佩雷格林，这件事我瞒了你这么久——或者不如说，是不得不瞒你，因为连我自己也未曾全然确信；可现在你听着：我自己就是那株蓟草泽赫里特，而且我绝不放弃我对贤王塞卡基斯之女、天仙般的加玛赫公主的求婚权利。我已经找到她了，可就在同一瞬间，那帮魔鬼似的更夫抓住我，把我拖进了监狱。"

"怎么！"佩雷格林喊道，惊得半僵在那里，"连你也卷进了这桩天下最离奇的公案？"

"什么公案？"佩普施问。

佩雷格林不再迟疑，像先前对斯瓦默先生讲过的那样，把装订匠家里的种种遭遇、随后他自己家中的种种，一古脑儿全告诉了朋友。他连跳蚤大师的现身也不隐瞒，只是——这个不难想见——把那枚显微镜片在自己手里的秘密，独自藏在心里。

乔治双眼冒火，咬着嘴唇，捶着脑门；等佩雷格林讲完，他疯子似的喊道："这虚伪的人！这背信的女子！"绝望的爱情自啮其心，他贪渴地要饮尽佩雷格林无意间递到他手中的那杯毒酒的最后一滴，便逼着佩雷格林把多尔特耶的一举一动、一颦一笑统统重述一遍，还时不时咕哝着打断他："搂在怀里！偎在胸口！滚烫的亲吻！"随后他又猛地离开窗口，比画着疯人的种种手势，绕着屋子团团乱转。佩雷格林喊他听自己讲完余下的事，说自己还有许多宽慰他的话要说——全是白费，佩普施反而愈发狂态大作。

===Original===
The door was opened, and an officer of the council announced to Peregrine that no sufficient cause had been found for his longer imprisonment, and he might return home.

The first use Peregrine made of his regained freedom was to offer himself as bail for George Pepusch, testifying that he was really George Pepusch, with whom he had lived in intimacy at Madras, and who was known to him for a man of fortune and respectability.

Master Flea exhausted himself in very philosophic and instructive reflections, which amounted to this: that the Thistle, Zeherit, in spite of his rough exterior, was very kind and reasonable, but a little too overbearing, and fairly considered was quite correct in his censure of Mr. Peregrine’s way of life, though somewhat too harsh perhaps in his expressions. He too﻿—that is, Master Flea﻿—would really advise Mr. Peregrine henceforth to go abroad in the world.

“Believe me,” he said, “it will bring you many advantages to leave your solitude. You need no longer fear seeming shy and confused, as with the mysterious glass in your eye you command the thoughts of men, and it is therefore impossible that you should not always maintain the right tact. How firmly and calmly may you stand before the highest, while their inward souls lie open to your eyes. Therefore, move freely in the world; your blood will circulate more lightly, all melancholy brooding will cease and, which is the best of all, motley ideas and thoughts will arise in your brain; the image of the fair Gamaheh will lose its brightness, and you will soon be better able to keep your word with me.”

Peregrine felt that both George Pepusch and Master Flea meant him well, and he resolved to follow their wise advice. But when he heard the sweet voice of his beautiful beloved, he could not think how it was possible for him to leave the house, which had become a paradise to him.
===Chinese===
牢门开了，市政会的一名官员向佩雷格林宣布：没有发现足以再羁押他的理由，他可以回家了。

佩雷格林把重获的自由用在了头一件事上：自任乔治·佩普施的保人，具结作证：此人确系乔治·佩普施，当年与他同在马德拉斯亲密相处，据他所知，家道殷实，品行端方。

跳蚤大师发表了一大篇极富哲理、极有教益的宏论，归结起来无非是：蓟草精泽赫里特尽管外表粗粝，心地却很善良，人也通情达理，只是气性略嫌专横；平心而论，他对佩雷格林先生那套活法的指摘是全然不错的，只是措辞或许稍稍严苛了些。他——也就是跳蚤大师——也当真要奉劝佩雷格林先生，从今往后到世上去走动走动。

"相信我，"他说，"走出你这离群索居的日子，会给你带来许多好处。你再不必担心显得羞怯慌乱了，因为那枚神秘的镜片嵌在你眼里，世人的心思尽由你俯察，你便绝无可能失了分寸。哪怕站在最尊贵的人物面前，你也可以站得多么坚定、多么从容——他们的内里灵魂，全都摊开在你眼前。所以，放胆到世上去走动吧；你的血液会流转得更轻快，一切忧郁的枯想都会止息，而顶要紧的是，五光十色的念头和思想会在你脑子里生发出来；美人加玛赫的影子会褪去它的光焰，你不久也就能更好地对我践约了。"

佩雷格林觉得，乔治·佩普施和跳蚤大师都是一番好意，便打定主意听从他们明智的忠告。可是一听到他美丽恋人的甜美嗓音，他又实在想不出，自己怎么可能离得开这栋房子——它于他早已成了一座乐园。
===Original===
At length he brought himself to visit a public promenade. Master Flea had fixed the glass in his eye, and taken up a place in his collar, where he gently rocked himself to and fro at his ease.

“Have I at last the pleasure of seeing my good friend Mr. Tyss again? You make yourself scarce, my dear sir, and we have all been longing for you. Let us go into a coffeehouse, and take a glass of wine together. I am truly rejoiced to see you.”

It was thus that he was addressed by a young man, whom he had seen scarcely two or three times. The thoughts ran thus: “Is the stupid misanthrope visible again? But I must flatter him, that I may soon borrow money of him. He’ll not surely be possessed by the devil, and accept my invitation; I have not a halfpenny in my pocket, and no innkeeper will trust me any longer.”

Two well-dressed girls now crossed him. They were sisters, distantly related to him.

“Ah, cousin!” cried one of them, laughing, “do we meet you at last? It is not well done to lock yourself up so that one can never get a sight of you. You do not know how fond mamma is of you, because you are such a sensible man. Promise me to come soon. There, kiss my hand.” The thoughts ran thus: “How! what is this? what has come to our cousin? I wanted to make him blush and stammer, and formerly he used to run away from every girl, but now he stands and eyes me so strangely, and kisses my hand without the least shyness. If he should be in love with me? That would be a fine thing! My mother says that he is somewhat stupid, but what does that signify? I will have him: a stupid man, when he is rich as my cousin is, is the very best.” The sister had merely lisped, with downcast eyes and blushing cheeks, “Come to us shortly, dear cousin.” The thoughts ran thus: “Our cousin is a very handsome man, and I do not understand why mamma calls him silly, and can’t endure him. If he should come to our house, he will fall in love with me, for I am the prettiest girl in all Frankfurt. I will have him, because I want a rich man, that I may sleep till twelve o’clock in the day, and wear dearer shawls than my sister.”

A physician, in passing, perceived Peregrine, stopped his carriage, and called out, “Good morning, my dear sir: you look uncommonly well; heaven keep you so! But, if anything should happen, think of me, the old friend of your late father: such sound constitutions as yours I can soon set to rights. Adieu.” The thoughts ran thus: “I believe the fellow is constantly well out of pure avarice, but he looks tolerably pale now, and seems at last to have something the matter with him. Well; only let him once come under my hands, and he shall not soon get up from his bed again; he shall undergo a sound penance for his obstinate health.”
===Chinese===
他终于鼓起劲来，去逛了一处公共游憩场。跳蚤大师把镜片嵌进他的眼睛，自己则在衣领上占了一个位置，舒舒服服地随着脚步轻轻摇来晃去。

"我总算又有幸见到我的好朋友蒂斯先生啦？您可真是难得露面啊，亲爱的先生，我们大家可都盼着您呢。咱们上一家咖啡馆，同干一杯酒吧。见到您，我真是打心眼儿里高兴。"

这样同他寒暄的是个年轻人，统共同他见过不过两三面。那人心里转的念头是："这厌世的蠢货又露面了？可我还得奉承他，好过些日子向他借钱。他总不至于鬼迷了心窍，当真接受我的邀约吧；我口袋里连半个便士也没有，也没有哪家酒馆主人再肯赊我的账了。"

这时，两位衣饰入时的姑娘同他打了个照面。她们是姊妹俩，同他沾着点远亲。

"哎，表哥！"其中一个笑着喊道，"可算见着您了？把自己关得那么严实，叫人怎么也捞不着您一面，这可不应该。您不知道妈妈多疼您，因为您是这么个明白事理的人。答应我快来吧。喏，吻一下我的手。"她心里转的念头是："咦！这是怎么了？咱们这位表哥是中了什么邪？我本想逗他脸红结巴的——从前他见了哪个姑娘不是扭头就跑？如今却站在那儿，这样古怪地打量我，吻起我的手来竟没有半分羞怯。莫非他爱上我了？那可妙了！妈妈说他有点傻，可那有什么要紧？我要定他了：一个男人，只要像我表哥这样有钱，呆头呆脑的才是顶好的呢。"那位姊妹只是垂着眼帘、红着脸庞，细声细气说了一句："亲爱的表哥，早日到我们家来呀。"她心里转的念头是："咱们这位表哥生得真俊，我不明白妈妈为什么叫他傻子，还容不得他。他要是上我们家来，准会爱上我，因为我是全法兰克福最漂亮的姑娘。我要定他了，因为我想要个有钱的男人，好教我白天睡到十二点，披的披肩也比姐姐的更贵。"

一位医生乘车路过，认出了佩雷格林，忙叫停车，喊道："早安，亲爱的先生：您气色好得很哪；上天保佑您长此康健！不过万一有个头疼痛热，可要想着我——我是您先父的老朋友。像您这样结实的底子，我很快就能调治妥帖。再会。"他心里转的念头是："我看这家伙长年不生病，纯粹是出于吝啬；不过他眼下脸色相当苍白，似乎终于要有什么毛病上身了。好；只消他一旦落到我手里，就休想痛痛快快再爬下床来；他那顽固的健康，得好好受一场补赎。"

===Original===
Immediately after this, an old merchant cried out to him, “My best greetings to you, worthy Mr. Tyss; see how I am forced to run and bustle, and plague myself with business. You have done wisely in withdrawing from it, though with your quick-sightedness you could not fail of doubling your father’s fortune.” The thoughts were thus: “If the fool would only meddle with business, he would speculate away his whole fortune in a short time, and that would be a real delight. His old papa, whose joy was in ruining other people that wished to help themselves by a little bankruptcy, would turn himself about in his grave.”

Many more such cutting contrasts between words and thoughts occurred to Peregrine. He always directed his answers rather by what people meant than by what they said, and, as he penetrated into their inmost intents, they themselves were puzzled what to think of him. At last he felt wearied, snapped his fingers, and immediately the glass vanished from the pupil of his left eye.

On returning to his house he was surprised by a strange spectacle. A man stood in the middle of the passage, looking steadfastly through a strangely-formed glass at Mr. Swammer’s door. Upon this door sun-bright circles played in rainbow colours, and then met in one fiery point, that seemed to pierce through the wood. As this took place a deep sighing was heard, broken by cries of pain, which came, as it appeared, from the room. To his horror, Peregrine fancied that he distinguished Gamaheh’s voice.

“What do you want? what are you doing here?” he exclaimed to the man, who really seemed to be practising diabolic arts, the rainbow circles growing with every moment quicker and brighter, the centre-point piercing more keenly, and the cries sounding more painfully from the chamber.

“Oh!” exclaimed the stranger, closing his glass, and hastily putting it into his pocket, “Oh! the landlord. Your pardon, my dear sir, that I am operating here without your permission; I did indeed pay you a visit to request it, but Alina told me you had gone out, and the business here would admit of no delay.”
===Chinese===
紧接着，一位老商人朝他嚷道："向您致以最诚挚的问候，可敬的蒂斯先生；瞧瞧我，不得不东奔西走、操劳不歇，让生意折磨得苦不堪言。您抽身出来，实在明智——虽说以您的眼力，本不难把令尊的家产翻上一番。"他心里的念头是这样："这傻瓜但凡肯沾一沾生意，不出多久准会把全部家当投机个精光，那才真叫人心大快呢。他的老太爷——此人平生一乐，就是坑垮那些想借小小一纸破产自救的人——在坟里也要辗转反侧了。"

言辞与心思之间这等辛辣的对照，佩雷格林随后还领教了许多。他应答时，一向是照人家的真意、而不是照人家的口头来对付；他既把人们心底的盘算看得通透，那些人反倒自己纳起闷来，摸不准该把他看成什么人。末了他觉得倦了，弹了个响指，镜片当即从他左眼的瞳孔里隐去了。

回到家中，一幅奇景叫他吃了一惊。一个男子站在过道正中，正透过一具形状古怪的镜片，死死盯住斯瓦默先生的房门。那门上映着太阳一般明亮的圆圈，闪着彩虹七色流转游移，末了汇作一点火尖，仿佛要把木头穿透。随着这番光景，屋里传出深深的叹息，间或被痛苦的喊叫截断——听那声音，正是从那个房间里出来的。佩雷格林毛骨悚然，恍惚间竟听出那是加玛赫的声音。

"你想干什么？你在这里搞什么名堂？"他冲那人喝道。那人当真像是在行使妖术——彩虹的圆圈一刻快似一刻、一刻亮似一刻，中心那火尖扎得一刻深似一刻，屋里的喊声也一声惨似一声。

"哦！"那陌生人叫了一声，合拢他的镜片，慌忙塞进衣袋，"哦！是房东先生。亲爱的先生，恕我不曾求得您的准许，就在此地施起术来；我确实登门求过准许，可阿莉娜告诉我您出去了，而这边的公务又刻不容缓。"

===Original===
“What business?” said Peregrine, pretty harshly; “what business is it that will admit of no delay?”

“Don’t you know,” replied the stranger with an odious grin, “don’t you know that my ill-advised niece, Dörtje Elverdink, has run away? You were arrested, though with great injustice, as her seducer, on which score I will with great pleasure testify your perfect innocence, if it should be requisite. It is not to you, but to Swammerdam, once my friend, and now my enemy, that the faithless Dörtje has fled. She is in that chamber﻿—I know it﻿—and alone, since Swammerdam has gone out. I cannot get in, as the door is barred and bolted, and I am too mild to employ force, but I have taken the liberty to torment her a little with my optical glass, that she may know I am her lord and master in spite of her imaginary princess-ship.”

“You are the devil!” exclaimed Peregrine, in the highest indignation, “You are the devil! but not lord and master of the beautiful Gamaheh. Out of my house! Practise your devil’s tricks where you will, but here you will fail with them, I can promise you.”

“Don’t put yourself in a passion,” replied Leeuwenhoek. “Don’t put yourself in a passion, my dear Mr. Tyss; I am an innocent man, who means nothing but good. It is a little monster, a little basilisk, that sits in yonder room, in the shape of a lovely woman. If the abode with my insignificance displeased her, she might have fled, but the traitress should not have robbed me of my most precious treasure, the best friend of my soul, without whom I am nothing. She should not have run away with Master Flea. You will not understand what I mean, worthy sir, but﻿—”

Here Master Flea, who had planted himself in a secure place, could not refrain from bursting out into a fine mocking laugh.
===Chinese===
"什么公务？"佩雷格林厉声问道，"什么公务竟刻不容缓？"

"您竟不知道么，"陌生人狞笑着答道，"您竟不知道，我那个没头脑的侄女多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克逃走了？您被当作拐带她的嫌疑抓了起来——虽然冤枉得很——日后倘若用得着，我自会满心乐意出具证明，担保您完全清白。那薄情的多尔特耶投奔的不是您，而是斯瓦默丹——他从前是我的朋友，如今是我的仇敌。她就在那个房间里——这我知道——而且独自一人，因为斯瓦默丹出去了。我进不去，门上了闩加了杠，而我这个人又太温和，不屑动武；我便只好冒昧用我的光学镜片给她一点小小的折磨，好教她明白：任凭她做她那凭空捏造的公主梦，我终究是她的主子、她的老爷。"

"你就是魔鬼！"佩雷格林怒不可遏地喝道，"你就是魔鬼！可你决不是美人加玛赫的主子和老爷。滚出我的房子！你的鬼把戏爱到哪儿使唤就去使唤，只是在这儿，我敢向你担保，它们休想得逞。"

"您可别动这么大的肝火，"列文虎克答道，"别动这么大的肝火，亲爱的蒂斯先生；我是个清白无辜的人，心里存的只有善意。坐在那边屋里的，是个小怪物，一条小巴西利斯克（basilisk），不过化作了一位可爱女子的模样。若是嫌同我这微末之人住在一处委屈了她，她尽可以逃走；可这背信的女子不该把我最珍贵的宝贝、我灵魂的至交抢了去——离了他，我便一无是处。她不该带着跳蚤大师私奔。您不会明白我这话的意思，可敬的先生，不过——"

这时，早已在一处稳妥地方站定的跳蚤大师，忍不住爆发出一阵漂亮的讥笑。

===Original===
“Ha!” cried Leeuwenhoek, struck with a sudden terror, “ha! what was that? Can it be possible? Here, on this spot? Permit me, my dear sir﻿—”

Thus saying, Leeuwenhoek stretched out his hand, and snatched at Peregrine’s collar, who dexterously avoided his grasp, and, seizing him with a strong arm, dragged him towards the door, to fling him out without farther ado. But just as he had reached the door, it was opened from without, and in rushed George Pepusch, followed by Swammerdam.

No sooner did Leeuwenhoek perceive his enemy Swammerdam, than he burst from Peregrine with the utmost exertion of his last strength, and planted himself with his back against the door of the mysterious chamber, where the fair one was imprisoned. Swammerdam, seeing this, took a little telescope from his pocket, drew it out at full length, and fell upon his adversary, exclaiming, “Draw, scoundrel, if you have courage!”

Leeuwenhoek had quickly a similar instrument in his hand, drew it out as the other had done, and cried, “Come on; I am ready, and you shall soon feel my prowess.”

Each now put his glass to his eye, and fell furiously upon the other with sharp, murderous glances, now lengthening and now shortening his weapon by drawing the tubes in and out. There were feints, parries, thrusts, in short, all the tricks of the fencing-school, and with every moment they seemed to grow more angry. Whenever one was hit he cried out aloud, sprang into the air, cut the most wonderful capers, made the most beautiful entrechats, and turned pirouettes, as well as the best *pas seul* dancer on the Parisian stage, till his adversary fixed him fast with the shortened telescope. When the other was hit he did precisely the same, and in this way they went on interchangeably with the most violent springs, the maddest gestures, and the most furious cries. The perspiration dropped from their brows, the bloodred eyes seemed starting from their heads, and as there appeared no other cause for their St. Vitus’ dance than their looking at each other through their glasses, they might have been taken for maniacs, just escaped from the madhouse. For the rest, it was a very pretty sight.
===Chinese===
"哈！"列文虎克喊了起来，猛地吃了一惊，"哈！那是什么？莫非可能？就在这儿，就在这个地点？容我看看，亲爱的先生——"

话音未落，列文虎克便探出手来，一把抓向佩雷格林的衣领；佩雷格林灵巧地避开了他的手，反用一条有力的臂膀将他擒住，径直往门口拖去，要不容分说把他扔出门去。可他们刚到门口，门却从外面开了，乔治·佩普施冲了进来，身后跟着斯瓦默丹。

列文虎克一望见仇人斯瓦默丹，便拼尽最后的力气从佩雷格林手中挣脱出来，背抵着那间幽禁着美人的神秘房间的房门站定。斯瓦默丹见了，从口袋里取出一具小小的望远镜，一节节拉到全长，扑向他的对手，口中喝道："拔家伙吧，恶棍，你要是还有胆量的话！"

列文虎克手里转眼也多了一具同样的兵器，学着对方的样子把它拉直了，喊道："来吧；我准备好了，你马上就会尝到我的手段。"

两人各自把镜片贴上眼睛，带着凶狠杀气的目光狂暴地扑向对方，又把镜筒一抽一插，将手中的兵器时长时短地耍弄。虚晃、格挡、直刺——总之，击剑学堂里的全套招数悉数登场，而且他们的怒气仿佛一刻盛似一刻。谁只要挨了一下，便放声大叫，腾空跳起，做出种种最奇妙的滑稽腾跃：交叉跳（entrechat）跳得美极了，单足旋转（pirouette）转个不停，赛得过巴黎舞台上最出色的*pas seul*（独舞）名家——直到对手用缩短的望远镜将他牢牢钉住。另一个挨了打，也如法炮制；就这样你来我往，蹦得一次比一次凶，手势一次比一次疯，喊声一次比一次厉。汗珠从他们额头上滚落，血红的眼珠仿佛要从脑壳里迸出来；这场圣维特斯舞蹈病（St. Vitus' dance）一般的发作，除了隔镜互望之外看不出别的缘由，真要被人当成刚从疯人院里逃出来的疯子了。撇开这些不说，这场面倒真好看得很。

===Original===
Swammerdam at last succeeded in driving Leeuwenhoek from his post by the door﻿—which he had maintained with obstinate bravery﻿—and thus carrying on the war in the remoter parts of the ground. George Pepusch saw the opportunity, pressed against the unoccupied door, that was neither barred nor bolted, and slipped into the chamber, but in the next moment he rushed out, exclaiming, “She has fled! Fled!” and then hurried out of the house with the rapidity of lightning.

Both Leeuwenhoek and Swammerdam were seriously wounded, for both hopped and danced about after a mad fashion, and with their howlings and cryings made a music to it that seemed like the shrieks of the damned in hell. Peregrine knew not how to set about separating them, and thus ending a contest, which was as ludicrous as it was terrific. At last the combatants perceived that the door stood wide open, forgot their duel and their pains, put their destructive weapons into their pockets, and rushed into the chamber.

Mr. Tyss took it grievously to heart that the fair one had fled from his house, and wished the abominable Leeuwenhoek at the devil, when the voice of Alina was heard upon the stairs. She was laughing aloud, and muttered between, “What strange things one does see! Wonderful! incredible!”
===Chinese===
斯瓦默丹终于把列文虎克从门前那个岗位上赶开了——那岗位他凭着一股顽固的骁勇死守不放——战火就这样烧到了场地的远端。乔治·佩普施瞅准时机，扑向那扇无人把守、既未上闩也未加杠的门，侧身溜进了房间；可转瞬之间他又冲了出来，喊道："她跑了！跑了！"随即闪电一般奔出了宅子。

列文虎克和斯瓦默丹两个都身负重伤，因为两人全都疯疯癫癫地又蹦又跳，一面还嗥叫哭喊，配出的那套音乐，活像地狱里沉沦亡魂的哀嚎。佩雷格林不知该怎样才能把两人拆开，好结束这场既骇人又可笑的争斗。末了，两位斗士发觉房门四敞大开，霎时忘了决斗、忘了疼痛，把那两件凶器往口袋里一塞，一齐冲进了房间。

美人竟从自己家里逃走了，蒂斯先生为此痛心疾首，正把那可憎的列文虎克咒到魔鬼那儿去，楼梯上忽然传来了阿莉娜的声音。她正放声大笑，笑里还夹着喃喃自语："这世上的怪事可真见得着！稀奇！简直叫人不敢信！"

===Original===
“What?” cried Peregrine dejectedly, “what wonder has happened now?”

“Oh, my dear Mr. Tyss!” exclaimed the old woman, “only come upstairs directly, and go into your chamber.”

And she opened the room-door with a cunning titter. On entering, O wonder! O joy! the little Dörtje Elverdink tripped up to him, in her dress of tissue, as he had before seen her at Mr. Swammer’s.

“At length I see you again!” lisped the little one, and contrived to nestle up so closely to Peregrine, that he could not help embracing her most tenderly in spite of all his good resolutions. His senses seemed ecstacied by love and joy.
===Chinese===
"什么？"佩雷格林垂头丧气地问，"这会儿又出了什么稀奇事？"

"哎呀，我亲爱的蒂斯先生！"老太太喊道，"这就快上楼，到您自己房间里去！"

说着她打开房门，狡黠地嗤嗤一笑。一进门——啊，奇迹！啊，欢喜！小小的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克迈着碎步迎上来，穿着她先前在斯瓦默先生家那身银纱衣裙。

"我可算又见着你了！"小人儿咬着细音说，一面千方百计往佩雷格林身上偎，偎得那样紧，害得他纵有一万个贤明的决心，也忍不住把她至温柔地搂进了怀里。他的官能仿佛被爱与欢喜灌醉了。

===Original===
It has often happened to a man that in the height of his transports he has hit his nose somewhat roughly, and, being suddenly awakened out of his heaven by the earthly pain, has tumbled down again into the vulgar world. Just so it chanced with our Mr. Tyss. In stooping down to kiss Dörtje’s sweet mouth, he gave his nose, of goodly dimensions, a hard blow against the diadem of shining brilliants, which the little one wore in her raven locks. The pain of the blow upon the sharp points of the stone brought him sufficiently to himself to perceive the diadem. The diadem reminded him of the Princess Gamaheh, and with this recollection recurred all that Master Flea had told him of the little siren. He bethought himself that a Princess, the daughter of a mighty king, could not possibly care about his love, and therefore all her pretended affection must be a mere trick, by which the dissembler hoped to regain possession of Master Flea. With this consideration a cold ice stream seemed to rush through his veins, which, if it did not quite extinguish, at least damped, the love flames.

Peregrine gently freed himself from the arms of the little one, who had lovingly embraced him, and said with downcast eyes, “Oh, heavens! you are the daughter of the mighty King Sekakis, the beautiful Gamaheh. Your pardon, princess, if a feeling, which I could not master, hurried me into folly, into madness. But yourself, lady﻿—”

“What are you saying, my fair friend?” interrupted Dörtje Elverdink; “I the daughter of a mighty king? I a princess? I am your Alina, who will love you to distraction, if you﻿—but how is this? Alina, the queen of Golconda? she is already with you; I have spoken with her﻿—a good kind woman, but she has grown old, and is no longer so handsome as in the time of her marriage with the French general. Woe is me! I am not the right one; I never ruled in Golconda. Woe is me!”
===Chinese===
一个男人在情致最酣的当口，鼻子猛地撞上什么东西，也是常有的事：天上的极乐被这一下尘世的疼痛惊破，他便重新跌回到俗世里来。我们这位蒂斯先生赶上的正是这样一桩事。他俯身去吻多尔特耶甜蜜的嘴唇，却教他那只规模可观的鼻子，结结实实撞在小人儿乌黑鬈发间那顶璀璨的钻石宝冠上。宝石的尖端把这一撞的痛楚直送进他的知觉，把他撞得清醒过来，认出了那顶宝冠。宝冠教他想起加玛赫公主；这一想，跳蚤大师对这小塞壬（siren）讲过的种种，便一齐涌回心头。他转念想到：一位公主、一位威势赫赫的君王之女，断断看不上他的爱情；可见她佯装出来的种种柔情，无非是一条诡计，那弄虚作假的人指望借它把跳蚤大师重新弄回手中。想到这里，一股冰冷的急流仿佛冲过他的血管；爱情的火焰纵然不曾被它整个浇熄，至少也被它捂得半暗了。

佩雷格林轻轻从深情搂着他的小人儿臂弯里脱出身来，垂着眼睛说："哦，上天！您是威势赫赫的塞卡基斯王的女儿，美丽的加玛赫。公主，恕罪——是一种我克制不住的感情，把我卷进了痴愚，卷进了疯狂。可是您自己，女士——"

"您在说什么呀，我亲爱的朋友？"多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克打断了他，"我，一位大王的女儿？我，一位公主？我是您的阿莉娜呀，只要您——可这是怎么回事？阿莉娜，戈尔康达女王？她已经在您这儿了；我还同她说过话呢——一位善良和气的女人，只是上了年纪，不比她嫁那位法国将军的时候标致了。我真苦啊！我不是那一个；我从来没在戈尔康达做过女王。我真苦啊！"

===Original===
The little one had closed her eyes, and began to totter. Peregrine conveyed her to a sofa.

“Gamaheh!” she went on, speaking in a state of somnambulism, “Gamaheh, do you say? Gamaheh, the daughter of King Sekakis? Yes, I recollect, in Famagusta! I was indeed a beautiful tulip﻿—Yet no, even then I felt desire and love in my breast. Still, still on that point﻿ ﻿…”

She was silent, and seemed to be falling into a perfect slumber. Peregrine undertook the perilous enterprise of placing her in a more convenient position, but, as he gently embraced her, a concealed pin prickled him sharply in the finger. According to his custom he snapt his fingers, and Master Flea, taking it for the concerted signal, immediately placed the microscopic glass in his eye.
===Chinese===
小人儿闭上了眼睛，身子开始摇晃。佩雷格林把她搀到一张沙发上。

"加玛赫！"她梦游似的接着往下说，"加玛赫，你说？加玛赫，塞卡基斯王的女儿？是的，我想起来了，在法马古斯塔！我那时确实是一株美丽的郁金香——可是不，就连那时，我胸中也已怀着渴望与爱了。嘘，嘘，说到那一点……"

她不作声了，仿佛渐渐沉入酣睡。佩雷格林着手进行那桩冒险的事业，想把她安置得舒坦些；可他轻轻搂住她的时候，一根暗藏的别针狠狠扎了他指头一下。他照着习惯弹了个响指，跳蚤大师只当这是约定的暗号，当即把显微镜片嵌进了他的眼睛。

===Original===
Now, as usual, Peregrine saw behind the tunicle of the eyes the strange interweaving of nerves and veins, which pierced deep into the brain. But with these were twined bright silver threads, a hundred times thinner than the thinnest spider’s web, and it was these very threads that confused him, for they seemed to be endless, branching out into something indistinguishable even by the microscopic eye; perhaps they were thoughts of a sublimer kind, the others of a sort more easily comprehended. Then he observed flowers, strangely blended, which took the shape of men, then again men, who dissolved as it were into the earth, and peeped forth again as stones and metals. Amongst these all manner of beasts were in motion, who underwent innumerable changes, and spoke strange languages. No one appearance answered to the other, and in the plaintive sounds of sorrow that filled the air, there was a dissonance, corresponding with that of the images. But it was this very dissonance that ennobled still more the deep fundamental harmony, which broke out triumphantly, and united all that seemed irreconcileable.

“Do not puzzle yourself,” whispered Master Flea, “do not puzzle yourself, my good Peregrine; those which you see, are the images of a dream. Even if anything more should lurk behind them, now is not the time for farther inquiry. Only call the little deceiver by her real name, and then sift her as much as you please.”

As the lady had many names, it must have been difficult, one would have thought, for Peregrine to hit upon the right, but, without the least reflection, he exclaimed, “Dörtje Elverdink! dear, charming girl; was it no deceit? Is it possible that you can love me?”
===Chinese===
和先前一样，佩雷格林隔着眼睛的薄膜，看见神经与血管奇异地交织着，深深探入脑中。可这一回，其中还缠绕着一些明亮的银线，比最细的蛛丝还要细上百倍；偏偏就是这些银线叫他迷惑，因为它们仿佛无穷无尽，分出的枝杈连显微的眼睛也分辨不清——它们或许是更高一层的思想，其余那些不过是较易领会的种类罢了。接着他又看见一些花朵，奇异地混杂交融，化作人形；又看见人重新消融进泥土，再以石头和金属的形态探头露面。在这些东西中间，各样走兽游走不停，经历着数不清的形变，说着奇怪的语言。没有一幅景象与另一幅相应相合；满空回荡的哀惋悲声里，也有一种与这些景象相应的失调之音。然而恰恰是这失调之音，把那深沉的根本和声衬托得愈发崇高——和声凯旋一般迸发出来，把一切仿佛势不两立的东西都统一了起来。

"别绞这份脑汁了，"跳蚤大师悄声说，"别绞这份脑汁了，我好心的佩雷格林；你看见的这些，是一场梦的影像。就算它们背后还藏着别的什么，眼下也不是深究的时候。你只消喊出这小骗子真正的名字，然后随你怎么盘问都行。"

这位女士名号众多，照理说，佩雷格林要一语中的，怕是颇有难度；可他不假半分思索，脱口喊道："多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克！亲爱的、迷人的姑娘；这一切都不是骗局吧？你当真爱得上我么？"

===Original===
Immediately the little one awoke from her dreamy state, opened her eye, and said with burning glance, “What a doubt, my Peregrine! Could a maiden do as I have done, unless her breast were filled with the most glowing passion? Peregrine, I love you more than anyone, and, if you will be mine, I am yours with my whole soul, and remain with you because I cannot leave you, and not merely to escape from the tyranny of my uncle.”

The silver threads had disappeared, and the thoughts, properly arranged, ran thus: “How is this? At first I feigned a passion for him only to regain Master Flea for myself and Leeuwenhoek, and now I actually am fond of him. I have caught myself in my own snares. I think no more of Master Flea, and would like to be his, who seems lovelier to me than any man I have ever seen.”

It may be easily supposed what effect these thoughts produced in Peregrine’s breast. He fell on his knees before the fair one, covered her hand with a thousand burning kisses, called her his joy, his heaven, his whole happiness.
===Chinese===
小人儿当即从迷梦中醒来，睁开眼睛，目光灼灼地说："这是哪门子的疑心，我的佩雷格林！一个姑娘家，若不是胸中燃着最炽烈的情火，能做出我做的这一切么？佩雷格林，我爱你胜过世上一切人；你若肯属于我，我便是你的，连同我整个的灵魂；我留在你身边，是因为我离不开你，并不只是为了逃脱我叔父的暴虐。"

银线消失了，心思排布得井然有序，是这样转的："这是怎么回事？起初我对他佯装痴情，只为把跳蚤大师夺回来——还给我，也给列文虎克；如今我竟真的喜欢上他了。我把自己织的罗网套上了自己。跳蚤大师，我不再想他了；我愿意属于眼前这个人——在我看来，他比我见过的任何男人都可亲可爱。"

这些念头在佩雷格林胸中激起怎样的波澜，是不难想见的。他跪倒在美人面前，千百个滚烫的吻落满她的手，唤她作他的欢喜、他的天堂、他全部的幸福。

===Original===
“Well!” lisped the maiden, drawing him gently to her side, “well, my love, you certainly will not deny a request, on the fulfilment of which depends the repose, nay, the very existence of your beloved.”

“Demand,” replied Peregrine, tenderly embracing her, “demand anything, my life﻿—anything you will. Your slightest wish is my command. Nothing in the world is so dear to me that I would not with pleasure sacrifice it to you and your affection.”

“Woe is me!” lisped Master Flea, “who could have imagined that the little traitress would have conquered? I am lost!”

“Hear then,” replied Gamaheh, after having returned with equal fire the glowing kisses, which Peregrine imprinted on her lips, “Hear then: I know how the﻿—”
===Chinese===
"那么！"少女咬着细音说，轻轻把他拉到自己身旁，"那么，我的爱人，你总不至于回绝我一个请求吧——你的心上人的安宁，不，连她的性命，可全悬在这个请求能不能如愿上呢。"

"说吧，"佩雷格林答道，温柔地搂着她，"说吧，要什么都行，我的命根儿——随你要什么。你最轻微的一句心愿，就是给我的圣旨。世上再没有什么东西贵重到我不肯欢欢喜喜献给你、献给你的爱情的。"

"我真苦啊！"跳蚤大师咬着细音叹道，"谁能想得到，这小叛徒居然得胜了？我完啦！"

"那就听着，"加玛赫答道——她已用同样炽烈的火，回报了佩雷格林印上她双唇的滚烫亲吻——"那就听着：我知道那——"

===Original===
The door burst open, and in rushed George Pepusch.

“Zeherit!” cried the little one in despair, and fell back on the sofa, senseless.

The Thistle, Zeherit, flew to the princess, took her in his arms, and ran off with the speed of lightning.

For this time Master Flea was saved.
===Chinese===
房门砰地大开，乔治·佩普施冲了进来。

"泽赫里特！"小人儿绝望地喊了一声，直挺挺晕倒在沙发上，人事不省。

蓟草精泽赫里特扑向公主，把她抱进怀里，闪电一般飞奔而去。

这一回，跳蚤大师总算脱了险。

## V Fifth Adventure / 五 第五奇遇



===Original===
With the speed of lightning﻿—as the reader has already learned at the conclusion of the fourth adventure﻿—George Pepusch snatched the fair one from the arms of the enamoured Peregrine, and left him behind petrified with astonishment and terror. When at length the latter came to his recollection, and would have followed his robber-friend, all was still and desolate in the house. Upon his repeated calling, the old Alina came pattering up the stairs from one of the farthest rooms, and declared that she had not observed any, the slightest part of the whole business.

Peregrine was nigh going mad at the loss of Dörtje, but Master Flea began to console him in a tone that must have inspired the most desperate with confidence: “You are not yet quite certain, my dear Mr. Peregrine, whether the fair Dörtje Elverdink has really left your house. As well as I can judge of such things, she is not far off; I seem to feel her nearness. But, if you will follow my friendly counsel, you will leave her to her fate. Trust me, she is as capricious as the wind. It may be, as you have said, that she now is really fond of you, but how long will it be before she plunges you into such misery, that you will be in danger from it of losing your reason, like the Thistle, Zeherit? I say again, give up your lonely way of life. You will be the better for it. How many women have you known, that you should take Dörtje for the handsomest of her sex? What maiden have you approached with love, that you should believe that Dörtje alone can love you? Go to, Peregrine; experience will show you better. You are a well-made, handsome man, and I should not be so keen-sighted, as Master Flea really is, if I could not see beforehand that love would smile upon you in a very different way from what you may expect.”

Peregrine had already broken the ice by going abroad in public places, and it was therefore the less difficult for him to visit societies, from which he had formerly withdrawn himself. In this Master Flea rendered him excellent service with his microscopic glass, and he is said during this time to have kept a daybook, and to have made notes of the most remarkable and pleasant contradictions between words and thoughts, as they daily occurred to him. Perhaps the editor of this strange tale, called *Master Flea*, may find some future opportunity of bringing to light many worthy impartments from this same daybook; here it would only stop the current of the history, and, therefore, would not be welcome to the reader. So much, however, may be said, that many of the phrases with the corresponding thoughts seemed to be stereotyped as it were; as for example, “Favour me with your advice,” the thought being, “He is fool enough to think I ask his advice in a matter that I have long since resolved upon, and that tickles him.” “I have the most perfect confidence in you,” the thought being, “I knew long ago that you were a scoundrel,” etc. It should also be mentioned that many folks mightily puzzled Peregrine with his microscopic observations. These were the young men, who fell into raptures upon everything, and poured themselves forth in a torrent of splendid phrases. Amongst these the most remarkable were the young poets, who were boiling over with imagination and genius, and were particularly adored by the ladies. To these were associated the bluestockings, who were as familiar with metaphysics as the less learned part of their sex with scandal, and could talk like any parson in his pulpit. If it seemed strange to Peregrine that the silver threads should twine together out of Gamaheh’s brain into an undistinguishable something, he was not a little astonished at what he saw in the heads of those above mentioned. He saw indeed the strange weaving of nerves and veins, but remarked at the same time, that when the owners of them spoke most learnedly on art and science, they did not penetrate the brain, but were reflected outwards, so that all recognition of the thoughts was out of the question. He imparted his observation to Master Flea, who usually sat in a fold of his neckcloth, and Master Flea was of the opinion that what Peregrine took for thoughts were in reality none, but merely words, which in vain endeavoured to become thoughts.

===Chinese===
正如读者在第四奇遇结尾处已经获知的那样，乔治·佩普施迅若闪电，从痴情入迷的佩雷格林怀中夺走了那位美人，把他撂在原地，惊骇得呆若木石。待他终于回过神来，想去追赶那位强盗朋友时，宅子里已是一片死寂凄清。他再三呼唤，老阿莉娜才从最深远处的一间屋子里啪嗒啪嗒地奔上楼来，一口咬定这桩事情从头到尾、连一丝一毫她都不曾瞧见。

痛失多尔特耶，佩雷格林险些发疯。跳蚤大师却开口劝起他来，那口吻，任凭最绝望的人听了也必定生出信心：“亲爱的佩雷格林先生，你还不能完全确定，美丽的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克是不是真的离开了你的家。凭我对此类事情的判断，她走得不远；我仿佛觉出她就在近处。不过，你若肯听我这句友善的忠告，就随她去自安天命吧。相信我，她任性得就像风。也许真如你所说，她如今是真心喜欢你；可要过多久，她就会把你掷进那样的苦海，害得你因此大有丧失神智之虞，就像蓟草精泽赫里特那样？我再说一遍：抛掉你那离群索居的活法吧，这对你只有好处。你见识过多少女人，就把多尔特耶当作她那一性里最美的？你又用爱情亲近过哪一位姑娘，就认定唯独多尔特耶能爱你？去吧，佩雷格林，阅历自会教你看明白。你身量匀称，相貌堂堂；倘若我竟不能预先看出，爱情将以一种与你所料全然不同的方式朝你微笑，那我就算不得目光如炬——跳蚤大师本该是目光如炬的。”

佩雷格林既已在公共场所露面、破了坚冰，再去那些他从前自行退出的社交场，便不那么为难了。在这上头，跳蚤大师的显微镜片为他出了大力气。据说他在那段时间里还记着一本日记簿，把每日遇到的言语与心思之间最可观、最有趣的矛盾一一录了下来。这部题为《跳蚤大师》（*Master Flea*）的奇书的编者，将来也许有机会，从这本日记簿里披露出许多弥足珍贵的见闻；搁在这里，它们只会截断史流的进程，因而不会讨读者喜欢。不过，有几句却尽可一说：许多套话连同与之相应的心思，简直像是浇成了铅版。譬如嘴上说：“请不吝赐教。”心里想的是：“他傻透了，竟当真以为我会就一件早已拿定主意的事去请教他，这可正搔着他的痒处。”嘴上说：“我对您有完全的信任。”心里想的是：“你是个恶棍，我早就知道了。”诸如此类。还应提上一句：有好些人，叫佩雷格林的显微观察大大地犯了难。这便是那些对什么都狂热惊叹、把漂亮辞藻像激流一样泼洒出来的年轻人。其中最可观的，又数那些想象力和天才都沸腾四溢、尤其受太太们崇拜的年轻诗人。与他们同列的还有那些蓝袜才女：她们熟稔形而上学的程度，不亚于她们同性中学问稍逊的那部分人熟稔绯闻韵事的程度，讲起话来，个个赛过登坛布道的牧师。如果说，加玛赫脑中的银丝绞缠成一个无从分辨的东西，已叫佩雷格林觉得奇怪，那么他在上述这些人脑袋里看到的，就远不止是奇怪了。他确实看见了神经与血管奇异的编织，可同时也察觉：每当它们的主人就艺术与科学大发宏论，那些话并不曾钻进脑子里去，而是向外反射了出来，因此要想认出个念头，全然无从谈起。他把这观察说给跳蚤大师听——大师惯常坐在他衣领的褶缝里——跳蚤大师认为，佩雷格林当作念头的那些东西，其实根本不是念头，只是些徒然想变成念头的字眼罢了。

===Original===
If Mr. Tyss began now to amuse himself in society, his faithful companion also laid aside much of his gravity, and exhibited himself as a knavish little voluptuary, an amiable *roué*. He could not see the fair neck or the white bosom of any beauty, without slipping out of his hiding place with the first opportunity, and springing on the inviting spot, where he very dexterously contrived to elude the attacks of pursuing fingers. This manoeuvre combined a double interest. In the first place, he found a pleasure in it for the thing itself, and then, he hoped, by drawing Peregrine’s attention to the fair ones, to cast Dörtje’s image into shadow. This however seemed to be a fruitless labour, for none of all the ladies, whom he now approached without the least timidity, seemed to him so fair and lovely as his little princess. The great cause however of his continued constancy was that in none he found the words and thoughts so united in his favour as with her. He was convinced that he could never leave her, and this he repeated incessantly. Master Flea was in no little alarm.

One day Peregrine remarked that the old Alina laughed very cunningly, took snuff more frequently than usual, muttered strangely﻿—in short, acted altogether like one who is big with a secret and would fain be disburdened of it. To everything she replied, “Yes, one can’t tell that! one must wait!” whether these words were suited to the occasion or not, till at last Peregrine, full of impatience, exclaimed, “Speak it out at once; tell me what is the matter, without creeping around me with those mysterious looks.”

“Ah!” cried the old woman, clasping her withered hands together, “ah! the dear little thing! the sweet little puppet!”

===Chinese===
蒂斯先生既开始在社交场里取乐，他那位忠实的伙伴也撂下了几分庄重，露出一副促狭的小色鬼做派，一个招人疼的*roué*（放荡子）。但凡撞见哪位美人露出皓颈或雪白的酥胸，他必定瞅准头一个机会从藏身处溜出来，蹦上那块诱人的地方，又极灵巧地躲闪开追捕上来的手指。这一手包含着双重的兴味：其一，事情本身就叫他快活；其二，他指望把佩雷格林的注意引到众美人身上，好多把多尔特耶的倩影罩进阴影。可这看来是白费力气：他如今毫不怯场地亲近过的这些太太小姐里，没有一个在他眼里及得上他的小公主那样又美又可爱。而他这份恒心所以维系不坠，最大的缘由是：无论在谁身上，他都不曾见过言辞与心思这样齐心合意地偏袒他。他坚信自己永远离不开她，这话他也絮絮叨叨念个没完。跳蚤大师着实捏了一把汗。

一天，佩雷格林发觉老阿莉娜笑得诡里诡气，吸鼻烟比往常更勤，嘴里念念有词——总而言之，一举一动活像个怀揣秘密、巴不得赶紧卸下的人。不论什么事她都回一句：“是呀，这谁说得准呢！总得等等看！”这话应景不应景，全然不论。末了佩雷格林按捺不住，嚷道：“有话痛快说出来！到底出了什么事？别再拿那副神神秘秘的做派围着我打转了。”

“啊！”老太太喊道，两只干瘪的手紧紧扣在一起，“啊！那个亲爱的小东西！那个可爱的小傀儡！”

===Original===
“Whom do you mean?” asked Peregrine angrily.

“Ah!” said the old woman, smirking. “Ah! whom should I mean but our princess, below here with Mr. Swammer﻿—your bride, Mr. Tyss?”

“Woman!” cried Mr. Tyss, “unlucky woman, she is here! In the house! And you do not tell me till now?”

“Where,” replied the old woman, without in the least losing her composure, “Where should the princess be but here, where she has found her mother?”

“How!” cried Peregrine, “what is it you say, Alina?”

===Chinese===
“你说的是谁呀？”佩雷格林怒冲冲地问。

“嗳！”老太太嬉皮笑脸地说，“嗳！除了咱们那位公主，还能说谁？就在这儿楼下，斯瓦默先生那儿——您的新娘哪，蒂斯先生！”

“女人！”蒂斯先生喊道，“不吉利的女人，她在这儿！就在这座宅子里！你竟拖到这会儿才告诉我？”

“不然呢，”老太太答道，丝毫不见慌乱，“不然公主要能在哪儿？可不就在这儿，在她寻见了亲娘的地方？”

“什么！”佩雷格林喊道，“你说什么，阿莉娜？”

===Original===
“Yes,” rejoined the old woman, drawing herself up, “Yes, Alina is my right name, and who knows what else may come to light, in a short time, before your nuptials?”

Peregrine entreated her, by all the angels and devils, to go on, but, without paying the least attention to his hurry, she seated herself snugly in the armchair, drew out her snuffbox, took a prodigious pinch, and demonstrated to Peregrine very circumstantially, that there was no worse failing than impatience.

“Calmness, my son; calmness is above all things requisite, or otherwise you run the risk of losing all in the moment that you think you have gained it. Before you get a word out of me, you must first promise to seat yourself there, quite quietly like a pretty-behaved child, and for the life of you not to interrupt me in my story.”

Nothing was left to Peregrine but to obey the old woman, who, when he had seated himself, related things that were strange enough to hear.

===Chinese===
“正是，”老太太应道，挺直了腰板，“正是，阿莉娜正是我的本名。再说，赶在您大喜之前这短短几日，谁知道还会有什么水落石出呢？”

佩雷格林指天誓日，把一切天使和魔鬼都请了出来，只求她接着往下讲；她却半点不理会他的心急，自顾自舒舒服服往扶手椅里一坐，摸出鼻烟盒，狠狠吸了一大撮，然后掰开揉碎地向佩雷格林论证：天下的毛病，再没有比性急更坏的了。

“要沉得住气，我的儿；沉住气比什么都要紧。不然的话，就在你自以为大功告成的那一瞬，你就有把到手的一切统统赔掉的危险。想从我嘴里掏出一个字，你得先答应：规规矩矩坐到那边去，像个懂事听话的孩子，任凭什么也休得打断我讲故事。”

佩雷格林别无他法，只好依了老太太。等他坐定，老太太讲出来的那些事，可真够离奇的。

===Original===
According to the old woman’s tale, the two gentlemen, namely, Swammerdam and Leeuwenhoek, had another tough struggle in the chamber, and for a time kept up a terrible clatter. Then again all had become quite still, when a heavy moaning had made her fancy that one of the two was mortally wounded, but on peeping through the keyhole she perceived something quite different from what she had expected. Swammerdam and Leeuwenhoek had seized George Pepusch, and stroked and squeezed him with their fists, so that he grew thinner and thinner, during which operation he had uttered the moans heard by the old woman. At last, when he had grown as thin as a thistle-stem, they had tried to squeeze him through the keyhole, and the poor Pepusch was hanging with half his body out, when she ran away in terror. Soon afterwards she heard a loud laughing, and saw Pepusch in his natural form, quietly led out of the house by the two magicians, while at the room door stood Dörtje and beckoned her in. The little one wished to dress herself, and needed her assistance.

The old woman could not talk enough of the great heap of clothes which the princess brought out of a variety of chests and showed to her, each of which had appeared richer than the other. She declared that none but an Indian princess could possess such jewels as the little one; her eyes still ached with the glitter. She then went on to say how, during the dressing, she had talked of this and that, of the late Mr. Tyss, on the delightful life they had formerly led in the house, and at last the conversation had fallen upon her deceased relations.

“You know, my dear Mr. Tyss, that nothing is more valued by me than my late cousin, the calico-printer’s wife. She was in Maintz, and, I believe, even in the Indies, and could speak French and sing. If I owe to my cousin the unchristian name of Alina, I will forgive her that in the grave, since it is from her alone that I have learned polite manners and the art of speaking elegantly. As I was talking much of my cousin, the little princess asked after my father, my grandfather, and so on, higher and higher up the family. I opened my heart to her, told her that my mother had been almost as handsome as myself, except that I go beyond her in regard to the nose, which I derive from my father, and which is after the shape that has been usual in the family since the memory of man. Then I came to speak of the country wake, when I waltzed with Sergeant Drumstick, and wore the skyblue stockings with red clocks. Ah, dear God! we are all weak, sinful creatures! But oh! Mr. Tyss, you should have seen how the little princess, who at first had laughed and tittered, that it was a pleasure to hear her, now grew more and more quiet, and gazed on me with such odd looks, that I began to be terribly alarmed. And then think, Mr. Tyss, on a sudden, before I could prevent it, she lies on her knees before me, and will positively kiss my hand, exclaiming, ‘Yes, it is you! Now I recognise you! It is yourself!’ and when, quite astonished, I asked what it all meant﻿—”

===Chinese===
照老太太的说法，那两位老爷——就是斯瓦默丹和列文虎克——在房间里又狠斗了一场，有一阵子闹得叮当乱响、天翻地覆。随后又一切归于寂静，一阵沉重的呻吟声让她以为两人中必有一个受了致命伤；可凑到钥匙孔上一瞧，她看见的却同她料想的完全两样。斯瓦默丹和列文虎克逮住了乔治·佩普施，用拳头又捋又挤，把他挤得越来越薄；在那道工序里，他发出的，正是老太太听见的那声声呻吟。末了，等他薄得像一根蓟草茎了，两人便打算把他从钥匙孔里挤出去；可怜的佩普施半个身子还悬在外头，她已吓得撒腿跑了。不多时，她听见一阵放声大笑，又看见佩普施恢复了原形，被两位术士安安静静地领出了宅子；房门口站着多尔特耶，招手叫她进去。小姑娘要梳妆打扮，用得着她的帮衬。

公主从一口口箱子里搬出好大一堆衣裳，拿给她一一看过，一件比一件华贵——这个话头，老太太怎么也说不腻。她断言，除了印度的公主，谁也置办不出小姑娘那样的珠宝；那阵珠光宝气，晃得她眼睛到现在还发疼。她接着又讲，梳妆的时候，她东拉西扯，说起已故的蒂斯先生，说起他们从前在这宅子里过的快活日子，末了，话头落到了她那些亡故的亲眷身上。

“您是知道的，亲爱的蒂斯先生，这世上再没有比我那位故去的表姐更叫我高看的了，就是那位印花布匠的娘子。她到过美因茨（Maintz），我相信还到过东印度，会讲法国话，还会唱歌。我这条非基督徒的名字阿莉娜虽是拜她所赐，我到了坟里也会饶她这一条，因为我的礼貌规矩、我谈吐的雅致功夫，全是打她一个人那儿学来的。我正絮絮地夸我这位表姐，小公主便问起我父亲、我祖父，如此这般，一代高过一代，往上数去。我向她敞开了心，告诉她我母亲当年差不多同我一样标致，只是鼻子一处我比她还强；这鼻子是我从父亲那儿承下来的，论形状，自打有人记事起，我们家家门里传的就是这一副。接着我讲到那年的乡村节场，我同鼓槌中士（Sergeant Drumstick）跳华尔兹，穿的是天蓝色长袜、袜侧绣着红花边。啊，亲爱的上帝！我们都是软弱有罪的受造物！可是啊，蒂斯先生，您真该瞧瞧：小公主起先还咯咯直笑，笑得人听着都欢喜，后来却越来越安静，用那么古怪的眼神望着我，望得我心里发起毛来。您再想想，蒂斯先生，冷不防地，我还没来得及拦阻，她竟跪到了我面前，还非要亲我的手不可，嘴里喊着：‘是的，就是您！这下我认出您了！正是您本人！’我惊得呆了，问这都是什么意思——”

===Original===
Here the old woman stopped, and, when Peregrine pressed her to go on, she with great gravity and precision took a mighty pinch of snuff, and said,

“You’ll know in good time, my son, what farther happened. Everything has its time and hour.”

He was now more urgent than ever with the old woman to proceed, when she burst out into a roaring fit of laughter, upon which he admonished her, with a very sour face, that his room was not exactly the place for her to play off such fooleries. But the old woman, planting her hands in her sides, seemed ready to burst. The burning red of her brow changed to an agreeable mahogany, and Peregrine was upon the point of flinging a glass of water into the old woman’s face, when she recovered her breath and speech at the same time.

“I can’t help laughing,” she said, “I can’t help laughing at the foolish little thing. No; such love is no longer on earth. Only think, Mr. Tyss﻿—”

Here she broke out into a fresh fit of laughter, and Peregrine’s patience was well nigh exhausted. At last, with much difficulty, he got out of her that the little princess had taken up the whimsical notion of Mr. Tyss being positively determined to marry the old woman, and had compelled her solemnly to promise to reject his hand.

===Chinese===
老太太说到这里打住了。佩雷格林催她往下讲，她便端出十二分的庄严和条理，狠狠吸了一大撮鼻烟，说道：

“往后又出了什么事，我的儿，到了时辰你自然会知道。万事都有各自的时辰。”

他比先前更急地催老太太往下讲，她却轰然爆出一阵大笑。他于是拉下一张酸溜溜的脸教训她：他这间屋子，未必是她耍这路蠢把戏的地方。可老太太双手往腰间一叉，眼看就要笑破了肚皮。她额头上火烧般的红，渐渐变成一种怪受看的桃花心木色，佩雷格林正要把一杯水泼到老太太脸上，她这才一口气和嗓子同时找了回来。

“我忍不住要笑，”她说，“想起那傻乎乎的小东西我就忍不住。不；这样的爱情，如今世上是再没有了。您只要想想，蒂斯先生——”

说到这儿她又爆出一阵新的狂笑，佩雷格林的耐性眼看就要告罄。费了好大的劲，他才从她嘴里套出实情来：小公主竟起了那样一个怪念头，认定蒂斯先生是铁了心要娶这老太太，还逼着她郑重许下诺言，回绝他的求婚。

===Original===
It seemed to Peregrine as if he were mixed up in a scene of witchery, and he felt so strangely, that even the honest old Alina appeared to him a supernatural kind of being, from whom he could not fly with sufficient speed. But she still detained him, having something to communicate in all haste, that concerned the little princess.

“It is now certain,” she said confidentially, “It is now certain, my dear Mr. Tyss, that the bright star of fortune has arisen, but it is your business to keep it favourable. When I protested to the little one that you were desperately smitten with her, and far from any idea of marrying me, she replied, that she could not be convinced of it and give you her hand till you had complied with a wish that had long sat near her heart. She says that she had a pretty little negro boy in her service who had fled from her; I have indeed denied it, but she maintains that the boy is so little he might live in a nutshell.”

“Nothing will ever come of this,” exclaimed Peregrine violently, well knowing what the old woman was driving at, and rushed out of the room, and then out of the house, with great vehemence.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林只觉得自己搅进了一场妖法排演的戏文，浑身那样地异样，连老实的阿莉娜在他眼里都成了一种超自然的生灵，只恨自己的腿脚逃得不够快。可她还不放他走，说有一件关乎小公主的事，得赶紧向他交代。

“如今是十拿九稳了，”她凑近了咬耳朵，“如今是十拿九稳了，亲爱的蒂斯先生：福星已经升起，可要保住它的吉照，还得靠您的经营。我向小姑娘分辩，说您为她神魂颠倒，压根儿没有娶我的念头；她却回话说，您不先依了她一桩心愿，她就信不过这一层，也不能把手伸给您——那桩心愿在她心口窝里揣得好久了。她说，先前有个漂亮的小黑人男孩伺候她，后来从她那儿逃走了；我一口咬定没这回事，可她硬说那孩子小得很，住得进一枚坚果壳里。”

“这事是万万成不了的！”佩雷格林勃然喊道——老太太这话打的什么算盘，他再清楚不过——随即冲出房间，又一路冲出了宅子。

===Original===
It is an established custom, that when the hero of a tale is under any violent agitation, he should run out into a forest, or at least, into some lonely wood, and the custom is good, because it really prevails in life. Hence it could not be otherwise with Mr. Tyss, than that he ran from his house without stopping, till he had left the city behind him and reached a remote wood. Moreover, as in a romantic history no wood must be without rustling leaves, sighing breezes, murmuring brooks, etc., etc. it is to be supposed that Peregrine found all these things in his place of refuge. Upon a mossy stone, the lower half of which lay in a bright brook, Peregrine sat down with a firm resolution to reflect on his strange adventures, and, if possible, find the Ariadne clue which might show the way out of this labyrinth of mysteries. The murmurs of the leaves, returning at equal intervals, the monotonous babbling of the waters, the constant clap, clap of a distant mill, soon formed a ground which regulated the thoughts so that they no longer rushed wildly together without time or rhythms, but became an intelligible melody. Thus, after sitting some time on this pleasant spot, he got to reflect calmly.

“In reality,” he said to himself, “a fantastic tale-writer could not have invented wilder events than I have actually gone through in the short space of a few days. Beauty, love itself visits the lonely misogynist, and a look, a word, is sufficient to fan, in his breast, the flames which he had dreaded without knowing them. But the time, the place, the whole appearance of the strange siren are so mysterious, that it seems to be the result of magic. And then it is not long before a despised little insect evinces knowledge, understanding﻿—nay, even a sort of supernatural power. And this creature talks of things which to common minds are incomprehensible, in a way as if it all were nothing more than the familiar today and yesterday of usual life, as it appears repeated for the thousandth time.

“Have I come too near the flywheel, that dark unknown powers are driving, and has it caught me in its whirlings? Would not one believe, that the reason must be lost with such things, when they cross the path of life? And yet I find myself quite well, withal: nay, it no longer seems strange to me that a Flea King should have sought my protection, and in requital have entrusted me with a mystery that opens to me the secrets of thought, and thus sets me above the deceptions of life. But whither will or can all this lead? How, if under this singular mask of a flea, an evil demon lurked, who sought to lure me into destruction, who aimed to rob me of all the happiness that might bloom to me in the possession of Dörtje?﻿ ﻿… Were it not better to get rid at once of the little monster?”

===Chinese===
自古便有一条规矩：凡小说里的主人公心头掀起狂澜，必得奔进一座森林，至少也要奔进一片僻静的树林；这规矩是好的，因为人生里本就盛行此道。所以蒂斯先生也只能如此：从家里一口气跑个不停，把城市甩在身后，直跑到一处幽远的树林里。况且，在一段浪漫史里，树林断不可没有飒飒的叶、叹息的风、淙淙的溪，如此等等——因此不妨认定，佩雷格林在他的避难所里，把这些一样不缺地全找到了。一方长满青苔的石头，下半截浸在一道明亮的溪水里，佩雷格林在上头坐定，横下心来，要把他的奇遇细想一遍，倘若做得到，还要寻出那条阿里阿德涅（Ariadne）的引线，好走出这座神秘的迷宫。叶声按着均匀的间歇一浪一浪归来，溪水单调地絮语，远处磨坊的轮子啪嗒、啪嗒响个不停——这些很快就铺成一层底子，把思想纳入节拍，使它们不再乱糟糟地拥作一团、既无拍子也无韵律，而成了一支听得明白的曲子。就这样，在这处惬意的所在坐了一阵之后，他终于能够平心静气地思索了。

“说实在的，”他自忖道，“再狂放的传奇作家，也编不出比我这几天亲身经见的更荒唐的事体。美人，爱情本身，登门造访了那个孤僻的厌女者；一个眼神、一句话，便足以在他胸中煽起那他从前不曾识得、便已畏惧的火焰。可是那时辰、那地方、那位奇怪的海妖整个的现身，又如此神秘，透着魔法使然的底子。再者，没过多久，一只为人不齿的小虫子又显露出了学问、见识——不，甚至还有一种超自然的力量。这造物谈起那些在寻常头脑听来全然不可解的事情，那口气竟仿佛这一切不过是寻常生活里熟稔的今朝与昨夕，是重演了第一千遍的老戏。”

“我是不是凑得太近，撞上了那具由幽暗的无名力量驱转的飞轮，叫它把我卷进了旋涡里？这样的事情横穿人生的来路，谁不会说，理智非丢失不可？可我却自觉安然无恙：不，一位跳蚤国王曾寻求我的庇护、并以一桩奥妙相托作报——那奥妙替我打开思想的秘密，从而把我高高举起，超脱了人生的种种欺瞒——这在我眼里竟已不再显得奇怪。可这一切将要、又能把我引向何处？倘若这张跳蚤的奇异面具底下潜伏着一个恶灵，一心诱我走向毁灭，一心要夺走我据有多尔特耶时可能向我绽放的全部幸福，那又当如何？……与其如此，何不趁早摆脱掉这个小怪物？”

===Original===
“That was a very pitiful idea, Mr. Tyss!” exclaimed Master Flea, interrupting Peregrine’s soliloquy. “Do you imagine that the mystery I have entrusted to you is a trifle? Should not this gift pass for the most decided proof of my sincere friendship? Shame on you for being suspicious! You are surprised at the reason, the mind, of a little despised insect, and that proves﻿—don’t be offended﻿—the narrowness of your education in science. I wish, in regard to the thinking instinctive soul of animals, you had read the Greek Philo, or, at least, the treatise of Hieronymus Rorarius, *Quod Animalia Bruta Ratione Utantur Melius Homine*; or his oration *Pro Muribus*; or that you knew what Lipsius and the great Leibnitz thought of the mental power of beasts; or that you were aware what the profound Rabbi Maimonides has said about their souls; you would not then take me for a demon on account of my understanding, or measure the spiritual faculties by the proportions of the body. I suppose, at last, you will come to the shrewd opinion of the Spanish physician, Gomez Pereira, who could find nothing more in animals than mere artificial machines, without thought or freedom of will, moving arbitrarily and automatically. Yet, no: I cannot deem you so absurd, and am convinced that you have long ago learned better through my humble person. Moreover, I do not well understand what you call wonders, or in what way you are able to divide into the wonderful and natural, the appearances of our being, which﻿—in reality﻿—are ourselves, as we and they mutually condition each other. Do not, therefore, wonder at anything because it has not yet occurred to you, or because you fancy you do not see the connection of cause and effect; that only proves the natural or diseased obtuseness of your sight, which injures your perception. But﻿—do not take it amiss, Mr. Peregrine﻿—the drollest part of the business is that you want to split yourself into two parts, one of which recognises and willingly believes the so-called wonders; the other, on the contrary, is mightily astonished at this recognition and belief. Has it ever occurred to you, that you believe in the images of dreams?”

“I!” exclaimed Peregrine, “My dear fellow, how can you talk of dreams, which are only the result of some disorder in our corporeal or intellectual structure?”

At these words Master Flea burst into a laugh, as fine as it was mocking, and then said to Mr. Tyss, who was not a little confounded,

===Chinese===
“这可真是个可怜的念头，蒂斯先生！”跳蚤大师打断了佩雷格林的独白，喊道，“你莫非以为，我托付给你的那桩奥妙，是件不值一提的小事？这份馈赠，难道不该算作我真挚友情最确凿的凭据？你竟起疑心，真该臊得慌！你对一只小小贱虫的理智、心智大惊小怪，而这恰恰证明了——你可别见怪——你科学教养的偏狭。但愿你就动物的思维本能之灵魂，读过希腊人斐洛（Philo）的著作；或至少读过希罗尼穆斯·罗拉里乌斯（Hieronymus Rorarius）的论文《动物运用理性甚于人类》（*Quod Animalia Bruta Ratione Utantur Melius Homine*），或他那篇《为鼠辩护》（*Pro Muribus*）；但愿你知道利普修斯（Lipsius）和伟大的莱布尼茨（Leibnitz）对兽类心智之力有何高见；但愿你还晓得渊博的拉比迈蒙尼德（Rabbi Maimonides）就它们的灵魂说过些什么——那样，你便不会凭着我的一点见识就把我当作妖魔，也不会拿身量的比例去度量灵性的官能了。我猜，到头来你大约还要落到那位西班牙医师戈麦斯·佩雷拉（Gomez Pereira）的高明见解上去：他在动物身上，除了纯粹的人造机器之外什么也找不出来——没有思想，也没有意志的自由，只是任意而动、自动而行。不过，不：我不把你看作这等荒唐，并且深信，凭着我这微贱之身，你早就学得比这明白了。再者，我也实在不懂，你所谓的奇迹究竟是什么，你又凭什么把我们存在的种种显现，分作奇迹的和自然的——这些显现其实就是我们自己，因为我们与它们本是互相成全、互为条件的。所以，切莫因为一件事还不曾落到你头上，或你自以为看不出它的因果关联，就对它大惊小怪；那只能证明你的目力——天生的也罢、病态的也罢——过于迟钝，有损你的识见。可是——佩雷格林先生，你别见怪——这桩事里最滑稽的却是：你竟想把自己劈成两半，一半认得、并且心甘情愿地相信那些所谓的奇迹；另一半呢，偏偏又对这认与信大惊小怪。你可曾想过：你对梦中的影像，也是信的么？”

“我！”佩雷格林喊道，“亲爱的朋友，你怎么竟谈起梦来？梦不过是咱们肉体或心智的构造出了某种错乱才生出来的东西罢了。”

这话音刚落，跳蚤大师便爆出一阵又清亮又刻薄的笑，随后对颇为狼狈的蒂斯先生说道：

===Original===
“My poor friend, is your understanding so little enlightened, that you do not see the folly of such opinions? Since the time that Chaos melted together into plastic matter﻿—it may be a tolerably long time ago﻿—the spirit of the universe has formed all shapes out of this existing material, and from this come also dreams and their images. These images are sketches of what has been, or probably of what is yet to be, which the soul rapidly puts together for its amusement when the tyrant, called body, has released it from its slavish servitude. But here is neither time nor place to refute you, and bring you to a better conviction; perhaps, too, it would be of no use whatever to you. One thing only I should like to explain.”

“Dear master,” cried Peregrine, “speak, or be silent, as you think proper; do what to you seems best, for I plainly perceive that however small you may be, you have deep knowledge and sound understanding. You compel from me unconditional confidence, although I do not quite comprehend your figurative modes of speech.”

“Learn then,” resumed Master Flea, “that you are very strangely implicated in the history of the Princess Gamaheh. Swammerdam and Leeuwenhoek, the Thistle, Zeherit, and the Leech-Prince, as well as the Genius, Thetel, are all striving after the princess, and even I myself must confess that, alas! my old passion is reviving, and I could be fool enough to share my sovereignty with the false fair one. But you﻿—you, Mr. Peregrine, are the principal person, and without your consent, Gamaheh can belong to no one. If you wish to understand the more particular connection of the whole, which I myself do not know, you must speak to Leeuwenhoek about it; he has found it out, and will certainly let out much, if you will take the pains, and know how to question him.”

===Chinese===
“我可怜的朋友，你的悟性竟蒙昧至此，竟看不出这类见解的愚妄么？自从混沌凝成可塑的物质——那怕是相当久远的事了——宇宙的灵便凭着这现成的材料造出了万般形相，梦和梦中的影像也是从这里来的。这些影像，是已往之事、多半也是将成之事的草样，是灵魂在那位唤作肉身的暴君松开它、放它暂离奴隶差事的时候，飞快拼缀出来聊以自娱的。只是这里既没有时间也没有地方，容我驳倒你、把你引上更好的信念；再说，那对你或许也全无用处。我只想说明一件事。”

“亲爱的大师，”佩雷格林喊道，“你愿讲便讲，愿默便默，全随你的心意；你觉得怎样好便怎样办吧。因为我分明看出，任凭你身量如何微小，你却有渊博的学识和健全的悟性。你从我这里强取去的，是无条件的信任，尽管你那些譬喻式的谈吐，我还不能尽数领会。”

“那么你且听着，”跳蚤大师接着讲，“你同加玛赫公主的这段历史，牵缠得实在离奇。斯瓦默丹和列文虎克、蓟草精泽赫里特、水蛭王子，还有精灵忒特尔，个个都在争夺这位公主；连我自己也须得招认，唉！我那旧日的情焰又燃起来了，我竟会痴愚到想同那位假美人分享我的王权。可是你——你，佩雷格林先生，才是那首要的人物；没有你的应允，加玛赫谁也归不了。你若想明白这里头更确切的关窍——这连我自己也不知道——就必得去问列文虎克；他已把底细探了出来，只要你肯费些心，又懂得怎样盘问他，他准会漏出许多来。”

===Original===
Master Flea was about to continue, when a man leapt from the bushes in boiling passion, and flew upon Peregrine.

“Ha!” cried George Pepusch, with frantic gestures﻿—for it was he, “Ha! faithless, treacherous friend! Have I found you? Found you in the fateful hour? Up then! pierce this breast, or fall by my hand.”

With this he drew a brace of pistols from his pocket, pressed one into Peregrine’s hand, and took his ground with the other, crying, “Shoot, coward! shoot!”

===Chinese===
跳蚤大师正待往下讲，忽有一条汉子从灌木丛中怒气冲天一跃而出，直扑佩雷格林。

“哈！”乔治·佩普施喊道，手势狂乱——原来正是他，“哈！无信无义的朋友！我可找着你了？在这决定命运的时辰找着了你？那就来吧！刺穿这胸膛，不然就死在我手上！”

说罢，他从衣袋里掏出一对手枪，把一支塞进佩雷格林手里，自己握着另一支站定脚步，喝道：“开枪啊，胆小鬼！开枪！”

===Original===
Peregrine placed himself, but declared that nothing should induce him to the incurable madness of entering into a duel with his only friend, without even a suspicion of the cause. At all events he would in no case be the first to begin a murderous attack.

At this Pepusch burst into a wild laugh, and in the same moment the ball went through Peregrine’s hat. The latter remained staring at his friend in profound silence, without picking up the hat, which had fallen to the ground, when Pepusch advanced a few steps towards him, and murmured in a hollow voice, “Shoot!” Peregrine fired his pistol in the air.

With the voice and gestures of a madman, Pepusch now flung himself upon his friend’s breast, and cried out, in heartrending tones, “She is dying! dying for you, unlucky one! Quick! Save her! You can do it﻿—save her for yourself, and let me perish in my despair!”

Pepusch ran off so fast that Peregrine had lost sight of him on the instant, and now a fearful foreboding came over him, that his friend’s mad behaviour must have been occasioned by something terrible which had happened to the little one, whereupon he hastened back to the city.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林站定了位，却声明：任凭什么也不能诱使他染上那不可救药的疯病——连缘由的影子都摸不着，就同他独一无二的朋友去决斗。无论如何，杀人的头一枪他断不肯开。

听了这话，佩普施爆出一阵狂笑，就在同一瞬，子弹穿透了佩雷格林的帽子。佩雷格林深深盯着他的朋友，一言不发，连落在地上的帽子也不去拾。佩普施朝他逼近几步，嗓音空洞地咕哝道：“开枪！”佩雷格林把手枪朝天放了一响。

佩普施随即疯人一般连喊带比划，扑到他朋友的胸口，声声凄绝地喊道：“她要死了！为你死的，你这背运的人！快！救救她！你办得到的——把她救下来留给你自己，让我在绝望里毁灭吧！”

佩普施跑得飞快，佩雷格林转眼就没了他的影儿。一阵可怖的预感当头罩下：他朋友这疯癫的行径，必定是小姑娘那边出了什么可怕的事才招出来的。于是他急匆匆赶回城里。

===Original===
On entering his house, he was met by the old woman, loudly lamenting that the poor princess was on the sudden taken violently ill, and was dying. Mr. Swammer himself had gone after the most celebrated physician in Frankfurt.

With the feelings of death at his heart, he crept into Mr. Swammer’s room that was opened to him by the old woman. There lay the little one upon a sofa, pale and stiff like a corpse, and it was not till he knelt down and bent over her that he perceived her gentle breathing. No sooner had he touched her icy hand, than a painful smile played about her lips, and she lisped:

“Is it you, my sweet friend? Have you come to see her once again, who loves you so unspeakably﻿—who dies, alas! because she cannot breathe without you?”

Dissolving in sorrow, Peregrine poured himself forth in protestations of the tenderest love, and repeated, that nothing in the world was so dear to him that he would not sacrifice it to her. Out of words grew kisses, but in these kisses again words, like the breathings of love, were distinguishable.

===Chinese===
一进家门，老太太便迎上来，高声哭诉那可怜的公主突然害了急病，眼看要不行了。斯瓦默先生亲自去请法兰克福最有名气的医生了。

他心头怀着一份死亡的滋味，蹑手蹑脚走进斯瓦默先生的房间——房门是老太太替他开的。小姑娘躺在一张沙发上，苍白僵直，像一具尸首；直到他跪下去俯身向她，才觉出她那轻微的呼吸。他刚碰了碰她那只冰冷的手，一丝痛苦的微笑便在她唇边漾开，她口齿轻轻地喃喃道：

“是你吗，我甜蜜的朋友？你又来看她了么——她是那样说不尽地爱你——唉，她要死了，因为没有你，她便喘不上气来！”

佩雷格林悲不自胜，滔滔倾吐出最温柔的爱的盟誓，再三说：世上再没有一样东西，他珍爱到不肯拿来为她牺牲的地步。言语生出了亲吻，亲吻里又生出言语，像爱情的呼吸一般，分辨得清清楚楚。

===Original===
“You know, my Peregrine, how much I love you. I can be yours, you, mine. I can recover on the spot﻿—you will see me bloom again in my youthful splendour, like a flower refreshed by the morning dew, and joyfully lifting up his drooping head.﻿ ﻿… But﻿—give me up the prisoner, my dear, beloved Peregrine, or else you will see me perish before your eyes, in unutterable death pangs. Peregrine﻿—I can no more﻿—it is all over!”

With this she sank back upon the cushions, from which she had half raised herself; her bosom heaved tumultuously up and down, as if, in the death-pangs; her lips grew bluer, and her eyes seemed to break.

In wild anguish Peregrine caught at his neckcloth, from which Master Flea now leapt, of his own accord, upon the white neck of the little one, exclaiming, in a tone of the deepest grief, “I am lost!”

===Chinese===
“你是知道的，我的佩雷格林，我多么爱你。我能做你的人，你，做我的人。我能当场好起来——你会眼看着我重新焕发青春的光彩，像一朵让朝露润过的花，欢欢喜喜抬起它低垂的头。……可是——把那囚徒交给我吧，亲爱的、心肝般的佩雷格林，不然你就要眼看着我死在你面前，受那说不出的临终苦痛。佩雷格林——我撑不住了——全完了！”

说完，她倒回她方才半撑起身的软垫上；胸口剧烈地起伏，仿佛正处在临终的苦痛里；嘴唇越来越青，两只眼睛像是要碎了。

佩雷格林在狂乱的苦痛中一把抓住自己的领巾，跳蚤大师竟自动从那上头一跃而下，跳上小姑娘雪白的脖颈，用最深沉悲恸的声调喊道：“我完啦！”

===Original===
Peregrine stretched out his hand to catch the Master, but suddenly it seemed as if some invisible power held back his arm, and far other thoughts ran through his head than those which till now had occupied it.

“How!” thought he, “Because you are a frail man, and influenced by a mad passion, will you therefore betray him, to whom you have promised your protection? Will you therefore plunge a free, harmless people into eternal slavery, and utterly ruin the friend whose thoughts and words agree? No, no, recollect yourself, Peregrine! Rather die than be a traitor!”

“Give﻿ ﻿… up﻿ ﻿… the prisoner﻿ ﻿… I am dying!” stammered the little one, with failing voice.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林伸出手去捉大师，可忽然间，仿佛有股无形的力量拽住了他的胳膊；流过他脑际的，也换成了全然另一路念头，同方才盘踞在那里的那些再不相同。

“什么！”他想道，“就因为你是个软弱的人，受着一场疯狂痴情的摆布，就要出卖那位你许下保护的人么？就要把一个自由无害的民族掷进万世的奴役，把那位言辞与心思如一的朋友彻底断送么？不，不，醒醒吧，佩雷格林！宁可死，也不做叛徒！”

“把……囚徒……交给我……我要死了！”小姑娘气若游丝，结结巴巴地说。

===Original===
“No!” cried Peregrine, while in despair he caught her in his arms, “No! never! But let me die with you!”

And now a fine, penetrating harmony was heard, as if little silver bells were struck. Dörtje, with fresh roses on her lips and cheeks, started up suddenly from the sofa, and, breaking into a convulsive laughter, skipped about the chamber. She seemed to have been bit by the tarantula.

Peregrine gazed in terror on the strange spectacle, and the same did the physician, who stood at the door quite petrified, keeping out Mr. Swammer, who had followed him.

===Chinese===
“不！”佩雷格林绝望地把她搂进怀里，喊道，“不！决不！那就让我同你一起死吧！”

就在这时，响起了一阵清越沁人的和音，仿佛敲响了一串小小的银铃。多尔特耶唇上颊上绽出鲜润的玫瑰色，霍然从沙发上跳起身，迸出一阵痉挛般的大笑，在房间里蹦来蹦去。她像是叫塔兰图拉毒蛛咬着了。

佩雷格林惊恐地凝望着这奇怪的一幕；僵立在门口、呆若木石的医生也是一般无二——他还把跟在身后的斯瓦默先生拦在外头，不放他进来。

## VI Sixth Adventure / 六 第六奇遇

===Original===
All the passersby stopped, stretched out their necks, and peeped through the window into the coffee room. With every moment the crowd grew greater, the pressure more violent, and the noise louder. All this was occasioned by two strangers, who﻿—besides that their form, their dress, their whole manner had something extraordinary about it, that was repulsive and ridiculous at the same time﻿—played off many wonderful tricks, such as had never been seen before. The one, an old man, of a dirty, disagreeable appearance, was dressed in a surtout of shining stuff. Sometimes he made himself thin and long, sometimes he would shrink himself up to a short fat fellow, winding about all the time like a worm. The other, with powdered hair, motly silk coat, underdress of the same, large silver buckles, and altogether resembling a *petit-maître* of the last half of the foregoing century, repeatedly flew up to the ceiling, and then gently let himself down again, while, with a cheerful voice, he trilled discordant songs in a language altogether unknown.

According to the host’s declaration, they had both come in﻿—one a short time after the other﻿—like orderly people, and had called for wine. Then they had gazed more and more keenly on each other, and entered into conversation, and although the language of it was unintelligible to all the guests, yet their tone and manner showed they were engaged in a dispute, which grew warmer and warmer. On a sudden they had taken their present form and began these mad tricks, which continually attracted more spectators.

“The man who flies up and down so admirably,” exclaimed one of the spectators, “is the clockmaker Degen, of Vienna﻿—he who invented the flying machine, with which he is constantly contriving to tumble down upon his nose.”

“No,” replied another, “that is not the clockmaker. I should rather fancy that it was the Little Tailor of Sachsenhausen, if I did not know that the poor thing was burnt.”
===Chinese===
过路的人全都停下了脚，伸长脖子，隔着窗子往咖啡室里张望。人一刻比一刻多，拥挤一刻比一刻凶，喧声一刻比一刻响。这一切都是两个陌生人招来的：这两位，且不说那身形、那衣着、那整个做派都透着一股既可憎又可笑的离奇劲儿，还耍出了许多从未有人见过的奇妙把戏。一个是老头儿，相貌邋遢，叫人看着不受用，穿一件料子发亮的长礼服。他一会儿把自己抻得又细又长，一会儿又缩成一个矮胖子，同时从头到尾像条虫子似的蜿蜒扭个不停。另一个头发上扑着粉，身穿花绸外套，衬着同料的内裳，鞋上缀着大大的银扣，整个儿像上个世纪后半叶的一位*petit-maître*（浮华公子），一遍又一遍地飞上天花板，再轻轻悠悠落下来，一面还用欢快的嗓音，拖着腔唱着不成调的歌子，唱的是一种全然无人能懂的语言。

据店主声明，他们两位进来的时候——一个比另一个只晚片刻——都还是规规矩矩的体面人，各自要了酒。随后两人彼此越盯越紧，攀谈起来；虽说他们交谈的话满座客人一句也听不懂，可那腔调、那做派，分明是吵起了一场争执，而且火气越蹿越高。冷不丁地，两人都变出了如今这副模样，耍起这些疯把戏，于是看热闹的人便越引越多。

“那个飞上飞下飞得这般漂亮的，”一个看客嚷道，“就是维也纳的钟表匠德根（the clockmaker Degen）——就是发明了那架飞行器、成天价琢磨着拿它往下栽、摔自己鼻子的那一位。”

“不对，”另一个答道，“那不是钟表匠。要不是我知道那可怜人已经烧死了，我倒宁可猜他是萨克森豪森的小裁缝（the Little Tailor of Sachsenhausen）。”

===Original===
I know not whether my readers are acquainted with the Little Tailor of Sachsenhausen? Here it is.

> It happened that a pious little tailor at Sachsenhausen was coming out of church one Sunday with his wife, in all his best attire. The air was raw, the little tailor had taken nothing over night but a soft boiled egg and a few pickled gerkins, and in the morning a cup of coffee. Moreover he had been singing most vehemently in the church, and hence he began to feel in a piteous plight, and to long for a dram. As he had worked hard through the week, and had been particularly kind to his better half, making her a very pretty gown out of the pieces cabbaged from his customers, she consented to his going into the apothecary’s and getting himself a dram, which he did accordingly. The awkward apprentice, who was alone in the shop, made a mistake, and took down a bottle which, instead of a dram, contained inflammable gas, wherewith balloons are filled. Of this the apprentice poured out a full glass, and the tailor, putting it at once to his mouth, swallowed off the gas as an agreeable reviver. It made him, however, feel very strangely﻿—as if he had got a pair of wings on his shoulders, or as if someone were playing at football with him, for he felt himself compelled to jump up and down in the shop, and with every moment the impetus increased.

> “Eh! Gemini! Gemini!” he cried, “What a nimble dancer I have grown!”

> The apothecary’s apprentice stood with his mouth gaping wide from pure wonder, when it chanced that someone opened the door so hastily, that the opposite window flew open also. A strong current of air poured in, caught up the little tailor, and away he sailed through the window, since when he has not been seen. But it happened some time after, that the people of Sachsenhausen observed in the air a fireball, which lighted the whole country with its brightness, and then, being extinguished, fell to earth. All were eager to know what had dropped, and ran to the place, but found nothing more than a little heap of ashes, but with this the tongue of a shoe-buckle, a little piece of yellow satin with flowers, and something black, which to look at was like the horn-top of a walking stick. All were in deep council how such things could fall down from heaven in a fireball, when the wife of the departed tailor came up, and, on seeing these things, wrung her hands, took on most piteously, and cried out, “Ah, woe! that is my husband’s buckle! Ah, woe! that is my husband’s Sunday waistcoat! Ah, woe! that is my husband’s cane-top!” A very learned man, however, has declared that the cane-top was no cane-top, but a meteoric ball, or an abortive globe.

> Thus was made known to the people of Sachsenhausen and to all the world that the poor little tailor﻿—to whom the apothecary’s apprentice had given inflammable gas instead of a dram﻿—was burnt in the air, and had fallen to earth, as a meteoric ball, or an abortive globe.
===Chinese===
我的读者们可知道萨克森豪森的小裁缝是什么来历？不知道也无妨，故事就在这里。

> 话说一个礼拜天，萨克森豪森一位虔敬的小裁缝穿戴着他全套最好的衣裳，同妻子一道从教堂里出来。天气阴冷，小裁缝头天晚上只吃了一枚溏心蛋和几条腌黄瓜，早上又只喝了一杯咖啡；加上他在教堂里扯着嗓子唱了半天的圣歌，于是渐渐觉得身子发虚，怪可怜见的，一心盼着一小杯烧酒提提神。他一星期辛辛苦苦做活，对自家那口子又格外恩爱——用从主顾活计里昧下来的碎布头，给她拼了件挺体面的袍子——她便应允他进药房去喝上一杯，他也当真去了。店里只有那个笨手笨脚的学徒，一着忙拿错了瓶子，倒出来的不是烧酒，而是灌气球用的可燃气体。学徒斟了满满一杯，小裁缝端起来一饮而尽，把这气当作提神的妙品咽了下去。可这东西叫他立时觉得异样万分——仿佛肩膀上生出了一对翅膀，又仿佛有谁拿他当皮球踢，因为他只觉得自己不由自主地在铺子里又蹦又跳，而且那股冲劲儿一刻大似一刻。

> “嗳唷！双子星哪！双子星哪！”他喊道，“瞧我变成多轻巧的一个舞手啦！”
