> 药房学徒惊得张大了嘴巴，呆呆站着。恰在此时，有人推门推得太急，把对面的窗子也给震开了。一股强劲的气流灌进来，卷起小裁缝，他就打窗口一路飞了出去，从此再没有人见过他。可是过了些时日，萨克森豪森的人们望见天空中出现一颗火球，火光照亮了四野，随后熄灭，坠落在地。人人都急着要知道掉下来的是什么，跑到那地方一看，却只找到一小堆灰烬，外加一只鞋扣的扣舌、一小片绣花黄缎子，还有一样黑乎乎的东西，看上去像手杖顶上的角质箍。大家正绞尽脑汁，琢磨这些东西怎么会随火球从天上掉下来，那位去了世的裁缝的妻子恰好走来，一见这些东西便绞着双手，哭得死去活来，喊道：“啊呀，苦哇！这是我男人的鞋扣！啊呀，苦哇！这是我男人的节日背心！啊呀，苦哇！这是我男人的杖头！”不过，后来有一位很有学问的人断言：那杖头并非杖头，而是一颗陨星，或者说一枚半途夭折的小天体。

> 就这样，萨克森豪森的百姓和全世界都知道了：那可怜的小裁缝——药房学徒错把可燃气体当烧酒给他的那一位——是在半空里烧掉的，然后作为一颗陨星、一枚半途夭折的小天体，落回了地面。

===Original===
The taverner was at length impatient that the odd guest did not cease making himself now larger now smaller, without paying him any attention, and held the flask of Burgundy, which he had ordered, close to his nose. The stranger caught fast hold of it immediately, and did not let go till he had drained the last drop; then he sank as if fainting into an armchair, and could scarcely move himself.

The guests observed with astonishment that he swelled more and more during the drinking, and now appeared quite thick and shapeless. The fly-work of the other seemed also to be at a stand; he was about to sit down, panting and breathless, but, perceiving how his adversary lay there, half dead, he flew suddenly upon him, and began to belabour him soundly with his fists. The host, however, pulled him off, and declared that he would turn him out of the house, if he did not keep quiet. If they both wished to show their juggler’s tricks, they were welcome to do so, but without quarrelling and fighting like blackguards.

The flying gentleman seemed to take it somewhat ill that the host should suppose he was a juggler. He protested that he was nothing less than a vagabond, who went about playing off legerdemain tricks; he had formerly been ballet master to a celebrated king, but now practised in private as an amateur, and was called﻿—as his functions required he should be﻿—Legénie. If, in his just indignation at the abominable fellow there, he had sprung somewhat higher than was fitting, that was his own business, and concerned no one else.

The host on his part opined, that all this did not justify any fisticuffs; to which the amateur replied that mine host did not know the malicious fellow, or he would willingly allow his back to be drubbed black and blue. He had formerly been a French customhouse officer, and now gained a livelihood by bloodletting, cupping, and shaving, and was called Monsieur Leech, a nuisance to everybody, by his awkwardness, stupidity, and gluttony. It was not enough that the scoundrel, wherever he met him, whisked away the wine from his very lips, as he had done just now, but he was plotting to carry off his bride, whom he intended to carry home from Frankfurt.
===Chinese===
店主终于沉不住气了：那古怪的客人只顾一会儿变大一会儿变小，全不把他放在眼里；他便把客人点的那瓶勃艮第酒（Burgundy）径直送到他鼻子跟前。那陌生人立刻一把抓牢，不到喝干最后一滴决不撒手；随后他像晕过去似的瘫进一把扶手椅，几乎动弹不得。

客人们惊讶地看着他越喝越胀，这会儿已经显得又粗又肥、没了形状。另一位的那套飞功似乎也歇了场；他正气喘吁吁、上气不接下气地正要坐下，一眼瞥见对手半死不活地瘫在那里，便冷不防朝他扑去，抡起拳头结结实实地一顿好揍。店主慌忙把他拖开，声言他若再不安分，就把他撵出店门。他们两位若要耍把戏，悉听尊便，只是不许像流氓恶棍似的又吵又打。

那位会飞的先生见店主把自己认作把戏匠，心里似乎颇为不快。他申明，自己绝非什么走江湖耍戏法的流浪汉；他从前在某位著名国王驾前当过芭蕾舞大师，如今不过私下里以业余身份操练操练，人称——这也合着他职分的要求——勒热尼（Legénie）。方才他出于正当的义愤，痛恨那边那个可憎的家伙，蹦得略高了些、有失体统，那是他自己的事，与旁人无干。

店主则以为，这一切再怎么说也构不成动拳头的道理。业余大师回说：店主大人不认得那个歹毒的家伙，若是认得，保管心甘情愿看着他被打得青一块紫一块。此人是法兰西海关出来的，如今靠放血、拔火罐、剃头营生，人称水蛭先生（Monsieur Leech）；此人笨拙、愚蠢、饕餮，凭这三样本事成了人人嫌弃的祸害。这恶棍走到哪儿，就在他嘴边把酒夺走，方才便是如此；这还不够，居然还设下圈套要拐走他的新娘——他原打算从法兰克福把新娘迎回家去的那一位。

===Original===
The Douanier had heard all that the Amateur advanced, and, glancing at him with his little malicious eyes, said to the host, “Don’t believe a syllable that the gallows-bird there is chattering. An admirable ballet master, truly! who with his elephant feet crushes the legs of the fair dancers, and with his pirouette knocks a tooth out of the manager’s jaw at the wing. And his verses, too! They have as awkward feet as himself, and tumble here and there like drunkards, treading the thoughts to pap. Because he flutters heavily in the air at times, like a drowsy gander, the conceited peacock fancies he is to have the fair one for his bride.”

At this the indignant Amateur cried out, “Thou Satan’s worm, thou shalt feel the gander’s beak,” and would have fallen upon the Douanier again, when the host seized him from behind, with strong arm, and, amidst the rejoicing of the assembled crowd, flung him out of the window.

No sooner was the Amateur gone than Monsieur Leech resumed the plain solid form in which he had entered. The people without took him for quite another person than the juggler, who had played such strange tricks, and quietly dispersed. The Douanier thanked mine host in the most obliging terms for his aid against the Amateur, and, to prove his gratitude, offered to shave him for nothing, and more pleasantly than ever he had been shaved in his life before. The host felt his beard, and it seeming to him at the moment as if the hairs were terribly long, he accepted Mr. Leech’s offer, who accordingly set about it, at first, with a light, dexterous hand, but on a sudden he cut his nose so shrewdly, that the blood streamed down. The host, deeming this to be nothing else than malice, seized the Douanier, who flew as nimbly out of the door as the Amateur through the window. Immediately after, there arose a loud tumult without, and scarcely allowing himself time to stop the bleeding of his nose with lint, he flew out to see what devil was raising this new uproar. There, to his no little astonishment, he saw a young man, who with one hand grasped the Amateur, and with the other the Douanier, and with rolling eyes exclaimed, “Ha! Satan’s brood! you shall not cross my way, you shall not rob me of Gamaheh!” while his prisoners intermixed their cries of, “A madman! Save﻿—save us, host﻿—he mistakes us﻿—he will murder us﻿—”
===Chinese===
税关官（Douanier）把业余大师的这一番话听了个全，拿他那双促狭的小眼睛瞟了他一眼，对店主说：“那边那只该上绞架的鸟儿吱吱喳喳，您一个字也别信。好一位了不起的芭蕾舞大师！他那双象蹄子，专会踩断美貌舞娘的腿；他来一个单足旋转，就能在侧台边上敲掉经理嘴里一颗牙。还有他写的那些诗！那一双诗脚同他自己的一样笨，东倒西歪像群醉汉，把思想踩成一片稀烂。只因他时不时像只犯困的呆鹅，在半空里呼哧呼哧扑腾几下，这只自负的孔雀便妄想把美人娶作新娘。”

听了这话，业余大师勃然大怒，喊道：“你这撒旦的虫子，叫你尝尝呆鹅的喙！”说着又要朝税关官扑上去；店主却从背后伸出一双铁臂把他擒住，在围观人群的一片欢腾声中，把他从窗子里扔了出去。

业余大师一去，水蛭先生便恢复了进店时那副敦敦实实的本来形貌。外头的人们只当他同方才大耍怪把戏的那位把戏匠全然是两个人，便安安静静散了。税关官拿最殷勤不过的话谢过店主帮他对付业余大师，为表感激，提出分文不取替他剃一回脸，还保证剃得比他这辈子哪一回都受用。店主摸了摸胡子，当下只觉胡茬仿佛长得吓人，便接受了水蛭先生的这番美意。水蛭先生于是动起手来，起初手上又轻又巧，冷不防却狠狠割了他鼻子一下，血登时淌了下来。店主认定这除了歹意再无别的，一把揪住税关官；那一位夺门而出的身法，同业余大师方才破窗而出一样麻利。紧跟着门外又起了一片大乱；店主顾不得拿棉绒按一按鼻血，便飞奔出去，要看是哪个魔鬼又掀起了这场新的喧闹。只见——叫他好不吃惊——一个年轻人，一手揪着业余大师，一手揪着税关官，眼珠骨碌碌地转，高声喊道：“哈！撒旦的孽种！你们休想挡我的道，休想把加玛赫从我手里夺走！”他手下的两个囚徒则夹声哀号：“疯子！救命——救救我们，店主——他认错人了——他要杀我们——”

===Original===
“Eh!” cried the host, “what are you about, my good Mr. Pepusch? Have you been offended by these strange people? Perhaps you are mistaken in them. This is the Ballet Master, Monsieur Legénie, and this the Douanier, Monsieur Leech.”

“Ballet Master Legénie! Douanier Leech!” repeated Pepusch, in a hollow voice.

He seemed as if waking out of a dream, and trying to recollect himself. In the meantime two honest citizens, of his acquaintance, came out of the inn, who joined in persuading him to be quiet, and let the fellows go about their business.

Again Pepusch exclaimed, “Ballet Master Legénie! Douanier Leech!” and let his arms drop powerless by his side. With the speed of wind, the released prisoners were off, and it seemed to many in the street as if the Amateur fled over the roofs of the neighbouring houses, and the barber was lost in the puddle that had collected itself between the stones before the door.
===Chinese===
“嗳！”店主喊道，“您这是唱的哪一出，我的好佩普施先生？这两位生客得罪您了吗？兴许您认错了人。这一位，是芭蕾舞大师勒热尼先生；那一位，是税关官水蛭先生。”

“芭蕾舞大师勒热尼！税关官水蛭！”佩普施声音空洞地跟着念了一遍。

他像是大梦方醒，正竭力收摄心神。这时，两位他相识的正派市民从客栈里走出来，也凑上来劝他息怒，放那两个家伙自去干他们的营生。

佩普施又喊了一遍：“芭蕾舞大师勒热尼！税关官水蛭！”两条胳膊便无力地垂在了身边。获释的两个囚徒一阵风似地去了；街上许多人恍惚看见，业余大师掠过左近几栋房子的屋顶逃去，而那位剃头匠则没进了门前石板缝间积起的一汪泥水里。
===Original===
The two citizens invited the distracted Pepusch to come in and drink a glass of old hock with them, an offer which he readily accepted, and seemed to enjoy the generous wine, though he sat silent and abstracted, and answered not a word to all that could be said to him. At last, however, his features brightened up, and he said, very kindly, “You did well, my friends, in hindering me from killing on the spot those wretches, who were in my power. But you know not what dangerous creatures lurk beneath their masks.”

Pepusch paused, and it may be easily supposed with what eagerness the citizens waited for what he had to discover. The host also had approached them, and all three poked their heads together, with their arms crossed upon the table, and held in their breath, that they might not lose a syllable from Pepusch’s mouth.

“See, my good people,” he continued solemnly, “see; he﻿—whom you call the Ballet Master, Legénie﻿—is none other than the evil, awkward genius, Thetel; the other, whom you take for the Douanier, Leech, is the hateful bloodsucker, the Leech-Prince. Both are in love with the Princess, Gamaheh, who as you know, is the daughter of the mighty king, Sekakis, and are here to make her false to the Thistle, Zeherit. This is the greatest folly that ever entered into a foolish brain, for besides the Thistle, Zeherit, there is but one person in the world to whom she can belong, and this person would perhaps vainly enter into the contest with Zeherit. For soon the Thistle will bloom at midnight in full splendour and strength, and in the death of love dawns the morning of a higher life. Now, I myself am the Thistle, Zeherit, and, therefore, my good friends, you cannot blame me if I am indignant with those traitors, and altogether take the whole affair much to heart.”

The three listeners opened their eyes wide, and stared, speechlessly, at Pepusch, with open mouths. They had tumbled out of the clouds, as people say, and their heads were humming with the fall. But Pepusch emptied a bumper, and, turning to the host, said, “Yes, yes, mine host; you will soon see that I shall bloom as the *Cactus grandiflorus*, and the whole country round will be impregnated with its perfume. You may believe me, friends.”
===Chinese===
两位市民请心绪散乱的佩普施进店去，同他们喝一杯陈年莱茵白酒；这番好意他欣然领受，看来也颇受用那醇厚的好酒，只是坐在那里一言不发、神思恍惚，任凭别人说什么，他一个字也不答。末了，他脸上渐渐开朗起来，十分和气地说：“朋友们，多亏你们拦住我，没叫我当场结果了那两个落在我手里的混账东西，你们做得好。只是你们不知道，他们那两张假面底下，潜伏着怎样危险的角色。”

佩普施顿住了。不难想见，两位市民是何等急切地等着他抖出底来。店主也凑了过来；三颗脑袋挤作一堆，胳膊交叠在桌上，屏住呼吸，生怕漏掉佩普施嘴里吐出的一个字。

“看吧，好人们，”他郑重其事地接着说，“看吧：那一位——你们唤作芭蕾舞大师勒热尼的——不是别人，正是那个凶恶而又蠢笨的精灵忒特尔；另一位，你们当作税关官水蛭的，是那个可恨的吸血鬼水蛭王子。两个都恋着加玛赫公主——你们知道，她便是威严的塞卡基斯王的女儿——跑到这里来，是要教她背弃蓟草精泽赫里特。这是钻进愚蠢脑袋里的天字第一号的蠢念头，因为除了蓟草精泽赫里特，普天之下她只能属于一个人，而这个人同泽赫里特较量起来，恐怕也是白费气力。因为不久，蓟草就要在午夜时分，以全部的光彩和伟力盛放，而在爱的死亡之中，一个更高生命的黎明正破晓而来。而我，我自己就是蓟草精泽赫里特。所以，好朋友们，我若对那两个奸贼义愤填膺，把这桩事整个儿放在心上，你们总怪不得我。”

三位听客睁大了眼睛，张着嘴，哑口无言地瞪着佩普施。他们是像俗话说的那样从云端里栽了下来，摔得脑袋里嗡嗡直响。佩普施却把满满一大杯酒一饮而尽，转向店主说：“是啊，是啊，店主；你很快就会看见，我将像*Cactus grandiflorus*一样开花，四乡八野都要浸透它的芬芳。信我的话吧，朋友们。”

===Original===
The host could utter nothing but an exclamation of stupid surprise, “Eh! that would be the deuce!” The two citizens exchanged mysterious glances, and one, taking George’s hand, said with a doubtful smile, “You seem to be somewhat disquieted, my good Mr. Pepusch; how, if you were to take a glass of water, and﻿—”

“Not a drop!” exclaimed Pepusch, interrupting the well-meant counsel, “Not a drop! Has water ever been poured upon boiling oil without increasing the fury of the flames? I am disquieted, you say? In truth that may well be the case; how the devil can I be otherwise, after having exchanged shots with my bosom friend, and then sending a bullet through my own brain? Here, into your hands I deliver up the murderous weapons, now that all is over.”

Pepusch drew a brace of pistols from his pocket, whereat the host started back; the citizens snatched at them, but no sooner had they fairly hold of them, than they burst out into immoderate laughter. The pistols were of wood, a plaything from the Christmas fair.

Pepusch seemed to pay no attention to what was going on about him; he sat in deep thought, and continually cried out, “If I could but find him! if I could but find him!”
===Chinese===
店主只憋出一句傻乎乎的惊叹：“嗳！那可真见了鬼了！”两位市民交换了一个意味深长的眼色，其中一位握住乔治的手，挂着将信将疑的微笑说：“您似乎有些心神不宁，我的好佩普施先生；要不，喝上一杯清水，然后——”

“一滴也不喝！”佩普施打断这番好意的规劝，喊道，“一滴也不喝！滚油上浇过水，几时不曾把火烧得更旺？你说我心神不宁？说实话，这倒很可能；同我的知心朋友交过了火，又亲手把一颗子弹送进自己的脑袋之后，鬼知道我还能怎么安下心来？来，既然一切都完了，我把这凶器交到你们手里。”

佩普施从口袋里掏出一对手枪，店主吓得倒退了一步；两位市民伸手去抢，可刚一到手，便迸发出毫无节制的狂笑。那手枪是木头做的，是圣诞集市上买来的玩意儿。

佩普施似乎全不理会身边正发生什么；他坐在那里出神，嘴里不住地喊：“只要我能找到他！只要我能找到他！”

===Original===
The host took courage, and modestly asked, “Whom do you mean, my good Mr. Pepusch? Whom can you not find?”

“Know you,” said Pepusch solemnly, and fixing the host with a keen gaze, “Know you anyone to be compared, in might and wondrous power, with the king Sekakis; then name his name and I will kiss your feet. But for the rest, I would ask you if you know anyone who is acquainted with Mr. Peregrine Tyss, and can tell me where I may meet him at this present moment?”

To this the host replied, smirking amiably, “Here I can serve you, respected Mr. Pepusch, and inform you that he was with me an hour ago, taking a glass of wine. He was very thoughtful, and when I asked ‘What news on ’Change? he suddenly cried out, ‘Yes, sweet Gamaheh! I have renounced you! Be happy in my George’s arms!’ Upon this a thin curious voice said, ‘Let us now go to Leeuwenhoek’s, and peep into the horoscope.’ Immediately Mr. Tyss emptied his glass, and they went away together﻿—that is, Mr. Tyss and the voice without a body. Probably they have gone to Leeuwenhoek’s, who is lamenting that his well-disciplined fleas have, one and all, deserted him.”

The words were scarcely out of the host’s mouth than George started up in a fury, and, seizing him by the throat, cried out, “Scoundrel, what do you say? Renounced? renounced her Gamaheh! Peregrine! Sekakis!”
===Chinese===
店主壮起胆子，小心翼翼地问：“您说的是谁呀，我的好佩普施先生？您找不着的是谁呢？”

“你可知道，”佩普施神情郑重，目光灼灼地盯住店主，“你可知道有什么人，在威权和神通上比得上塞卡基斯王？你说出一个来，我就吻你的脚。至于别的，我倒要请问：你认不认得什么人熟悉佩雷格林·蒂斯先生，能告诉我此时此刻我上哪儿能找到他？”

店主听了，和气地抿嘴一笑，答道：“这个忙我倒帮得上，尊敬的佩普施先生。可以奉告的是：一个钟头以前，他还在我这里，喝了一杯酒。他一副心事重重的样子；我问‘交易所里有什么新闻？’他忽然喊道：‘是的，甜蜜的加玛赫！我放弃你了！在我乔治的怀抱里幸福地过吧！’这时一个细细的、怪里怪气的声音说：‘我们这就到列文虎克家去，窥一窥天宫图吧。’蒂斯先生当即干了那杯酒，两人便一道走了——也就是说，蒂斯先生，外加那个没有身体的声音。多半是到列文虎克家去了；那一位正伤心呢：他那些训练有素的跳蚤，一只不剩，全都跑了。”

话音未落，乔治已勃然跃起，一把扼住店主的喉咙，喝道：“恶棍，你说什么？放弃了？他放弃了加玛赫！佩雷格林！塞卡基斯！”

===Original===
The host’s story, however, was perfectly correct. He had heard Master Flea, who was summoning Peregrine, in his fine silver tones, to go to the microscopist, Leeuwenhoek, for what purpose the reader knows already: Peregrine had really gone thither, and was received by Leeuwenhoek with that soft odious friendliness﻿—and that humility of compliment﻿—which announce the burdensome and reluctant recognition of superiority. But, as Mr. Tyss had the microscopic glass in the pupil of his eye, all this complimenting and subservience availed Antonie van Leeuwenhoek nothing in the world; on the contrary, Peregrine only the more discovered the hatred which filled the heart of the microscopist. While he protested how much he felt honoured and rejoiced by Mr. Tyss’s visit, the thoughts ran thus: “I wish that the devil had plunged you ten thousand fathoms deep in the abyss! But I must feign friendship and submission towards you, as the cursed constellation has placed me under your dominion, and my whole being in some sort depends upon you. But perhaps I may be able to outwit you, for, in spite of your high descent, you are a simple fool. You fancy that Dörtje Elverdink loves you, and will perhaps marry her. Only come to me about it and you fall into my hands, in spite of the power that dwells within you without your knowing it, and I will employ everything to ruin you, and gain possession of Dörtje and Master Flea.”
===Chinese===
然而店主的话分毫不差。他听见的是跳蚤大师，正用他那银铃般清亮的嗓音催促佩雷格林到显微镜学家列文虎克家去——为的什么，读者早已知道了。佩雷格林也果真去了；列文虎克接待他的，是那种软腻腻、令人齿冷的亲热，外加那种谦恭备至的客套——这副做派，泄露的正是一种不胜负担、却又无可奈何的对优势者的承认。可是蒂斯先生瞳孔里既嵌着那片显微镜片，这一整套的恭维与奉承，对安东尼·范·列文虎克便没有半点用处；恰恰相反，佩雷格林反倒越发看清了那填满显微镜学家心胸的仇恨。他口口声声说，蒂斯先生的造访使他如何倍感荣宠、如何欢欣，心里转的念头却是：“但愿魔鬼把你扔进深渊，一沉就是万丈！可我不得不对你装出友谊和顺从，因为那可诅咒的星象把我置于你的辖制之下，我的整个存在，多少都系在你身上。不过，兴许我还能骗过你，因为尽管你出身高贵，却是个头脑简单的傻瓜。你自以为多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克爱你，说不定还要娶她。只消你为这事来找我，你就落进我的手心了——哪怕你身怀着自己浑然不知的神力；我要调动一切手段毁掉你，把多尔特耶和跳蚤大师统统抢到手。”

===Original===
Peregrine naturally regulated his conduct by these thoughts, and took good care not to say a syllable about Dörtje Elverdink, and pretended that he came to see Leeuwenhoek’s collection of natural rarities.

While now Leeuwenhoek opened the great drawers, Master Flea whispered very gently in Peregine’s ear, that his (Peregrine’s) horoscope was lying on the table by the window. Here he saw all manner of lines, that mysteriously crossed each other, and many other wonderful signs, but as he was entirely deficient in astronomical knowledge, all remained confused and dark to him, look as keenly as he would. Yet it seemed strange to him, that, in the bright red point, in the middle of the table on which the horoscope was drawn, he plainly recognised himself. The longer he looked at this point, the more it gained the shape of a heart, and the more brightly it reddened. Still it only sparkled as through a web, with which it was overspread.

Peregrine plainly saw that Leeuwenhoek wanted to draw off his attention from the horoscope, and as he ran no risk of being deceived, very rationally resolved to question his friendly enemy at once, and without any circumlocution, as to the meaning of the mysterious table. Leeuwenhoek assured him, with a malicious smile, that nothing would give him greater pleasure than the explaining to his respected friend the signs upon the table, which he himself had drawn, according to his slight knowledge in such matters.

The thoughts ran thus: “Hoho! are you after that, my wise sir? In truth Master Flea has not advised you ill. I myself am to explain the table, and help you to the understanding of the magic might that dwells in your worthy person! I might invent some lies for you, but of what use would it be? For if I were to tell you the truth, you would not understand a syllable, but would remain stupid as ever? From pure convenience, therefore, and not to put myself to the trouble of invention, I will tell you so much of the signs of the table as seems good to me.”
===Chinese===
佩雷格林自然照着这些念头行事，格外小心，对多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克只字不提，只推说自己是来观赏列文虎克那批博物奇珍收藏的。

趁列文虎克开那几口大抽屉的当儿，跳蚤大师在佩雷格林耳边悄悄说，他——佩雷格林的——天宫图就摊在窗前那张桌上。他在图上看见种种神秘交错、玄不可解的线条，还有许多别的奇妙符号；可他对天文之学全然无知，任凭怎样瞪大眼睛，眼前只是一团混沌的黑暗。只有一桩事他觉得古怪：在绘着天宫图的桌子正中那个鲜红的点上，他清清楚楚认出了自己。他盯着这个点看得越久，它越成了一颗心的形状，红色也越发明亮。只是它始终隔着一张罩在它上面的罗网，在那后头闪闪发光。

佩雷格林看得分明：列文虎克是想把他的注意力从天宫图上引开。他既没有受骗之虞，便十分在理地拿定主意，立刻直截了当地向这位友善的敌人问个明白：这张神秘的图究竟作何解。列文虎克挂着恶意的微笑向他担保说，为他尊敬的朋友解说图上的符号——那是他凭自己在这些学问上的一点浅薄知识亲手绘下的——再没有比这更叫他快意的事了。

心里转的念头却是：“嗬嗬！你要打听的原来是这个，我的聪明先生？说真的，跳蚤大师给你出的主意不坏。倒要我自己来解这张图，帮你弄明白你那可敬的躯壳里住着怎样的魔法伟力！我本可以编几句谎话哄你，可那有什么用处？因为就算我把真话告诉你，你也是一个字听不懂，照样蠢得没法子？所以呀，纯粹图个省事，也省得费神编造，这张图上的符号，我觉得讲多少合适，便讲多少。”
===Original===
Peregrine knew now that if he were not to learn all, at least he would not be deceived with falsehoods.

Leeuwenhoek placed the tablet on something like an easel, which he brought forward from a corner of the room, and both seating themselves before it, considered it for a time in silence. At length Leeuwenhoek began with much solemnity:

“You, perhaps, do not suspect that those lines, those characters on the table, which you are so attentively considering, are your own horoscope, drawn by myself, with mysterious astrologic art, under the favourable influence of the stars. How came you to such a presumptuous idea? what could make you wish to unravel the web of my fate, to read my destiny? so might you ask, my friend, and with perfect justice, if I were not able to show you my inward call thereto. I know not whether you have heard of the celebrated rabbi Isaac Ben Harravad. Among other profound knowledge he had the strange gift of reading by men’s faces whether the soul had previously inhabited another body, or whether it was to be considered quite fresh and new. I was yet very young when the rabbi died of an indigestion, brought on by eating of a dish highly seasoned with garlic. The Jews ran away with the body so quickly that the deceased had not time to collect and carry off all his knowledge, which the illness had scattered. Laughing heirs divided the property, but I had fished off that wonderful seer-gift, in the very moment that the Angel of Death had set his sword upon the rabbi’s breast. In this way the wonderful faculty has come to me, and I, like the rabbi Isaac Ben Harravad, can read in the faces of men, whether the soul has before occupied another body or not. Your brow, Mr. Tyss, when I saw it the first time, excited the strangest thoughts and doubts. I was certain of the previous existence of your soul long ago, and yet the form, prior to your present life, remained a perfect mystery. I was forced to have recourse to the stars, and draw your horoscope, to solve the difficulty.”
===Chinese===
佩雷格林这下明白了：他纵然不能尽知底细，至少不会受谎话的欺哄。

列文虎克把图板架上一样类似画架的东西——那是他从房间角落里搬出来的——两人便挨着图前坐下，默默端详了半晌。末了，列文虎克无比郑重地开了口：

“你如此专心端详的图上那些线条、那些符号，恐怕你万万想不到，它们正是你本人的天宫图——是我凭着神秘的占星之术、在诸星吉照之下亲手绘制的。你怎么会起这般狂妄的念头？是什么叫你想来拆解我命途的丝网、来占读我的运数？我的朋友，你尽可以这样问我，而且全然在理——倘若我拿不出我内心受召的凭据来的话。我不知道你可曾听说过那位大名鼎鼎的拉比伊萨克·本·哈拉瓦德（Isaac Ben Harravad）。他在种种渊深学问之外，还有一样奇异的禀赋：只消看人的脸，便看得出那灵魂从前可曾寄居过另一副躯壳，还是得算作全新铸就的。拉比死于一场消化不良——因贪食一盘重蒜的菜肴而起——那时我还很年轻。犹太人们抢走尸体抢得太快，死者竟来不及把他那些被疾病打散的学问一一收拢带走。捡了便宜的继承人们瓜分了遗产，可就在死亡天使把剑抵上拉比胸口的那一刻，我把那奇妙的先知禀赋钓到了手。就这样，这桩奇能落到了我身上；我同那位拉比伊萨克·本·哈拉瓦德一样，也能从世人的脸上读出，那灵魂从前可曾占用过另一副躯壳。蒂斯先生，你的额头，我头一回见到时，便激起了最古怪的念头与疑窦。你的灵魂前生有自，这一点我早就确信无疑；只是你此生之前的那副形骸，始终是个彻头彻尾的谜。我不得不求助于星辰，绘出你的天宫图，来解这道难题。”

===Original===
“Well!” exclaimed Peregrine, “And have you discovered anything, Mr. Leeuwenhoek?”

“Certainly!” replied Leeuwenhoek, assuming a still more solemn tone, “Certainly! I have discovered that the physical principle﻿—which now animates the agreeable body of my very worthy friend, Mr. Peregrine Tyss﻿—existed long ago, although only as a thought or consciousness of a shape. Look here; consider attentively the red point in the centre of the table. That is not only yourself, but the point is the form, of which your physical principle once could not be conscious. As a sparkling carbuncle, you then lay in a deep mine of the earth, but stretched over you, on the green surface of the ground, slept the beautiful Gamaheh, and her form also passed away in unconsciousness. Strange lines and foreign constellations cross your life from the point of time when the thought first put on a form, and became Mr. Peregrine Tyss. You are in possession of a talisman without knowing it, and this talisman is that very red carbuncle. It may be that King Sekakis wore it as a precious jewel in his crown, or perhaps﻿—in some measure﻿—was the carbuncle itself. Enough﻿—you possess it now, but a certain event must take place if its slumbering power is to be awakened, and with this waking of the power of your talisman will be decided the fate of an unhappy creature, who hitherto has led a shadowy life between fear and changing hope. Alas! it was only a shadowy life that the sweet Gamaheh could gain by the profoundest magic, as the operative talisman was stolen from us. You alone have killed her, you alone can breathe fresh life into her, when the carbuncle glows again in your breast.”
===Chinese===
“那么！”佩雷格林喊道，“你可发现了什么，列文虎克先生？”

“当然！”列文虎克答道，摆出一副愈发郑重的腔调，“当然！我发现：那个如今赋予我可敬的朋友佩雷格林·蒂斯先生这具可爱躯壳以生机的本原，早就存在了——尽管当初只是一缕思绪，或者说，对一个形状的混沌意识。看这里；仔细看桌子正中的那个红点。那不仅仅是你本人；那一点，正是你的本原当初无从自觉的那个形状。那时你是一颗熠熠生辉的贵榴石（carbuncle），躺在大地深处的一座矿里；而在你上方，铺展在绿茵茵的地表上，睡着美丽的加玛赫，她的形骸也一同消散在无意识里。自那缕思绪初次披上形骸、化身为佩雷格林·蒂斯先生的时辰起，奇特的线条与异邦的星座便纵横穿过了你的一生。你身怀一件护符而不自知，这护符正是那颗红贵榴石。塞卡基斯王或许曾把它当作稀世的珠宝镶在王冠上；又或者——在某种程度上——他自己便是那颗贵榴石。够了——如今它在你手里；只是它沉睡的伟力若要唤醒，必得先发生一桩特定的事变；而随着你护符伟力的苏醒，一个不幸生灵的命运也将就此判定——她至今还在恐惧与变幻不定的希望之间，过着影子一般的日子。唉！自从那件灵验的护符从我们手里被偷走，甜蜜的加玛赫纵使施出最渊深的魔法，也只换来这样一段影子似的生活。唯独是你杀死了她，也唯独是你，能在贵榴石重新在你胸中燃亮之时，把新鲜的生命吹还给她。”

===Original===
“And can you,” interrupted Peregrine, “can you explain what that event is which is to awake the power of the talisman?”

The microscopist stared with open eyes at Peregrine, like a person who is suddenly surprised into confusion, and who does not know what to say. The thoughts ran thus: “If I had but held my tongue about the talisman which the unlucky rascal carries within him, and which gives him so much power over us that we must all dance to his pipe! And now I am to tell him the event on which depends the awaking the strength of his talisman! Shall I confess to him that I don’t know myself, that all my art fails to loosen the knot in which the lines meet? Nay, that when I consider the planetary centre of the horoscope, I feel most piteously, and my own learned head seems to me no better than a painted block for periwigs? Far from me be any such confession that would lower me, and put arms into his hands against myself. I will fasten something upon the idiot who fancies himself so wise﻿—something that shall make his blood run cold, and take from him all farther inclination of teasing me.”

“My dearest sir,” said the Flea-tamer, putting on a very important face, “My dearest Mr. Tyss, don’t ask me to speak of this event. You know that the horoscope does indeed plainly and perfectly instruct us as to the existence of certain circumstances, but﻿—such is the wisdom of Eternal Might﻿—the event of threatening dangers always remains dark and doubtful. I esteem you too highly as an excellent kindhearted man to put you into disquiet and anxiety before the time, otherwise I should at least tell you so much: that the event which is to give you the consciousness of power, would in the same moment destroy your present form of being with the most horrible agonies of hell. But no! on that too I will be silent, and now not another word of the horoscope. Do not however fret yourself, although the affair looks bad enough, and I, with all my knowledge, can hardly see any chance of a favourable issue to the adventure. Perhaps you may be saved from this peril by some unexpected constellation which is now beyond the reach of observation.”
===Chinese===
“那么你能不能，”佩雷格林打断他，“你能不能讲个明白，那桩能唤醒护符伟力的事变，究竟是什么？”

显微镜学家睁大眼睛瞪着佩雷格林，活像一个人冷不防被逼进了窘境、张口结舌说不出话来。他心里转的念头是：“我要是压根没提那件护符就好了——那倒霉坯子贴身带着的东西，给了他这样的大权，害得我们全得跟着他的笛子跳舞！如今倒好，还要我去告诉他那桩决定他护符伟力苏醒的事变！难道要我向他招认，连我自己也不知道，我这一身本事全解不开线条交汇的那个结？更别说，每当我细察天宫图上的行星中心，我便惨然自伤，觉得自己这颗饱学的脑袋，同一块漆来戴假发的木头疙瘩也差不了多少？这种自贬身价、亲手递刀把子给他的招认，万万使不得。我要往那个自以为聪明的白痴身上安点什么——安上一样叫他血液冰凉的东西，叫他从此再提不起半点撩逗我的兴致。”

“我最亲爱的先生，”驯蚤人摆出一副非同小可的面孔说道，“我最亲爱的蒂斯先生，别逼我讲这桩事变。您知道，天宫图固然能明明白白、十全十美地昭示我们种种情势的存在，可是——永恒大能的智慧如此安排——凶险临头的那个事变，却总是幽暗难明。我把您看作一位顶好不过的仁厚君子，敬重还敬重不过来，断断不肯让您提前担惊受怕；不然的话，我至少也要告诉您这么多：那桩将赋予您权力之自觉的变故，会在同一瞬间，以地狱最可怕的痛楚，毁掉您如今的存在形式。可是不！这一点我也缄口不言，天宫图的事，从现在起一个字也不再多讲。只是您也别自寻烦恼，尽管事情看上去够糟，而我凭我全部的学问，也实在看不出这桩奇遇有什么善终的希望。兴许，会有一颗眼下还观测不到的、出乎意料的星，把您从这场危难里搭救出来，也未可知。”

===Original===
Peregrine was astonished at this deceit, yet still the whole state of the thing, the peculiar situation in which Leeuwenhoek stood without suspecting it, appeared to him so exceedingly pleasant, that he could not help breaking out into a loud fit of laughter. The microscopist, somewhat surprised at this, asked, “What are you laughing at so vehemently, my dear Mr. Tyss?”

“You do wisely,” replied Peregrine, still laughing, “You do very wisely in keeping secret, out of pure kindness, this threatening event, for besides that you are too much my friend to put me into fear and terror, you have yet another excellent reason for your silence, which is nothing else than that you do not know a syllable about the matter. In vain was all your labour to unriddle that knot: your whole astrology goes but to little, and if Master Flea had not fallen upon your nose, all your arts would have helped you little.”

Leeuwenhoek’s brow was red with rage; he clenched his fist, gnashed his teeth, and trembled so violently with agitation, that he would have tumbled from his seat, if Peregrine had not held him as firmly by the arm as George Pepusch grasped the unlucky taverner by the throat, who at length succeeded in saving himself by a dexterous side-spring. Hereupon George rushed out and entered Leeuwenhoek’s room just as Peregrine was holding him fast upon his seat, while he muttered furiously between his teeth, “Cursed Swammerdam! is it *you* that have done this?”
===Chinese===
佩雷格林对这套骗术暗自惊讶；然而事情的这个整个局面——列文虎克浑然不觉、身临其中的那个绝妙境地——在他看来实在有趣得很，忍不住放声大笑起来。显微镜学家颇为诧异，问道：“亲爱的蒂斯先生，您笑得这样起劲，是笑什么呀？”

“你做得聪明，”佩雷格林依旧笑着答道，“你做得太聪明了，出于一片纯粹的仁心，把那桩骇人的事变秘而不宣；因为除了你我交情深重、不忍叫我恐惧惊惶之外，你这份沉默还有一个绝妙的理由——那就是：这件事你本来就连一个字也不知道。解那个结，你是白费了气力：你那一整套占星术不过尔尔；倘若不是跳蚤大师偏偏落在你鼻子上，你那一身神通也帮不了你多少忙。”

列文虎克的额头气得通红；他攥紧拳头，咬牙切齿，激愤得浑身乱颤，眼看就要从座位上栽下去，幸亏佩雷格林牢牢抓住了他的胳膊——抓得同乔治·佩普施扼住那倒霉店主喉咙的手一样紧；那店主到底凭一个灵巧的侧跃脱了身。于是乔治冲出门去，闯进列文虎克的房间，正赶上佩雷格林把他死死按在座位上，只听他从牙缝里恶狠狠地挤出一句：“可诅咒的斯瓦默丹！干出这事的，难道是*你*？”

===Original===
No sooner did Peregrine perceive his friend than he let go of the microscopist, and going up to him, asked anxiously if that strange frenzy were over which had so dangerously possessed him. Pepusch seemed softened almost to tears, and protested that he had not in all his life committed so many follies as in the course of that one day. Amongst these not the least was that after he had sent a ball through his head in the forest, he had gone into a tavern﻿—where he did not know﻿—had talked to people of strange things, and murderously set upon the host, because from his broken speech he gathered that which was the very happiest thing that could befall him. All his paroxysms would now soon have reached the highest pitch, for the bystanders had taken his words for insanity, and he had to fear: instead of reaping the fruit of the happiest event, that he would be confined in a madhouse. With this he explained what the host had let drop concerning Peregrine’s conduct and declarations, and asked, with downcast eyes, whether such an act of self-denial, in favour of an unhappy friend, was probable, or even possible, in the present day, when heroism had vanished from the earth.
===Chinese===
佩雷格林一见朋友，立刻松开显微镜学家，迎上前去，焦灼地问他，那阵凶险地缠住了他的离奇疯癫，可曾过去了。佩普施几乎软下泪来，声言他有生以来所干的荒唐事，加在一起也没有这一天里干的多。这些荒唐里顶要紧的一桩是：他在树林里把一颗子弹送进了自己的脑袋，随后居然走进一家客栈——哪一家，他自己也不知道——对着人们讲起种种怪事，还凶神恶煞地扑向店主，只因从店主断断续续的话音里，他听出了那件对他而言天大的喜事。他的狂态眼看就要登峰造极，因为旁人已把他的话当成了疯话，他不能不担心：非但收不得这桩天大喜事的果实，反倒要被关进疯人院去。说到这里，他把店主漏出的、关于佩雷格林言行的话解说了一遍，垂着眼睛问道：为了成全一个不幸的朋友而作出的这等克己，在英雄主义已经从世上绝迹的今天，还像回事吗？甚至还可能吗？
===Original===
At these declarations from his companion Peregrine revived in his inmost heart. He protested with warmth that for his part he was far removed from doing anything that might in the least annoy his tried friend; that he solemnly renounced all pretensions to the heart and hand of the fair Dörtje Elverdink, and willingly gave up a paradise, though it had indeed opened upon him most seductively.

“And it was you,” said Pepusch, rushing into his friend’s arms, “It was you that I would have murdered, and because I did not believe you, I therefore shot myself. Oh, the madness of a mind ill at ease!”

“I pray you,” said Peregrine, “I pray you come to your senses. You speak of having shot yourself, and yet stand fresh and sound before me. How do these things agree?”

“You are right,” replied Pepusch, “it seems as if I could not speak to you so rationally as I really do, if I had actually sent a ball through my brain. The people, too, maintain that my pistols were not particularly dangerous﻿—nor, indeed, of iron, but of wood﻿—in fact mere toys; and so neither the duel nor the suicide could have been anything more than a pleasant mockery. We must have changed our parts, and I have begun to mystify myself and play the child, at the moment you have left the world of dream to enter into real life. But be this as it may; it is requisite that I should be certain of your generosity and my fortune, and then the clouds will dissipate which trouble my sight, or perhaps deceive me with the illusions of the *Fata Morgana*. Come, my Peregrine, accompany me to the fair Dörtje Elverdink.”
===Chinese===
听了同伴这些话，佩雷格林的内心深处重新活了过来。他热烈地声言：就他而言，任何可能让这位久经考验的朋友有半点不快的事，他都是远远不会去做的；他郑重放弃对美丽的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克的心与手的全部权利要求，心甘情愿交出一座天堂——尽管那座天堂确实曾向他敞开过，敞开得那样媚惑人心。

“原来是你，”佩普施说着，扑进朋友的怀抱，“我险些谋杀的就是你；正因为我不信你，我才朝自己开了枪。哦，一颗不得安宁的心，疯起来竟到这般地步！”

“我求你，”佩雷格林说，“我求你清醒过来。你说你朝自己开了枪，可你分明好端端、结结实实地站在我面前。这些话怎么对得上榫呢？”

“你说得对，”佩普施答道，“倘若我真的把子弹送进了脑子，想来我也就不能像现在这样，同你说话说得头头是道了。人们也都一口咬定，我那对手枪没有什么大危险——其实也不是铁的，是木头的——说白了，就是玩具；这么着，那场决斗也好，那场自杀也好，充其量不过是一场快活的滑稽戏。想必是我们俩掉换了角色：你离开梦境、踏进真实人生的那一刻，我却开始把自己装进迷魂阵，扮起小孩子来了。不过，是也罢不是也罢；我必须确知你的高义和我的福气，那时，遮蔽我目光的云翳自会消散——不然，它们没准还要拿*Fata Morgana*（海市蜃楼）的幻景来哄骗我呢。来吧，我的佩雷格林，陪我到美丽的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克那里去。”

===Original===
Pepusch took his friend’s arm, and was hastening off with him, but their intended walk was spared, for the door opened, and in tripped Dörtje Elverdink, lovely as an angel, and behind her the old Swammer. Leeuwenhoek, who had so long remained dumb, casting angry looks first at Pepusch and then at Peregrine, seemed, upon seeing the old Swammerdam, as if struck by an electric shock. He stretched his clenched hands towards him, and cried out in a voice hoarse with rage, “Ha! do you come to mock me, you old deceitful monster? But you shall not succeed. Defend yourself: your last hour has struck.”

Swammerdam started a few steps back, and as Leeuwenhoek was ready to fall upon him with his telescope, drew the like arms for his defence. The duel, which had begun at Peregrine’s, seemed about to be renewed. George Pepusch threw himself between the combatants, and while with his left hand he beat down a murderous glance of Leeuwenhoek’s, which would have stretched his adversary to the earth, with the left he turned aside the weapon of Swammerdam, so that he could not injure Leeuwenhoek. He then declared that he would not allow of any battle between them, till he thoroughly knew the cause of their dissension. Peregrine found this protest so reasonable, that he did not hesitate to throw himself between the champions with a similar declaration. To this the combatants were forced to yield. Swammerdam, moreover, asserted that he had not at all come with hostile intentions, but merely to enter into some composition with Leeuwenhoek, and thus to end a feud which had so long divided two similarly-created principles, whose united researches only could exhaust the deepest springs of knowledge. With this he looked smilingly at Peregrine, into whose arms Dörtje had fled, and expressed a wish that he would mediate.

Leeuwenhoek, on the other hand, admitted that Dörtje was indeed the apple of contention, but that he had just now discovered a new trick of his unworthy colleague. It was not only that to revive his unjust pretensions to Dörtje, he denied the possession of a certain microscope which he had received on a certain occasion as a quittance, but the more to torment him﻿—Leeuwenhoek﻿—he had given it to another. In answer to all this, Swammerdam swore, high and low, that he had never received the microscope, and had great reason to believe that Leeuwenhoek had shamefully purloined it.
===Chinese===
佩普施挽起朋友的胳膊，正要一道匆匆离去，这段路却省下了：房门开了，多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克轻快地走进来，美得像个天使，身后跟着老斯瓦默。列文虎克一直闷声不响，只把愠怒的目光从佩普施身上移到佩雷格林身上；一见老斯瓦默丹，他却像被雷殛了一下。他朝他伸出攥紧的双拳，用愤怒得嘶哑的嗓音喊道：“哈！你是来嘲弄我的吗，你这老奸巨猾的怪物？可你休想得逞。招架吧：你的末日已经敲响了。”

斯瓦默丹倒退了几步；见列文虎克抡起望远镜就要扑上来，他也拔出同样的兵器防身。那场始于佩雷格林门前的决斗，眼看就要重演。乔治·佩普施抢身横在两位斗士中间：左手一把击落列文虎克那道杀气腾腾的怒视——那道怒视本会把对手撂倒在地——左手又拨开斯瓦默丹的兵器，叫他伤不着列文虎克。随后他声言：不把两人失和的缘由弄得水落石出，他绝不容他们交手。佩雷格林觉得这番拦阻入情入理，便毫不犹豫地也横身挡在两位勇士中间，作了同样的声明。两位斗士只得依了。斯瓦默丹还一口声明，他此来全无敌意，不过想同列文虎克订立某种和约，了结这场使两个同源而生的本原长期不和的宿怨——唯有两家合力钻研，才能汲干学问最深的泉源。说着，他笑吟吟地望着佩雷格林——多尔特耶早已投进他的怀抱——表示但愿由他来居中调停。

列文虎克则承认，多尔特耶确确实实是那只引起争端的苹果，只是他新近又识破了他那位不肖同行的一条新诡计：此人为了翻他那些对多尔特耶的不义之请，竟矢口否认自己占有一架显微镜——那本是他在某个场合当作抵偿之物收下的——而且，为了更狠地折磨他列文虎克，竟把那架显微镜转送了旁人。对此，斯瓦默丹指天画地地发誓：他从来没有收到过那架显微镜，而且大有理由相信，是列文虎克恬不知耻地把它昧下了。

===Original===
“The fools!” softly whispered Master Flea to Peregrine, “The fools! they are talking of the microscope which is in your eye. You know that I was present at the treaty of peace concluded between them about the possession of the princess, and, when Swammerdam was flinging into the pupil of his left eye the microscopic glass which he had, in fact, received from Leeuwenhoek, I snapped it up, because it was not Leeuwenhoek’s, but my lawful property. Tell them plainly at once, that you have the jewel.”

Upon this Peregrine made no hesitation in declaring that he was in possession of the microscopic glass which Swammerdam should have received, but did not receive, from Leeuwenhoek, and moreover that the union was not yet settled, and neither Leeuwenhoek nor Swammerdam had at present the unconditional right to look on Dörtje Elverdink as his foster-daughter.

After much argument, it was agreed by the disputants that Mr. Tyss should marry Dörtje Elverdink, who tenderly loved him, and then after seven months, should decide which of the two microscopists was the most desirable father-in-law.
===Chinese===
“这对傻瓜！”跳蚤大师轻声对佩雷格林说，“这对傻瓜！他们说的那架显微镜，就在你的眼睛里。你知道的，当年他们俩为公主的归属订立和约，我正是在场的；斯瓦默丹把那片显微镜片往他左眼瞳孔里安的时候——那片子他确实是从列文虎克手里收下的——我一口把它叼了过来，因为它本不是列文虎克的东西，而是我名正言顺的私产。马上明明白白告诉他们：宝物在你手里。”

听了这话，佩雷格林便毫不迟疑地声明：斯瓦默丹本该从列文虎克手里收到、却并没有收到的那片显微镜片，如今正在他手中；而且，这门亲事也还没有定局，眼下无论列文虎克还是斯瓦默丹，都还没有无条件把多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克看作自家养女的权利。

又争执了半晌，两位争执者总算议定：蒂斯先生应当娶下深情爱着他的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克；七个月之后，再行裁定：两位显微镜学家当中，哪一位是更可心的岳父。

===Original===
However beautiful Dörtje appeared in a dress so admirable that it might seem to have been fashioned by the Loves, and whatever burning looks of passion she might cast at Peregrine, yet he still thought of his protégé as well as of his friend, and remained true to his plighted word, declaring again that he renounced Dörtje’s hand. The microscopists were not a little astonished when Peregrine announced George Pepusch for the man who had the justest claims to the princess, and that he, at all events, had no right to interfere with her choice.

With tears in her eyes the maiden staggered towards Peregrine, who caught her in his arms as she was sinking senseless to the earth. “Ingrate!” she sighed. “You break my heart in thrusting me from you. But you will have it. Take, then, my parting kiss, and let me die!”

Peregrine bent down to her, but when his mouth touched her mouth, she bit his lips so violently that the blood started, at the same time exclaiming merrily, “Monster! it is so one must punish you! Be reasonable, be civil, and take me, let the other cry out as he will.”
===Chinese===
多尔特耶那身衣裳美得出奇，简直像出自众爱神之手；无论她投向佩雷格林的目光里燃着怎样炽烈的情焰，他心里仍旧惦着他的被保护人，也惦着他的朋友，信守自己许下的誓言，再次声明放弃多尔特耶的手。当佩雷格林宣布，对公主最有权利的人是乔治·佩普施，而他本人无论如何都无权干涉她的选择时，两位显微镜学家不由得大为惊愕。

姑娘眼中噙着泪，踉踉跄跄地朝佩雷格林走去，人晕晕沉沉、眼看就要栽倒，佩雷格林将她接进了怀里。“忘恩负义的人！”她叹息道，“你把我从你身边推开，是要碎我的心。可你非要如此。那就收下我诀别的吻，让我死吧！”

佩雷格林朝她俯下身去；可他的嘴一触到她的嘴，她便狠狠咬住他的嘴唇，咬得血都渗了出来，口中还欢天喜地地嚷道：“怪物！就得这样罚你！放明白些，放规矩些，把我娶了吧——随那一位怎么嚎去！”
===Original===
During this the two microscopists had fallen together by the ears again, heaven knows wherefore, while George Pepusch flung himself quite disconsolately at Gamaheh’s feet, and cried out in a voice that sounded wretched enough for any lover﻿—

“Oh, Gamaheh! is then your passion quite extinguished? Do you no more remember the glorious times in Famagusta? no more the pleasant days in Berlin? no more﻿—”

“You are a fool!” interrupted the little one, laughing, “You are a fool, George, with your Gamahehs, your Thistle, Zeherit, and all the other nonsense that you must once have dreamed. I did like you, do like you, and will have you﻿—although the tall one yonder pleases me better﻿—if you solemnly promise, nay swear, to bend all your mind to﻿—”

Here she softly whispered something to Pepusch, and Peregrine thought he collected that Master Flea was the subject of it. In the meantime the dispute between the microscopists had grown hotter and hotter; they had again recourse to their weapons, and Peregrine was busy in trying to sooth their wrath, when the company was again augmented. The door was burst open amidst a strange screaming and croaking, and in rushed the Amateur, Monsieur Legénie, and the barber, Leech. With wild, furious gestures they flew upon the princess, and the barber had already caught her by the shoulder, when Pepusch thrust away the odious assailant with irresistible might, wound about his whole flexible body, and squeezed it together in such a manner that he shot up into the air, quite thin and long, roaring aloud with pain all the time.
===Chinese===
这边，两位显微镜学家天知道为的什么又厮吵起来；那边，乔治·佩普施万念俱灰地扑倒在加玛赫脚边，用一副足以让任何情人闻之酸鼻的声调喊道——

“哦，加玛赫！难道你的情火竟熄灭了吗？你再也不记得法马古斯塔那些辉煌的时光了吗？再也不记得柏林那些快活的日子了吗？再也不——”

“你是个傻瓜！”小姑娘笑着打断他，“你是个傻瓜，乔治，抱着你那些加玛赫呀、蓟草精泽赫里特呀，还有你想必哪一夜梦里攒下的那一堆胡话。我从前喜欢你，如今也喜欢你，也肯要你——虽说那边那位高个子更中我的意——只要你郑重许下，不，起誓，把你的全副心思都用在——”

说到这里，她凑到佩普施耳边，轻轻说了句什么；佩雷格林自觉听出，说的题目正是跳蚤大师。这当口，两位显微镜学家的争执越吵越凶，又各自操起了兵器，佩雷格林正忙着平息他们的怒火，屋里的人马却又添了一拨：门在一阵怪异的尖叫与嘶鸣声中轰然洞开，业余大师勒热尼先生和剃头匠水蛭一齐冲了进来。两人张牙舞爪、气势汹汹地朝公主扑去；剃头匠已经抓住了她的肩头，佩普施却以不可抗拒的神力推开那可憎的袭击者，把他那具绵软柔韧的身子整个缠住，猛地一束——束得他又细又长，直射向半空，一路痛得嗷嗷大叫。

===Original===
While this was going on with the barber, the two microscopists had reconciled themselves in an instant on the appearance of the common enemy, and made a united attack on the Amateur with much success. It availed him nothing that when he was sufficiently drubbed below, he rose up to the ceiling, for Leeuwenhoek and Swammerdam had both seized short thick sticks, and whenever the Amateur descended, they drove him up again by blows, dexterously applied to that part of the body which best can bear them. It was a pretty game of racket, at which the Amateur by compulsion indeed played the most fatiguing﻿—and at the same time the most ungracious﻿—part, namely that of the ball.

This war seemed to inspire the little one with the greatest terror; she clung to Peregrine, and entreated him to bear her away from such an abominable uproar. This he could the less refuse, as there seemed to be no need of him on the field of battle, and he therefore carried her home, that is, into the apartments of his lodger. But no sooner had she got there and found herself alone with Peregrine, than she employed all the arts of the most refined coquetry to allure him into her snares. However firmly he bore in mind that all this was merely falsehood, and aimed at bringing his protégé into captivity, yet such a dizziness of the senses seized him, that he did not even think of the microscopic glass, which might have served him as an active antidote. Master Flea was again in danger; he was, however, saved this time by Mr. Swammer, who entered with George Pepusch. The former appeared to be exceedingly delighted, but the latter had wrath and jealousy in his burning glances. Peregrine left the room, and with wounded heart he strolled through the streets of Frankfurt. He went through the gate and onwards, till he reached the very spot where the strange adventure had happened with his friend, Pepusch. Here he again thought over his wonderful destiny; the image of Gamaheh appeared to him lovelier than ever; the blood rolled more quickly in his veins, his pulse beat more violently, and his breast seemed ready to burst with feverish desire. He felt only too painfully the greatness of the sacrifice which he had just made, and with which he fancied that he had lost all the happiness of life.
===Chinese===
剃头匠那边正闹着，两位显微镜学家一见共同的仇敌露面，霎时捐弃前嫌，联手向业余大师发起进攻，战果赫赫。他在下头挨够了揍、蹿上天花板，也无济于事：列文虎克和斯瓦默丹各自操起一根又短又粗的棍子，业余大师每回落下来，他们便照着他身上最经得起打的地方，手疾眼快地一顿好揍，把他重新打上去。这真是一场妙不可言的墙网球戏：业余大师身不由己，偏偏扮演着最吃力——同时又最不讨好——的角色，那便是球。

这场大战似乎把小姑娘吓得不轻；她紧紧攀住佩雷格林，恳求他把她从这可憎的喧嚣里抱走。这他更无法拒绝——战场上似乎也用不着他了——于是他把她抱回了家，也就是说，抱回了他那位房客的居所。可她一到那里，发觉同佩雷格林独处一室，便施展出最精微妩媚的全副本事，要把他诱进罗网。他尽管牢牢记得，这一切无非是虚情假意，图的是把他的被保护人重新关进囚笼，可一阵神魂颠倒的晕眩还是攫住了他，他竟连显微镜片都没想到——那本可以作他一剂灵验的解药。跳蚤大师又临险境；不过这一回，救他的是斯瓦默先生，他同乔治·佩普施一道走了进来。前者满面喜色，后者那双燃烧的眼睛里，却盛着怒火与醋意。佩雷格林走出房间，揣着一颗受了伤的心，在法兰克福的街上游荡。他出了城门，一路走去，一直走到他同朋友佩普施遭遇那桩奇事的地点。在这里，他又把自己奇妙的命途从头想了一遍；加玛赫的身影在他眼里，显得比以往任何时候都可爱；血在血管里流得更快，脉搏跳得更急，胸膛里憋着一腔炽热的渴望，几乎要胀裂开来。他这才痛切地感到，他方才作出的牺牲何等巨大；他觉得，随着这牺牲，他已把人生全部的幸福一齐断送了。

===Original===
The night had drawn in when he returned to the city. Without being aware of it, perhaps from an unconscious dread of going back to his own house, he wandered through many by-lanes, and at last into the Kalbecher-street. A man with a knapsack on his back asked him if the bookbinder, Lemmerhirt, did not live there? and on looking up, Peregrine saw that he was actually standing before the narrow dwelling. The windows of the industrious binder, who worked through the night, were shining brightly and loftily, and the door was opened to the man with a knapsack, who entered immediately.

Peregrine now recollected, with vexation, that, in the tumult of the last few weeks, he had forgotten to pay the bookbinder for several jobs that he had executed for him; he resolved to go and settle all the very next morning.
===Chinese===
他回到城里时，夜幕已经合拢。他不知不觉地——也许是出于一种重回自家宅邸的无名畏惧——在许多小巷里穿来穿去，末了拐进了卡尔贝歇街。一个背着行囊的汉子问他，装订匠莱默希特是不是住在这里？佩雷格林抬眼一望，才发觉自己正站在那栋窄小的住宅跟前。这位勤劳的装订匠通宵赶活，窗子高高地透出明亮的灯光；门为那背行囊的汉子开了，他当即走了进去。

佩雷格林这才懊恼地想起：前几周乱事纷纷，他竟忘了付清装订匠替他做的几件活计的工钱；他打定主意，第二天一早就去把账目一并结清。

## VII Seventh Adventure / 七 第七奇遇

===Original===
Although we are wholly deficient in any certain information respecting the result of the battle in Leeuwenhoek’s chamber, yet we cannot suppose otherwise than that the microscopists, with the help of George Pepusch, had obtained a complete victory over the hostile confederates: it had else been impossible that the old Swammer had returned so friendly and contented as he really did. With the same glad face, Swammer, or rather Mr. Jan Swammerdam, came the following morning to Peregrine, who was still in bed and earnestly conversing with his protégé, Master Flea. Upon seeing this visitor, Peregrine did not fail putting the microscopic glass into the pupil of his eye.

After many long and tedious excuses for his early visit, Swammerdam at last took his place on the bed, positively refusing to let Peregrine rise and put on his dressing gown. In the strangest phrases he thanked his landlord for the great civilities he had experienced, which, it seems, consisted in his having been received as a lodger, and also in that Mr. Tyss had allowed his household to be increased by the addition of a young female, who was sometimes too loud and vivacious. But the greatest favour shown by Mr. Peregrine, and not without some self-sacrifice, was in his having effected a reconciliation between him (Swammerdam) and his old friend, Antonie van Leeuwenhoek. In fact, as Swammerdam went on to say, both hearts had inclined to each other at the moment when they were attacked by the Amateur and the barber and had to protect Dörtje Elverdink from those monsters. The serious reconciliation of the microscopists had soon after followed.

Leeuwenhoek had perceived, as well as Swammerdam, the paramount influence which Peregrine had over both of them and the first use which they made of their renewed friendship was to consider in unison the strange horoscope of Mr. Tyss, and as far as possible, to interpret it.

“What my friend, Leeuwenhoek, could not do alone,” continued the microscopist, “was effected by our united powers, and thus this was the second experiment which, in spite of all the obstacles opposed to us, we undertook with the most splendid results.”

“The shortsighted fool!” lisped Master Flea, who sat upon the pillow, close to Peregrine’s ear. “He still fancies that the Princess, Gamaheh, was restored to life by him. A pretty life, indeed, is that to which the awkwardness of the two microscopists has condemned the poor thing!”

===Chinese===
关于列文虎克房间里那场争斗的结局，我们固然拿不到任何确切的音讯，可除了这样设想，再无别的可想：两位显微镜学家借乔治·佩普施之力，把那伙结伙来犯的仇敌打得一败涂地；不然的话，老斯瓦默绝不可能像实际上那样，回来时满面和气、心满意足。第二天早晨，斯瓦默——或者说扬·斯瓦默丹先生——便是带着同样一张欢天喜地的脸，来见佩雷格林的；他还躺在床上，正同他的被保护人跳蚤大师恳切交谈。一见到这位来客，佩雷格林忙不迭地把显微镜片嵌进了自己的瞳孔。

斯瓦默丹为自己一早登门道了许多又长又乏味的歉，末了终于在床沿上坐定，说什么也不肯让佩雷格林起身去穿晨袍。他用最古怪的字眼，感谢房东先生待他的种种高谊——照他看来，这份高谊无非在于：收留他做了房客；此外还在于，蒂斯先生容许家中添了一位年轻女子，而她有时未免嗓门太大、太活泛了些。至于佩雷格林先生所施的最大恩惠——而且并非没有几分自我牺牲——则在于促成了他（斯瓦默丹）与老友安东尼·范·列文虎克的重归于好。照斯瓦默丹接下去的说法，早在他们遭到业余大师和剃头匠袭击、不得不合力保护多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克免受那两个怪物毒手的当口，两颗心就已经彼此倾心了。随后不久，两位显微镜学家便郑重其事地言归于好。

列文虎克同斯瓦默丹一样，看出了佩雷格林对他们两人握有的无上影响；他们重修旧好后要办的头一件事，就是齐心思量蒂斯先生那张古怪的天宫图，并且尽力把它解说个明白。

“我朋友列文虎克独自办不成的，”显微镜学家接着说，“靠我们联手之力办成了；于是这便是第二场实验——尽管有种种阻碍横在我们面前，我们还是动手做了，而且成绩斐然。”

“这个近视的蠢货！”跳蚤大师坐在枕头上、紧挨着佩雷格林的耳朵，咬着舌音低声道，“他还一口咬定，加玛赫公主是他救活的呢。这两位显微镜学家的笨手笨脚，判给那可怜的人儿的，可真是一份‘好’日子！”

===Original===
“My dear friend,” continued Swammerdam, who had the less heard Master Flea, as he had just then begun to sneeze loudly, “my dear friend, you are particularly chosen by the spirit of the creation, a pet-child of nature, for you possess the most wonderful talisman, or, to speak more correctly and scientifically, the most splendid Tsilmenaja, or Tilsemoht, that was ever fed by the dew of heaven, and has sprung from the lap of earth. It is an honour to my art that I, and not Leeuwenhoek, have discovered that this lucky talisman sleeps for a time till a certain constellation enters, which finds its centre-point in your worthy person. With yourself, my dear friend, something must and will happen, which in the moment the power of the talisman awakes, may make that waking known to you. Let Leeuwenhoek have told you what he will, it must all be false, for in regard to that point, he knew nothing at all until I opened his eyes. Perhaps he tried to frighten you, my dear friend, with some terrible catastrophe, for I know he likes to terrify people without reason. But trust to me, Mr. Tyss, who have the highest respect for you, and swear it to you most solemnly: you have nothing to fear. I should like, however, to learn whether you do not as yet feel the presence of the talisman, and what you think of the matter altogether.”

At these last words Swammerdam eyed his host as keenly as if he would pierce his deepest thoughts, but of course he did not succeed so well in that as Peregrine with his microscopic glass, by means of which the latter learned that it was not so much the united war with the Amateur and the Barber, as the mysterious horoscope, that had brought about the reconciliation of the microscopists. It was the possession of the mighty talisman that both were striving after. In regard to the mysterious lines in the horoscope of Peregrine, Swammerdam remained in as vexatious ignorance as Leeuwenhoek, but he fancied the clue must lie within Peregrine, which would lead to the discovery of the mystery. This clue he now sought to fish out of the novice, and then rob him of the inestimable treasure before he knew its value. He was convinced this talisman was equal to that of the wise Solomon, since, like that, it gave him who possessed it the perfect dominion over the kingdom of spirits.

Peregrine paid like with like, himself mystifying Swammerdam, who thought to mystify him. He contrived to answer so dexterously, in such figurative speeches, that the microscopist feared the initiation had already begun, and that soon the mystery would be revealed which neither he nor Leeuwenhoek had been able to unravel.

===Chinese===
“亲爱的朋友，”斯瓦默丹接着说——他这时正起了大声打喷嚏的兴头，越发没有听清跳蚤大师的话——“亲爱的朋友，您是造化之灵格外拣选的人，是自然的宠儿，因为您占有天下最奇妙的护符——或者，说得更准确、更科学些：天下最华美的一株齐尔梅纳贾（Tsilmenaja），或称提尔塞莫特（Tilsemoht），它受天露的滋养，从大地的怀抱里生出来。这枚幸运的护符要沉睡一时，直到某个星象入位为止，而这星象的中心点，正落在您这位可敬的人身上——是我、而不是列文虎克，发现了这个秘密：这是我这门技艺的一桩光荣。亲爱的朋友，在您身上，必定要、也注定要发生一桩事情；待到护符之力苏醒的那一刻，这桩事情自会让您知道它醒了。任凭列文虎克对您讲过什么，那必定全是假的，因为在这一点上，直到我点开他的眼睛之前，他是一无所知的。亲爱的朋友，他也许拿什么可怕的灾祸吓唬过您，因为我知道，他就爱无缘无故地吓人。可是您要信得过我，蒂斯先生——我对您怀着最高的敬意，并且向您至为庄严地起誓：您什么也不必怕。不过我倒愿意领教一下：您是不是至今还不曾觉出护符的存在？对这桩事情，您总体上又是怎么个想法？”

说到最后几句，斯瓦默丹便紧紧盯住他的房东，仿佛要把他最深的念头看穿似的；不过在这上头，他当然不及握有显微镜片的佩雷格林高明——凭着这片镜子，佩雷格林得知：促成两位显微镜学家和解的，与其说是同业余大师和剃头匠联手打的那一仗，不如说是那张神秘的天宫图。两人真正觊觎的，是对那枚神通广大的护符的占有。至于佩雷格林天宫图里那些神秘的线条，斯瓦默丹同列文虎克一样，还蒙在让人干着急的鼓里；但他认定，通往这桩秘密的线索，必定就藏在佩雷格林身上。如今他就要设法从这位生手嘴里把线索钓出来，然后趁他还不知这件无价之宝的分量，把它抢到自己手里。他深信，这枚护符堪与智者所罗门王的护符比肩，因为它同那一枚一样，能使占有它的人对整个精灵王国握有完备的统治权。

佩雷格林以其人之道还治其人之身：斯瓦默丹想蒙他，他便也装神弄鬼地蒙斯瓦默丹。他把答话安排得那样圆熟，满口譬喻之词，叫这位显微镜学家暗暗担心：入门的点化只怕已经开始了，那桩他同列文虎克都参不透的玄机，眼看就要自行揭晓。

===Original===
Swammerdam cast down his eyes, hemmed, and stammered a few unintelligible words; he was really in a bad plight, and his thoughts were all in confusion.

“The devil! What’s this? Is this Peregrine, who speaks to me? Am I the learned Swammerdam or an ass?”

In despair he at last collected himself, and began,

“But to come to something else, most respected Mr. Tyss, and as it seems to me, something much more agreeable.”

According to what Swammer now went on to say, both he and Leeuwenhoek had perceived, with great pleasure, the strong inclination which Dörtje Elverdink had conceived for him. If they had both formerly been of a different opinion, each believing that Dörtje should stay with himself, and not think of love and marriage, yet they had now both come to a better conviction. They fancied that they read in Peregrine’s horoscope he positively must take Dörtje Elverdink for his wife, as the greatest advantage in all the conjunctures of his life, and as neither doubted for a moment that he was equally enamoured of her, they had looked upon the matter as fully settled. Swammerdam, moreover, was of opinion that Peregrine was the only one who, without any trouble, could beat his rivals out of the field, and that the most dangerous opponents﻿—namely the Amateur and the Barber﻿—could avail nothing against him.

===Chinese===
斯瓦默丹垂下眼睛，干咳了几声，结结巴巴吐出几个不知所云的词；他实在处境狼狈，心思乱作了一团。

“见鬼！这是怎么回事？跟我说话的，当真是佩雷格林吗？我到底是博学的斯瓦默丹，还是一头驴？”

绝望之下，他总算定下神来，开口说道：

“不过，现在来谈另一件事吧，万分尊敬的蒂斯先生——依我看，这件事也要可心得多。”

照斯瓦默接着讲下去的说法，他同列文虎克都满怀欣喜地觉察到，多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克对佩雷格林生出了一片强烈的倾慕。虽说两人从前都另执一说——各人都以为多尔特耶该留在自己身边，不该去想什么恋爱嫁娶——如今却都幡然醒悟了。他们自以为在佩雷格林的天宫图里读出：他命中注定非娶多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克不可，这是他一生一切关节上最大的便宜；况且两人一刻也不曾怀疑，他同样爱她爱得神魂颠倒，于是便把这事看作铁板钉钉了。此外，斯瓦默丹还认为，佩雷格林是唯一一个不费吹灰之力就能把众情敌尽数逐出情场的人，连最危险的对手——也就是业余大师和剃头匠——也奈何他不得。

===Original===
Peregrine found, from Swammerdam’s thoughts, that both the microscopists actually imagined they had read in his horoscope the inevitable necessity of his marriage with Dörtje. It was to this supposed necessity only they yielded, thinking to draw the greatest gain from the apparent loss of the little one, namely, by getting possession of Mr. Tyss and his talisman. But it may be easily supposed how little faith he must have in the science of the two microscopists, when neither of them was able to solve the centre-point of the horoscope. He did not, therefore at all yield to that pretended conjunction, which conditioned the necessity of his marriage with Gamaheh, and found no difficulty whatever in declaring positively that he renounced her hand in favour of his best friend George Pepusch, who had older and better claims to the fair one, and that he would not break his word upon any condition.

Swammerdam raised his green eyes which he had so long cast down, stared vehemently at Peregrine, and grinned with the cunning of a fox, as he said, if the friendship between him and Pepusch were the only scruple which kept him from giving free scope to his feelings, this obstacle existed no longer: Pepusch had perceived, although slightly touched with madness, his marriage with Dörtje was against the stars, and nothing could come from it but misery and destruction. He had therefore resigned all his pretensions, declaring only that, with his life, he would protect Gamaheh﻿—who could belong to no one but his bosom-friend, Tyss﻿—against the awkward dolt of an Amateur and the bloodthirsty Barber.

A cold shudder ran through Peregrine when he perceived, from Swammerdam’s thoughts, that all was true which he had spoken. Overpowered by the strangest and the most opposite feelings, he sank back upon his pillow and closed his eyes. The microscopist pressed him to come down himself, and hear from Dörtje’s mouth, from George’s, the present state of things, and then took his leave with as much ceremony as he had entered.

Master Flea, who sat the whole time quietly on the pillow, suddenly leaped up to the top of Peregrine’s nightcap. There he raised himself up on his long hind-legs, wrung his hands, stretched them imploringly to Heaven, and cried out in a voice half-stifled with tears,

“Woe to poor me! I already thought myself safe, and now comes the most dangerous trial. What avail me the courage, the constancy of my noble patron? I surrender myself! All is over.”

===Chinese===
佩雷格林从斯瓦默丹的心思里听出，这两位显微镜学家当真以为，他们在他的天宫图里读出了他非同多尔特耶成婚不可的定数。他们屈从的仅仅是这个臆想出来的定数，盘算着从表面上失去那小人儿一事里捞足最大的好处——也就是把蒂斯先生连同他的护符一并弄到手。可是不难想见，连天宫图的中心点都参不透的两位，他们的学问在他心里还能剩下几分信用。因此，他丝毫也不肯顺从那个虚构的星象交会——据说它注定了他非娶加玛赫不可；他斩钉截铁地宣布：他把她这只手让给他最好的朋友乔治·佩普施，因为对这位美人，佩普施握有更早、也更名正言顺的权利；而且无论任何条件，他都不肯食言。

斯瓦默丹抬起那双低垂已久的绿眼睛，狠狠盯住佩雷格林，脸上堆出狐狸一般狡黠的笑，说道：倘若他和佩普施之间的交情，是拦着他放任自己感情的唯一顾忌，那么这道障碍如今已不复存在：佩普施——尽管脑子稍稍沾了点疯气——已经看出，他同多尔特耶的婚事逆着星象，除了悲惨与毁灭，结不出别的果子。因此他已放弃了一切权利要求，只声明一件事：他要凭这条性命，保护加玛赫——她只能属于他的知心朋友蒂斯——使她不受那蠢牛一般的业余大师和嗜血的剃头匠的欺凌。

佩雷格林从斯瓦默丹的心思里发觉，他口中所说句句属实，一阵寒颤顿时窜遍全身。他被种种最古怪、又最针锋相对的感情压倒了，颓然倒回枕上，闭起了眼睛。显微镜学家恳切地劝他亲自下楼去，从多尔特耶口中、从乔治口中，亲耳听听眼下事情的原委，然后带着进门时那一套周到的礼数告辞而去。

跳蚤大师方才一直安安静静地坐在枕头上，这时霍地一跃而起，跳上了佩雷格林睡帽的帽顶。他直起两条长长的后腿立在帽尖上，绞着双手，又哀告似的把它们伸向苍天，用被泪水噎住了一半的嗓音喊道：

“我这苦命的人啊！我还只道自己已经脱险，如今最凶险的考验却来了。我高贵的恩主纵有胆量、纵有坚贞，于我又有什么用呢？我投降了！全完了。”

===Original===
“Why,” said Mr. Tyss, in a faint voice, “Why do you lament so on my nightcap, my dear master? Do you fancy that you alone have to complain? that I myself am not in the unhappiest situation in the world? for my whole mind seems broken up, and I neither know what to do, nor which way to turn my thoughts. But do not fancy, my dear master, I am foolish enough to venture near the rock upon which all my resolutions might be shipwrecked. I shall take care not to follow Swammerdam’s invitation, and to avoid seeing the alluring Dörtje Elverdink.”

“In reality,” said Master Flea, after he had taken his old post upon the pillow, by Peregrine’s ear, “In reality I am not sure that I ought not to advise you to go at once to Swammerdam’s, however destructive it may appear to myself. It seems to me as if all the lines of your horoscope were running quicker and quicker together, and you yourself were upon the point of entering the red centre. Well, let the dark destiny have decreed what it will; I plainly perceive even a Master Flea cannot escape such a conclusion, and it is as simple as useless to expect my safety from you. Go then: take her hand, deliver me to slavery, and that all may happen as the stars will it, without any interference, make no use of the microscopic glass.”

“Formerly,” said Peregrine, “formerly, Master Flea, your heart seemed stout, your mind firm, and now you have grown so fainthearted! You may be as wise as you will, but you have no good idea of human resolution, and at all events, rate it too meanly. Once more﻿—I will not break my word to you, and that you may perceive how fixed my determination is, of not seeing the little one again, I will now rise and betake myself, as I did yesterday, to the bookbinder’s.”

“Oh Peregrine!” cried Master Flea, “the will of man is a frail thing; a passing air will break it. How immense is the abyss lying between what man wills and what really happens! Many a life is only a constant *willing*, and many a one, from pure volition, at last does not know what he will. You *will* not see Dörtje Elverdink, and yet who will answer for it that you do not see her in the very moment of your declaring such a resolution?”

Strange enough, the very thing really happened which Master Flea had prophesied.

===Chinese===
“这是怎么说呢？”蒂斯先生声音微弱地说，“亲爱的大师，你干吗趴在我的睡帽上这样哀号？你以为只有你一个人有得抱怨吗？你以为我自己不正是天底下处境最不幸的人吗？我整个的心思仿佛都散了架，既不知道该做什么，也不知道该把念头往哪边引。不过亲爱的大师，你可别以为我糊涂到肯去闯那块礁石——我全部的决心都可能在那里撞得粉碎。我会留神不去应斯瓦默丹的邀，躲着不见那迷人的多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克。”

“说实在的，”跳蚤大师重新在枕头上、佩雷格林耳边坐回老位置，说道，“说实在的，我说不准自己是不是就不该劝你立刻到斯瓦默丹那里去，哪怕这在我自己看来形同毁灭。我只觉得，你天宫图上所有的线条正跑得越来越急、越聚越拢，你本人眼看就要踏进那个红色的中心点了。好吧，任凭黑暗的命运定下什么章程；我看得明白，就连跳蚤大师也逃不脱这样一个结局，指望你来保我平安，同指望一场空没有两样。那你就去吧：牵起她的手，把我交出去为奴；为了叫一切照星象的意旨、不受半点干扰地发生，显微镜片一次也不要用。”

“从前，”佩雷格林说，“从前，跳蚤大师，你的心显得那么坚韧，你的神志那么坚定，如今你怎么变得这样怯懦了！随你多么有智慧吧，你对人的决心终究没什么好见解，无论如何都把它估得太低了。我再向你重申一遍——我对你决不食言；为了让你看看，我不再见那小人儿的决心下得有多死，我现在就起身，像昨天一样，到装订匠家去。”

“哦，佩雷格林！”跳蚤大师喊道，“人的意志是件脆弱的东西；一阵过路的风就能把它吹断。人所意愿的，同实际发生的之间，隔着多大的一道深渊啊！有多少人的一生，只是一场绵延不绝的*意愿*；又有多少人，光凭着意愿活着活着，到头来竟不知道自己要的是什么。你*决意*不见多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克——可是谁敢担保，就在你宣布这项决心的那一刻，你不会恰恰见着她呢？”

也真够怪的，跳蚤大师预言的事偏偏应了验。

===Original===
Peregrine arose, dressed himself, and faithful to his intention, would have gone to the bookbinder. In passing Swammerdam’s chamber, the door was wide open, and﻿—he knew not how it happened﻿—he stood, leaning on Swammerdam’s arm, close before Dörtje Elverdink, who sent him a hundred kisses, and with her silver voice cried out, joyfully, “Good morning, my dear Peregrine!” George Pepusch, too, was there, looking out of the window and whistling. He now flung the window to with violence, and turned round.

“Ha!” he exclaimed as if he had just then seen Peregrine, “Ha! look! You come to see your bride. That’s all in order, and any third person would only be in the way. I too will take myself off, but let me first tell you, my good friend Peregrine, that George Pepusch scorns every gift which a compassionate friend would fling to him as if he were a beggar. Cursed be every sacrifice! I will have nothing to thank you for. Take the beautiful Gamaheh, who so warmly loves you, but take care the Thistle, Zeherit, do not take root, and burst the walls of your house.”

George’s voice and manner bordered upon brutality, and Peregrine was filled with vexation, when he saw how much his whole conduct was mistaken. Without concealing his disgust, he said,

“It never has entered into my head to cross you in your path, but the madness of jealousy speaks out of you, or you would see how innocent I am of all you have been brooding in your own soul. Do not ask of me to kill the snake which you have been nourishing in your breast for your own torment; learn too, I gave *you* no alms, I made *you* no sacrifice, in giving up the fair one, and with her, perhaps, the greatest blessing of my life. Other and higher duties, an irrevocable promise, compelled me to it.”

Pepusch, in the wildest wrath, raised his clenched hand against his friend, when Gamaheh sprang between them, and catching Peregrine’s arm, exclaimed,

“Let the foolish Thistle go; he has nothing but nonsense in his brain, and as is the way with thistles, is surly and obstinate without well knowing what he means. You are mine, and remain mine﻿—mine own dearest Peregrine.”

===Chinese===
佩雷格林起了身，穿好衣裳，正恪守初衷要往装订匠家去。路过斯瓦默丹的房间时，房门洞开着——他自己也不知是怎么回事——他已经倚着斯瓦默丹的胳膊，站在了多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克跟前；她朝他飞来上百个吻，用她银铃般的嗓子欢欢喜喜地喊道：“早安，我亲爱的佩雷格林！”乔治·佩普施也在那里，正望着窗外吹口哨。这会儿他砰的一声猛地把窗摔上，转过身来。

“哈！”他嚷道，仿佛直到此刻才看见佩雷格林似的，“哈！瞧啊！你是来看你的新娘子的。这都合乎规矩，再添第三个人反倒碍事了。我这就自己走开；不过先让我告诉你，我的好朋友佩雷格林：凡是朋友出于怜悯、像打发叫花子一样丢给乔治·佩普施的东西，他一概嗤之以鼻。什么牺牲，都见鬼去吧！我不欠你任何情分。美丽的加玛赫这样热烈地爱你，你就领去吧；只是小心提防，别让蓟草精泽赫里特在你家扎下根，把你家的墙壁胀裂了。”

乔治的声音和做派近乎粗暴；佩雷格林眼看自己这一片行止被误会到这般田地，心里好不懊恼。他毫不掩饰自己的反感，说道：

“我脑子里从来没有起过要拦你去路的念头；开口说话的是你心里的醋疯，不然你就会看到，你独自在心里孵了这一路的种种，我是何等清白无辜。别来要求我替你掐死那条你自己喂养在胸口、专供你自己煎熬的蛇；你也该明白：我放弃那位美人——连同她，或许还有我此生最大的福祉——既不是施舍*你*，也不是为*你*牺牲。是另一些更高贵的本分，是一句无可挽回的诺言，逼我这样做的。”

佩普施勃然大怒，举起攥紧的拳头就要朝他朋友挥去；加玛赫一下子蹿到两人中间，一把抓住佩雷格林的胳膊，喊道：

“让那株蠢蓟草去吧；他脑子里装的全是胡话，又天生一副蓟草脾气，横竖执拗，自己也不清楚自己要怎样。你是我的，也永远是——我的，我自己的、最亲爱的佩雷格林。”

===Original===
Thus saying, the little one drew Peregrine upon the sofa, and without further ceremony, seated herself upon his knees. Pepusch, after having sufficiently gnawed his nails, ran wildly out of the door.

Dressed again in the fairy dress of tissue, she appeared as lovely as ever. Peregrine felt himself streamed through by the electric warmth of her body, and yet, amidst it all, a cold mysterious shudder thrilled through him like the breathing of death. For the first time he thought that he saw something singular and lifeless deeply seated in her eyes, while the tone of her voice﻿—nay, even the rustling of her dress﻿—betrayed a strange being, who was never to be trusted. It fell heavily upon his heart that when she had spoken her real thoughts, she had been in this same silver tissue; he knew not why he should fancy anything menacing in it, and yet the idea of this dress was intimately blended with that of the supernatural, as a dream unites the most heterogeneous things, and all passes for absurd, the deeper connection of which we are unable to comprehend.

Far from wounding the fair one with a suspicion which was perhaps false, Peregrine violently suppressed his feelings, and only waited for a favourable opportunity of freeing himself and escaping from the snake of Paradise. At last Dörtje said,

“How is it, my sweet friend, you seem so cold and insensible today? What have you got in your head, my life?”

“I have a headache,” replied Peregrine, as indifferently as he was able. “Headache! Whims! Megrims! Nothing else, my sweet child. I must go into the open air, and all will be over in a few minutes. Besides, I am called away by a particular business.”

“It is all invention!” exclaimed Gamaheh, starting up hastily. “But you are a malicious monkey that must be tamed.”

===Chinese===
小人儿一面说着，一面把佩雷格林拽到沙发上，也不再多客气，一屁股坐上了他的膝头。佩普施把指甲啃了个够，发疯似的冲出门去了。

她又穿上了那袭银纱仙衣，风姿一如既往地可爱。佩雷格林感到她肢体上那电流般的暖意流遍自己全身；然而就在这暖意之中，一阵冰冷而神秘的战栗又穿透了他，像死亡的呼吸。他头一回觉得，在她眼睛深处坐着一种奇特的、没有生命的东西；她的语声——不，连她衣裙的窸窣——都透出一个永远信不得的陌生存在。他的心沉甸甸地记起：她吐露真心话的那一回，身上穿的正是这同一袭银纱；他不知道自己为什么偏要在这上头觉出什么凶兆来，可是这件衣裳的念头，已经同超自然之物的念头难分难解地搅在了一处——就像一场梦把最不相干的东西连到一块儿，而凡是我们参不透其深处关联的，一概被当作荒唐。

佩雷格林生怕拿这条也许冤枉了美人的疑心伤了她，狠狠按捺住自己的情绪，只等一个合适的时机脱开身，逃出这条伊甸乐园的蛇。末了多尔特耶说道：

“这是怎么了，我甜蜜的朋友？你今天显得这么冷淡、这么无动于衷。你脑子里装着什么呀，我的命根儿？”

“我头疼，”佩雷格林尽量装出满不在乎的样子答道，“头疼！一时胡思乱想！偏头疼！没别的了，我甜蜜的孩子。我得到露天去透透风，过几分钟就什么事都没有了。再说，还有一桩特别的事务唤着我呢。”

“全是编的！”加玛赫霍地跳起来喊道，“你不过是只存心捣蛋的猴子，得好好管教管教。”

===Original===
Peregrine was glad when he found himself in the open street, but as to Master Flea, he was quite extravagant in his joy, tittering and laughing incessantly in Peregrine’s neckcloth, and clapping together his forepaws till they rang again. This merriment of his little protégé was somewhat troublesome to Mr. Tyss, as it disturbed him in his meditations, and he begged of him to be quiet, for many grave people had already glanced at him with looks of reproach, fancying it was he who tittered and laughed, and played such foolish pranks in the open streets.

“Fool that I was!” exclaimed Master Flea, persisting in the ebullitions of his extravagant joy, “Fool that I was to doubt of the victory where no battle was needed. Why, you had conquered in the moment, when even the death of your beloved could not shake your resolution. Let me shout, let me rejoice, for all must deceive me if a bright morning sun do not soon arise, which will clear up every mystery.”

On Peregrine’s knocking at the bookbinder’s, a soft female voice cried, “Come in!” He opened the door, and a young girl, who was alone in the room, came forward, and asked him in a friendly manner what he wanted. She was about eighteen years old, rather tall than short, and slim, with the finest proportions. Her hair was of a bright chestnut colour, her eyes were of a deep blue, and her skin seemed to be a blended web of lilies and roses. But more than all this were the purity and innocence that sat upon her brow, and showed themselves in all her actions.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林来到露天的街上，才算松了口气。至于跳蚤大师，他可是欢喜得没了边：躲在佩雷格林的领结里咯咯地笑个不停，两只前爪啪啪地对拍，拍得脆响。他这位小小的被保护者如此欢腾，倒叫蒂斯先生有些吃不消：这一路搅了他的沉思，他只得求他安静些，因为好些个庄重的人已经朝他投来责备的目光，只当在露天的街上嘻嘻哈哈、闹这种蠢把戏的就是他本人。

“我真是个傻瓜！”跳蚤大师喊道，一味纵情宣泄他那过了头的欢喜，“我真是个傻瓜，竟怀疑起根本无须开打的胜利来！要知道，就连你心爱之人的死都撼不动你决心的那一刻，你早已打赢了。让我喊吧，让我乐吧——要是不久便有一轮明媚的朝阳升起、照亮每一桩玄机，那才真是人人都骗了我呢。”

佩雷格林敲了敲装订匠家的门，一个柔和的女声应道：“请进！”他推门进去，屋里只有一个年轻姑娘，迎上前来，和和气气地问他有什么事。她年方十八上下，个子偏高而不矮，身材苗条，比例姣好极了。她的头发是明亮的栗色，眼睛是深深的蓝色，皮肤仿佛百合与玫瑰织成的一幅锦缎。然而比这一切更动人的，是端坐在她眉宇间的纯洁与天真，这纯洁与天真在她的一举一动里都显露出来。

===Original===
When Peregrine gazed on the gentle beauty, it seemed to him as if he had been hitherto lying in bonds, which a benevolent power had loosened, and the angel of light stood before him. But his enamoured gaze had confounded the maiden: she blushed deeply, and casting down her eyes, repeated more gently than at first, “What does the gentleman want?” With difficulty Peregrine stammered out, “Pray, does the bookbinder Lemmerhirt live here?” Upon her replying that he did, but that he was now gone out upon business, Peregrine talked confusedly of bindings which he had ordered, of books which Lemmerhirt was to procure for him, till at last he came somewhat more to himself, and spoke of a splendid copy of Ariosto, which was to have been bound in red morocco with golden filleting. At this, it was as if a sudden electric spark had shot through the maiden; she clasped her hands, and with tears in her eyes, exclaimed, “Then you are Mr. Tyss?” At the same time she made a motion as if she would have seized his hand, but suddenly drew back, and a deep sigh seemed to relieve her full breast. A sweet smile beamed on her face, like the lovely glow of morning, and she poured forth thanks and blessings to Peregrine for his having been the benefactor of her father and mother, and not only for this﻿—no﻿—for his generosity, his kindness, the manner of his making presents to the children, and spreading joy and happiness amongst them. She quickly cleared her father’s armchair of the books, bound and unbound, with which it was loaded, wheeled it forward, and pressed him to be seated, and then presented to him the splendid Ariosto with sparkling eyes, well knowing that this masterpiece of bookbinding would meet with Peregrine’s approbation.

Mr. Tyss took a few pieces of gold from his pocket, which, the maiden seeing, hastily assured him that she did not know the price of the work, and therefore could not take any payment; perhaps he would be pleased to wait a few minutes for her father’s return. It seemed to Peregrine as if the unworthy metal melted into one lump in his hand, and he pocketed the gold again, much faster than he had brought it out. Upon his seating himself mechanically in the broad armchair, the maiden reached after her own seat, and from instinctive politeness he jumped up to fetch it, when, instead of the chair, he caught hold of her hand, and on gently pressing the treasure, he thought he felt a scarcely perceptible return.

“Puss, puss, what are you doing?” suddenly cried Rose, breaking from him, and picking up a skein of thread, which the cat held between her forepaws, beginning a most mystical web.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林凝望着这温柔的美人，只觉得自己此前一直躺在绳捆索绑之中，如今被一只仁慈的手解开了绑，光明的天使就站在他面前。可是他痴痴的目光把姑娘闹了个大红脸；她垂下眼睛，比起初更轻柔地又问了一遍：“先生要什么？”佩雷格林费了好大的劲，才结结巴巴说出一句：“请问，装订匠莱默希特是住在这儿吗？”她答说是，只是眼下出门办事去了；佩雷格林便语无伦次地说起他订过的装订活计、说起莱默希特要替他觅的几本书，说到末了才算定下神，讲起一部要用红色摩洛哥革（morocco）配金线镶边装帧的阿里奥斯托（Ariosto）漂亮精本来。话音未落，仿佛一道电火花倏地穿过姑娘全身；她双手一合，眼里噙着泪喊道：“这么说，您就是蒂斯先生了！”说着她做了个像要抓他手的动作，却又猛地缩了回去，一声长叹仿佛卸下了她胀满的胸怀。一缕甜甜的微笑照亮了她的脸，像晨光那样可爱；她向着佩雷格林千恩万谢、连声祝福，感谢他做了她父母的恩人——还不止于此——不，还感谢他的慷慨，他的仁慈，感谢他给孩子们送礼物时的那副心意，把欢欣和幸福撒满了他们的家。她赶紧把父亲那把扶手椅上堆着的书——上了封面的和还没上封面的——一一挪开，把椅子转过来，恳请他落座，然后眼波盈盈地捧出那部漂亮的阿里奥斯托；她深知，这件装帧杰作定能博得佩雷格林的称赏。

蒂斯先生从口袋里掏出几枚金币；姑娘一见，慌忙担保说，这部书该值多少钱她并不知道，所以一份货款也收不得；也许他肯稍候几分钟，等她父亲回来。佩雷格林只觉得，这不知趣的金属仿佛在手里熔成了一坨；他把金币塞回口袋，比掏出来时快了不知多少。他木然在那把宽大的扶手椅里落了座；姑娘转身去搬自己的椅子，他出于本能的礼貌跳起来要代劳，结果抓住的不是椅子，而是她的手；他轻轻握了握这件珍宝，觉得掌心里似乎回过来一丝几不可觉的轻握。

“咪咪，咪咪，你在干什么？”罗泽（Rose）忽然喊道，从他身边挣开，把一绞线从猫儿的前爪间拾起来——那猫儿正抱着这绞线，起劲地织一张顶神秘的网。

===Original===
Peregrine was in a perfect tumult, and the words “Oh, princess!” escaped him without his knowing how it happened. The maiden looked at him in alarm, and he cried out in the softest and most melancholy tone, “My dearest young lady!” Rose blushed, and said with maiden bashfulness, “My parents call me Rose; pray, do the same my dear Mr. Tyss, for I too am one of the children, to whom you have shown so much kindness, and by whom you are so highly honoured.”

“Rose!” cried Peregrine, in a transport. He could have thrown himself at her feet, and it was only with difficulty that he restrained himself.

Rose now related﻿—as she quietly went on with her work﻿—how the war had reduced her parents to distress, and how since that time she had lived with an aunt in a neighbouring village, till a few weeks ago, when, upon the death of the old lady, she had returned home.

Peregrine heard only the sweet voice of Rose, without understanding the words too well, and was not perfectly convinced of his being awake, till Lemmerhirt entered the room and gave him a hearty welcome. Soon after the wife followed with the children, and as thoughts and feelings are strangely blended in the mind of man, it happened now that Peregrine, even in the midst of all his ecstasy, suddenly recollected how the sullen Pepusch had blamed his presents to this very family. He was particularly delighted to find that none of the children had made themselves ill by his gifts, and the pride with which they pointed to a glass case, where the toys were shining, proved that they looked upon them as something extraordinary, never perhaps to recur. The Thistle, in his ill-humour, was quite mistaken.

“Oh, Pepusch!” said Peregrine to himself, “no pure beam of love penetrates thy distempered mind.” In this Peregrine again meant something more than toys and sugarplums.

===Chinese===
佩雷格林心里翻江倒海，一句“哦，公主！”不知怎么就脱口而出。姑娘惊惶地望着他，他却用最温柔、最忧郁的声调喊出一句：“我最亲爱的小姐！”罗泽红了脸，带着少女的羞怯说：“我父母叫我罗泽；求您也这样叫我吧，亲爱的蒂斯先生，因为我也是承您厚爱的孩子里的一个，我们全家都这样敬重您。”

“罗泽！”佩雷格林心荡神驰地喊出声来。他恨不得一头拜倒在她脚下，好不容易才克制住了自己。

罗泽接着讲述起来——手上还安安静静地做着活计——战乱怎样把她父母拖入了困顿，从那时起她又怎样住在邻村一位姨妈家里，直到几个星期前老太太去世，她才回了家。

佩雷格林只听得见罗泽甜甜的嗓音，话本身倒没有听真切；直到莱默希特走进屋来、热诚地欢迎他，他才完全确信自己是醒着的。不久，主妇也带着孩子们进来了。人的心思和情感原本就交糅得古怪：这会儿佩雷格林纵然欢喜到了云端，却猛然想起，闷闷不乐的佩普施当初怎样指责过他送给自己一家的礼物。他特别欣慰地发现，孩子们没有一个因为他的礼物吃出病来；他们指着一只玻璃柜——玩具们正在里面闪闪发亮——脸上那份骄傲表明，他们把这些礼物当作了稀世的奇珍，当作兴许再不会重现的东西。蓟草精那天心情坏，实在是错怪了。

“哦，佩普施！”佩雷格林自语道，“你那片病态的心境里，透不进一缕纯净的爱之光。”佩雷格林这话里指的，又不止是玩具和糖果了。

===Original===
Lemmerhirt approached Peregrine and began to talk in an undertone of his Rose, elevating her, in the fullness of his heart, into a perfect miracle. But what gave him the most delight was that Rose had an inclination for the noble art of bookbinding, and in the few weeks that she had been with him had made uncommon advances in the decorative parts, so that she was already much more dexterous than many an oaf of an apprentice who wasted gold and morocco for years, and set the letters all awry, making them look like so many drunken peasants, staggering out of an alehouse. In the exuberance of his delight, he whispered to Peregrine quite confidentially, “It must out, Mr. Tyss, I can’t help it. Do you know, that it was my Rose who gilded the Ariosto?”

Upon hearing this, Peregrine hastily snatched up the book, as if securing it before he was robbed of it by an enemy. Lemmerhirt took this for a sign that Peregrine wished to go, and begged of him to stay a few minutes longer, and this it was that reminded him at last of the necessity of tearing himself away. He hastily paid his bill and set off home, dragging along the heavy quartos as if they had been some treasure.

On entering his house he was met by the old Alina, who pointed to Swammerdam’s chamber with looks of fear and anxiety. The door was open, and he saw Dörtje Elverdink, sitting in an armchair, quite stiff, with a face drawn up, as if it belonged to a corpse, already laid in the grave. Just so stiff, so corpse-like sat before her Pepusch, Swammerdam, and Leeuwenhoek. The old woman exclaimed, “Is not that a strange, ghastly spectacle? In this manner the three unhappy beings have sat the whole day long, and eat nothing, and drink nothing, and speak nothing, and scarcely fetch their breath.”

Peregrine at first felt a slight degree of terror at this strange spectacle, but as he ascended the stairs, the spectral image was completely swallowed up by the sea of pleasure, in which the delighted Peregrine swam, since his seeing Rose. Wishes, dreams, hopes, were agitating his mind, which he longed to unburden to some friend, but what friend had Peregrine besides the honest Master Flea? And to him he wished to open his whole heart, to tell him all about Rose﻿—all in fact that cannot very well be told. But he might call and coax as long as he pleased﻿—no Master Flea would show himself; he was up and away, at last, in the folds of his neckcloth, where Master Flea had been wont to lodge upon his going abroad. Peregrine found, after a more careful search, a tiny box, whereon was written:

> “In this is the microscopic glass. If you look steadfastly into the box with your left eye, the glass will immediately be in its pupil; when you want to be freed from the instrument, you have only to gently squeeze the pupil, holding your eye over the box, and the glass will drop into it. I am busy in your service, and risk no little by it, but for so kind a protector I would hazard anything, as
> “Your most devoted servant,
> “Master Flea.”

===Chinese===
莱默希特凑到佩雷格林跟前，压低嗓门谈起他的罗泽来，说到情浓处，把女儿抬举成了一个十足的奇迹。而最叫他得意的，是罗泽对装帧这门高贵的技艺也一片倾心，来家的这几个星期，在装饰活计上进步惊人，手底下已经比许多糟蹋了多年金箔和摩洛哥革的蠢学徒麻利得多——那些学徒把烫上去的字母一个个贴得歪歪斜斜，活像一伙从酒馆里出来的醉汉，东倒西歪。他喜不自胜，凑到佩雷格林耳边，推心置腹地悄声说：“憋不住了，蒂斯先生，我实在憋不住。您可知道，给那部阿里奥斯托烫金的，正是我的罗泽？”

一听这话，佩雷格林慌忙一把抄起那部书，仿佛怕被哪个仇敌半路夺走似的。莱默希特却把这当作佩雷格林要告辞的意思，恳求他再多坐几分钟；偏偏正是这一挽留，才提醒他终究非走不可了。他匆匆付了账，便动身回家，一路拖着那几册沉甸甸的四开本，仿佛拖着什么宝贝。

一进家门，老阿莉娜便迎上来，又惊又急地直朝斯瓦默丹的房间指。房门敞着，他看见多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克直挺挺坐在一把扶手椅里，脸绷得变了形，仿佛那已经是入殓下葬的死人的脸。在她对面，佩普施、斯瓦默丹和列文虎克也这样直挺挺、死尸般地坐着。老妇人叫道：“这是多古怪、多吓人的一景啊！这三个苦命的人就这么坐了一整天，不吃，不喝，不说话，连气都喘不上来一口。”

见了这古怪的景象，佩雷格林起初也微微感到一阵恐怖；可是上楼的时候，鬼影已全然被欢喜的海洋吞没了——自打见了罗泽，满心欢喜的佩雷格林就一直泡在这片海里。心愿、梦景、希望在他心头翻腾，他巴不得向哪位朋友倾吐个痛快；可除了忠厚的跳蚤大师，佩雷格林还有什么朋友呢？他正要对他剖开整颗心，把罗泽的事、把那些实在不好出口的一切，一五一十讲给他听。可是任凭他怎么呼唤、怎么哄劝，跳蚤大师就是不露面——他到底起身走掉了。佩雷格林细细搜寻了一遍，末了在自己领结的褶子里——每逢他出门，跳蚤大师惯于在那里栖身——找到了一只小小的盒子，上面写着：

> “显微镜片就在匣中。您若用左眼向匣中凝望，镜片立时便入您的瞳孔；您若想卸下此物，只须把眼睛俯在匣口，轻轻一挤瞳孔，镜片便会落进匣里。我正为您的差事奔忙，担的风险着实不小；可为着您这样仁厚的保护人，我什么都敢豁出去。作为
> “您最忠实的仆人，
> “跳蚤大师。”

===Original===
Now here would be an excellent opportunity for a genuine romance-writer to expatiate on the difference between lust and love, and having handled it sufficiently in theory, to illustrate it practically in the person of Mr. Tyss. Much might be said of sensual desires, of the curse of the primal sin, and of the heavenly Promethean spark, which in love inflames that true community of spirit of the two sexes, which forms the actual necessary dualism of nature. Should now the aforesaid Promethean spark﻿—but the reader will perhaps be glad to escape the rest of this dissertation, though he may rest assured there is much in it whereby he might have been edified, had he been so inclined.

It must be evident to all that Peregrine only felt desire for Dörtje Elverdink, but that when he saw Rose Lemmerhirt, the real heavenly love blazed in his bosom. Little thanks, however, would be due to the editor of this most wonderful of all wonderful tales, if﻿—adhering to the stiff, formal pace of renowned romancers﻿—he could not forbear in this place exciting the weariness essentially requisite to a legitimate romance. No; let us go to the point at once: sighs, lamentations, joys, pains, kisses, blisses﻿—are all united in the focus of the moment, when the lovely Rose﻿—with the crimson of maiden modesty upon her cheeks﻿—confesses to the enraptured Peregrine that she loves him; that she cannot express how much, how immeasurably she loves him; that she lives in him only; that he is her only thought, her only joy.

But the crafty demon is wont to thrust his dark claws into the sunniest moments of life﻿—nay, to utterly obscure that sunshine by the shadow of his baleful presence. Thus it happened that evil doubts arose in Peregrine, and his breast was filled with suspicions. A voice seemed to whisper to him, “How! Dörtje Elverdink confessed her love, and yet it was mere selfishness, animated by which she sought to tempt you into breaking your faith and becoming a traitor to your best friend, poor Master Flea! You are rich; they say too that a certain frankness and good nature, by many called weakness, may procure you the doubtful love of men and even of women, and she, who now confesses a passion for you.” He hastily snatched at the fate-fraught box, and was on the point of opening it to place the microscopic glass in the pupil of his eye, and thus reading the thoughts of Rose, but he looked up, and the pure blue of her bright eyes seemed to be reflected on his inmost soul. Rose saw and wondered at his emotion.

===Chinese===
写到这里，一位地道的传奇作家本可以趁机铺陈一番情欲与爱情的分别，先在理论上把它谈个透，再拿蒂斯先生其人作实例加以印证。肉欲之求、原罪之诅，还有那普罗米修斯的天火——它在爱情里点燃两性间那真正的心灵契合，而这契合正构成自然必不可缺的真实二元——凡此种种，尽可大书特书。如今倘若接着谈上述那点普罗米修斯之火——不过，往下这篇宏论的去处，读者诸君或许倒乐得躲开；虽然他尽可放心，内里实在大有可供修心益智的东西，只要他有这份雅兴。

想必人人都看得明白：佩雷格林对多尔特耶·埃尔弗丁克，怀的只是情欲；而一见罗泽·莱默希特，他胸中燃起的才是真正的天上之爱。可是，这位编了一切奇妙故事中最奇妙的故事的编者，倘若也恪守名传奇作家那副僵硬刻板的步子，在此处偏偏按捺不住，非要激起合格传奇作品必不可少的那份倦意，那他可就领不到半句谢了。不；我们还是开门见山吧：叹息、哀诉、欢乐、苦痛、亲吻、至福——统统聚到了这一刻的焦点上：可爱的罗泽——两颊晕着少女羞怯的红霞——向心醉神迷的佩雷格林招认了她爱他；招认她说不出自己爱得有多深、多不可限量；招认她只活在他身上；招认他是她唯一的念头、唯一的欢乐。
